2b The type of type and newtype cannot itself be a row type — slice · array · matrix. The same holds with mut in front. A row type is written as it is where it is used. A row type inside another type can be given a name (type found option slice u8 .). If a type with its own name is needed, put it as a struct field (§6.2.7).

Table 21 — Diagnostics for naming a row type

ViolationDiagnostic
(2b) — the whole type is a sliceE-TYPE-ROWNAME
(2b) — the whole type is an arrayE-TYPE-ARRAY
(2b) — the whole type is a matrixE-MATRIX-USE

Rejected example — another name given to a slice — tests (2b)

Source

module ex_rowname .

type bytes slice u8 .

fn f input b bytes . output u64 . do
  return len b. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-ROWNAME

In plain words

A row type shows its element type and its length · dimensions in its words. Hidden behind another name, a reader has to go to the declaration to know them — and there become two ways of writing the same type. A struct field is different: a different name makes a different type, so that name does not hide but separates.

Note (informative)

The two are separated to prevent mistakes. If user numbers and order numbers are both u64, the processor does not notice when they are swapped. Separated with newtype, the processor catches that mistake.

3 When a newtype is used as a name that separates storage (brand), one brand points at one storage. Opening storage twice with the same brand is refused (E-BRAND-REUSED) — then one name would point at two, and handles from different storage would look like the same type. That is exactly the confusion the brand was meant to prevent. If they must be separated, declare one more newtype.

6.2.10 Reading inside a value — field§

1 field is the form that reads what is inside a value. Its shape is field <value> <step> …, with one or more steps. With several steps it goes down one level at a time from left to right — field o. inner deep . means inner of o, and deep inside that.

2 A step is one of two things. A name is a field of a struct, and an integer is a position in a row (array · slice). The two may be mixed.

3 field is also a place — it can come on the left of set. The reading place and the writing place have the same spelling.

4 If a field not in the declaration is written, the processor refuses the program. Positions in rows follow the bounds rules of §6.2.6.

Example — reading and writing a multi-level field

Source

module ex_field .

struct inner do
  a u64 .
end .

struct outer do
  i inner .
end .

export fn read_deep input o outer . output u64 .
do
  return field o. i a . .
end .

export proc bump_deep input o mut outer .
  effects state .
do
  set field o. i a . 1 .
end .

Note (informative)

The notation with attached dots (o.i.a) does not exist. If operations on values are made to look like name paths, the meaning is decided only once the reader knows whether o is a value or a module — and that is the work of name resolution, not of grammar. Written as a form, it is decided the moment it is read.

6.2.11 Values with lanes — vec§

1 vec <lane count> <element type> holds several values of the same type as one value (the same order as §6.2.6 (1a) — the lane count comes first; the old order vec <element type> <lane count> is E-TYPE-ORDER). scalable can also stand in the lane-count position (vec scalable u8). The lane count shall be a value fixed at translation time (§6.8).

2 Two vecs with different lane counts are different types and do not mix.

3 Arithmetic on a vec happens per lane, separately. Values do not cross between lanes.

3a The per-lane arithmetic is add · sub · mul · div · mod · neg · abs · min · max. Overflow of integer lanes wraps at the lane’s width (unlike scalars — the quotient of the smallest value of a signed lane divided by −1, and its neg · abs, are that value itself). If an integer lane’s div · mod has a lane whose divisor is 0, it stops as for scalars. The quotient is truncated towards 0, mod follows the sign of the divisor (§6.3), and min · max of signed lanes compare by sign (2026-11-02). Floating-point lanes have no mod — as for scalars, translation is refused (§6.3.3 (2a) · E-TYPE-KIND).

Note (informative)

If the machine can compute several lanes at once it does so; if not, it computes them one by one. Either way the answer is the same — the lane count is not a performance hint but part of the type.

Rejected example — the lane count written after the element type

Source

module ex_vec_order .

export fn sum4 input xs slice u32 . output u32 .
  requires ge len xs. . 4 . .
do
  let v vec u32 4 load xs. 0 . .
  return reduce_add v. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-ORDER

6.2.12 Sets made of bits — bitset§

1 bitset <bit count> holds whether each value from 0 up to, but not including, that count is present. The bit count shall be a value fixed at translation time.

2 Inserting or asking about a value outside the range does not conform.

3 A bitset is a set, not the bits of a word. Handling the bits of a word is bit operations (§6.3), and the two are different things.

4 Inserting an element is bitset_insert <set> <value> ., removing one is bitset_remove <set> <value> . — both change the set in place. add means only the addition of numbers, so using it on a set does not conform (E-BITSET-ADD).

4a The number of elements in a set is bitset_count <set>, and its type is usize. The number of elements of a stack is stack_count <stack>. Using one in place of the other, or using len (the length of a piece) on a set · stack, is refused at translation (E-TYPE-ARG).

Example — inserting into and removing from a set

Source

module ex_bitset .

fn members output usize . do
  var s bitset 8 bitset_new 8 . .
  bitset_insert s. 3 .
  bitset_insert s. 5 .
  bitset_remove s. 3 .
  return bitset_count s. . .
end .

Output

members() = 1

6.2.13 An eye laid over bytes — views§

1 A byte slice can be read, without copying, as if it were an array of another type. This is called a view.

2 When a view is obtained, the alignment and length contract is checked. If the contract is broken, the program stops.

3 A view does not copy bytes. Writing through a view changes the original bytes.

Caution — A view counts its length in elements

The length of a byte slice is the number of bytes, but seen as an array of four-byte elements its length is the number of elements. Seeing the same storage with two eyes, len answers differently — because they are counting different things.

4 The opposite of a view is encode. encode <shape> <value> writes the value into bytes in the layout the shape specifies. If laying over and reading is view, writing down is encode.

5 A view laid over a struct is a window. view <struct> <bytes> gives a window that lays the layout of that struct over a byte slice. Reading a field of the window reads the bytes at that place, and writing a field writes the bytes at that place — it is not a copy. The type of a window is view T (a read window) or mut view T (a write window), different from the type T of a struct value. try_view does the same but gives none if the length · alignment do not fit or a field does not fit (9a) — its type is option view T · option mut view T, and some_value is the window. The conditions on length and alignment are specified by §8.7.2.

6 Windows and values do not mix. Putting a window in the place of a struct name T · option T — binding · returning · putting a whole struct value into a window name — is refused at translation (E-VIEW-COPY). A copy that lives apart from the bytes is made with cast T <window> . (§6.2.16 — an explicit conversion is cast). Binding a window to another window name sees the same bytes. A window can also be passed to an input that takes a value (input p T .) — the receiver only reads.

Table 22 — Windows and values

WrittenWhat it is
let h view pt view pt b. .a read window over b. Reading a field reads b
let h mut view pt view pt b. .a write window over b. set field h. y . 9 . changes b — b shall be writable
let k mut view pt h. .another write window that sees the same bytes
var k pt cast pt h. . .a struct value that copied the fields of h. Writing to k leaves b as it is
put h. . (the input of put is mut pt)the bytes of b. If the receiver writes a field, b changes — it shall be a write window
output view pt · output mut view ptreturns a window that sees the bytes of an input

7 Whether something can be changed is written in its type (§8.8 (2)). A write window comes only from writable bytes — binding a write window from a shared input, a slice bound without mut, a string literal, a read window, or the result of an op that gives a read window is refused (E-TYPE-MUT). Writing a field through a read window or doing write_volatile is E-TYPE-MUT, passing a read window to a mut input is E-TYPE-ARGMUT, and returning a window over read-only bytes when the output is mut view T is E-TYPE-RETMUT. If an fn writes through a window over bytes it received as mut — whether laid directly or returned by another op — that is visible to the caller, so it is not pure (E-EFFECT-PURITY, §7.3 (6)).

8 A struct a view can be laid over is a struct whose byte layout is fixed. Each field shall be a number of fixed width (u8…u64 · i8…i64 · f32 · f64), a bool, an enum with a written width (§6.2.7 (2d)), a fixed array of those, or a struct made of such fields. Enums without a written width · slices · option · owned-handle fields have no fixed bytes, so neither a view nor encode can be used on such a struct (E-IR-UNDEF).

9 Every field of a struct read with view and view_array shall be plain (§8.7.1 (2)). A bool field that receives a byte other than 0 · 1 becomes a value that is neither true nor false, and an enum field that receives a number belonging to no variant becomes a value that is no variant, so if there is even one such field (including in inner structs) it is refused (E-TYPE-BITCAST) — for the same reason that bit_cast bool is refused. view_array bool is the same. encode, which writes values down into bytes, has no such restriction.

9a try_view measures such fields — if the byte of a bool field is not 0 · 1, or the number of an enum field is not the number of any variant of that enum, it gives none. It measures the fields of inner structs too. Reading an enum field of a value obtained that way gives the variant of that number, and writing an enum field puts that variant’s number into the bytes. If, after measuring, the underlying bytes change by another path so that they are no variant’s number, execution stops the moment that field is read.

Example — writing a field of a view changes the bytes, and binding from name to name copies

Source

module ex_struct_view .

struct pt do
  x u8 .
  y u8 .
end .

proc write_through input b mut slice u8 . output u8 . effects none . do
  let h mut view pt view pt b. . .
  set field h. y . 9 .     rem b becomes [1,9]
  return idx b. 1 . .
end .

proc copy_by_name input b mut slice u8 . output u8 . effects none . do
  let h view pt view pt b. . .
  var k pt cast pt h. . .  rem a copy that is a struct value
  set field k. y . 7 .
  return idx b. 1 . .      rem b is unchanged
end .

Output

write_through([1,2]) = 9 · copy_by_name([1,2]) = 2

Rejected example — binding a write window from bytes received read-only

Source

module ex_struct_view_ro .

struct pt do
  x u8 .
  y u8 .
end .

proc f input b slice u8 . output u8 . effects none . do
  let h mut view pt view pt b. . .  rem writing a field would change the caller's b
  set field h. y . 9 .
  return 0 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-MUT

Rejected example — binding a window in the place of a struct value

Source

module ex_struct_view_copy .

struct pt do
  x u8 .
  y u8 .
end .

fn f input b slice u8 . output u8 . do
  let h pt view pt b. . .  rem a window or a copy? — `view pt` or `cast pt`
  return field h. y . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-VIEW-COPY

Rejected example — a bool field can receive a byte other than 0 · 1

Source

module ex_struct_view_bool .

struct flag do
  active bool .
  n u8 .
end .

fn f input b slice u8 . output u8 . do
  let h view flag view flag b. . .
  return field h. n . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-BITCAST

6.2.14 Parameters with a narrowed range of values — range§

1 If range <low> <high> is written in the type position of a parameter, that parameter accepts only values between the two ends, both included.

2 If the caller cannot prove that the value is within that range, translation is refused. A value that came from outside the program (an argument of the entry point and the like) has no caller to prove it, so it is checked at the boundary, and the program stops if it is out of range.

2a If the caller’s type is wider than the range, that alone is refused (E-TYPE-WIDTH). A value of the same width, which is not caught by that check, has its range checked at the op’s entry and stops if it is out of range (E-VM-CONTRACT) — the same check as writing requires ge <name> <low> . and requires le <name> <high> ..

3 A declared range is used with the same standing as a contract of §6.4 — the checker may take it as a fact and remove the checks that follow.

In plain words

(3) makes (2a) necessary. If the checker takes the range as a fact and removes bounds checks, while nobody keeps that fact, values outside the range pass through where the checks were removed. Until 2026-09-16 a range parameter of the same width was checked at no layer (defect note 10) — the only thing to trust is what is checked.

Rejected example — the caller's type is wider than the range

Source

module ex_range_wide .

fn scale input a range 0 100 . output u8 .
do
  return narrow u8 a. . .
end .

fn call_scale input x u64 . output u8 .
do
  return scale x. . .    rem `u64` is wider than 0 … 100 — it was not shown to be within the range
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-WIDTH

Note (informative)

The same thing can be written with requires too. The difference is where it is written. Written in the type position, the range becomes the shape of the parameter, and the caller knows it from the signature alone. Written as a contract clause, one has to read the contract to know it.

6.2.15 Nailing down the byte layout — layout and align§

1 A layout can be written inside a struct declaration. If none is written, fields are placed in declaration order, each aligned to its type’s alignment (with as much padding between as alignment requires). The processor does not change the order.

2 layout packed . puts no padding between fields — the fields are packed together in declaration order.

2a The layout words are a closed two — packed and native. Anything else is refused (E-TYPE-LAYOUT).

3 align <n> . nails down the alignment of the place where that struct begins. n shall be a power of 2. The alignment shall be a power of 2 (E-TYPE-ALIGN) — no machine can keep any other number.

marker (표지)

A word attached after a field that fixes how that field is placed and how it can be reached. It is not a type, so it does not change the size of the value; it fixes only the byte order and the way of reaching.

4 A marker can be attached after a field. The markers are these five and no more; writing any other word is refused at translation (E-FIELD-MARK).

Table 23 — Field markers — a closed set of five

MarkerWhat it fixesMeaning
bigbyte orderplaced big-endian
littlebyte orderplaced little-endian
rwway of reachingcan be read and written
roway of reachingread only — writing is refused at translation
woway of reachingwrite only — reading is refused at translation

4a If no byte order is written, the machine’s order is followed (§5.6).

4b The way of reaching is enforced only in structs that mirror device registers (§7.6). A violation is refused at translation, not at execution (E-MMIO-PERM).

In plain words

Why is reading wo an error? Reading a write-only register yields garbage, or the act of reading itself moves the device. That is, that read is not useless but wrong. Once the device has said what it accepts, the type says it back.

Example — a header going out on the wire — no padding, big-endian

Source

module ex_layout .

struct wire_header do
  layout packed .
  magic u32 big .
  length u16 big .
  kind u8 .
end .

fn hdr_kind input b slice u8 . output u64 . do
  let v view wire_header view wire_header b. . .
  return widen u64 field v. kind . . .
end .

Note (informative)

Nailing the layout down and not nailing it down buy different things. Not nailed down, the processor can choose to fit the machine, and it is fast. Nailed down, those bytes have the same meaning outside too — what goes out on a wire or is read by a device needs that. So nail it down only where needed.

6.2.16 Explicit conversion — cast§

1 Reading a value as another type happens only where the source says so. The only conversion the processor does by itself is widening (§6.2.5 (2)); every other conversion is written by the author with a word. The centre of those words is cast <type> <value> — cast is the word that takes charge of explicit conversion in this language. widen is used only where the value does not change, and conversions that may change the value are written with cast — places that change the sign, cross kinds, or narrow.

1a There are three words that write conversions, each with its own place.

Table 24 — The three words that write conversions

WordIts placeIf the value does not fit
widenwidening in which the value fits as it is (the table of ⟦§6.2.5⟧ (4)). The processor also does it by itself — writing it makes it visibledoes not happen
narrowreduces the width within the same kind (integer → narrower integer)traps
castevery other conversion — changing the sign · between integers and floating point · between usize · idiff, whose width depends on the implementation, and other integers · truth value → integer · enum ↔ integertraps

1b A conversion that could be read either way does not happen by itself; it goes through cast. That is a conversion where the same bits could be read as two numbers, or that widens or narrows depending on the target machine, or that changes the kind of the value. This rule also applies to types and conversions that enter the language in the future — if a new conversion is ambiguous its spelling is cast, and nothing is decided in the direction of adding implicit conversions.

In plain words

When conversions are written in the source, a reader can find every place where a value may change with their own eyes. Having found and read the casts, they have seen every place in that program where a type changes. If there were even one place where the processor changed it secretly, that promise would be broken. So this language has no implicit conversions for convenience. Writing them takes a little effort, but when reading there is no guessing «what changed here».

2 cast traps if the value does not fit the target type. It is not truncated silently. That is, cast is not a mark that says «anything may happen» but a mark that says “I know this is a place where the value may change”.

3 Converting a floating-point number to an integer truncates towards 0 — 7.9 becomes 7 and -2.9 becomes -2. Only the fractional part is discarded; if the integer part does not fit the target type, it traps as in (2).

Caution —

Discarding the fractional part does not trap. So cast does not violate “there is no implicit conversion that loses a value” of §6.2.5 (1) — because that conversion is not implicit. The author wrote cast, and that word itself is the mark that says «the value may change here».

3-1 A cast to a floating-point type — whether from an integer or from another floating-point type — gives the nearest value in the target type, and when two are equally near, the one whose last digit is even. Rounding happens once — converting an integer to f32 does not go through f64 and round twice. A value that rounds beyond the largest finite value of the target type becomes the infinity of the same sign, and NaN is NaN. This conversion does not trap as in (2) — a floating-point type has an infinity for an overflowing value to go to. From f32 to f64 the value is unchanged. cast f32 <f64 1e300> is +∞, and cast f32 <u64 16777217> is 16777216.

3a Between usize · idiff and other integer types the only crossing is cast (§6.2 (3-3)). This conversion is both a widening and a narrowing depending on the implementation’s width, so cast is its spelling — if the value fits it is given as it is; if not, it traps as in (2). On an implementation where the width is the same or grows, this cast leaves no check.

4 From a truth value to an integer goes by cast — cast <integer type> <truth value> is 1 for true and 0 for false. That value fits every integer type, so the trap of (2) does not happen. A floating-point type is not a target of this conversion (E-TYPE-KIND).

4a There is no cast from an integer to a truth value — bool cannot be the target of cast (E-TYPE-KIND). Write what is being asked: ne n. 0 . or eq n. 0 .. Which values of the number are true is decided by that question.

4b Truth values are not in the table of widen (§6.2.5 (4)) — widen <integer type> <truth value> is refused (E-WIDEN-KIND). The spelling that turns a truth value into a number is cast alone.

Example — a truth value as a number

Source

module ex_bool_cast .

fn big input a u64 . output u8 . do
  return cast u8 gt a. 2 . . .
end .

fn count_big input a u64 . input b u64 . output u64 . do
  return add cast u64 gt a. 2 . . cast u64 gt b. 2 . . . .
end .

Output

big(5) = 1 · big(1) = 0 · count_big(5, 7) = 2

Rejected example — there is no cast that turns a number into a truth value

Source

module ex_cast_to_bool .
fn nonzero input n u64 . output bool . do
  return cast bool n. . .     rem write what is being asked — `ne n. 0 .`
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-KIND

5 An enum value is not a number. Putting an enum value in a place that takes a number — a name · field · argument · return declared as a number, arithmetic, comparison with numbers, indices, bit operations, widening · narrowing — is refused at translation (E-TYPE-KIND · E-TYPE-LET · E-TYPE-RETURN · E-TYPE-ARG · E-WIDEN-KIND), and putting a number in a place that takes an enum is refused too, literals included. If the variant’s number (§6.2.7 (2c)) is needed, write cast <integer type> <enum value> — if the number does not fit that type, it traps as in (2). Two values of the same enum can be compared with eq · ne, and with lt · le · gt · ge — the magnitude is the order of the numbers, and the numbers increase in declaration order, so it is the same as the order of declaration. Values of different enums are not compared (E-TYPE-KIND).

5a From a number to an enum goes by cast <enum> <integer> — it gives the variant with that number. If the number belongs to no variant, it traps (E-VM-CAST). This is the place where a number read from a file or a wire is turned back into an enum. The source shall be an integer (E-TYPE-KIND).

5b An enum with even one variant that carries values (§6.2.19) can be neither the source nor the target of cast (E-TYPE-KIND). One number cannot say everything about such a value — which variant it is is asked with isa · match, and the value is built with <enum>.<variant> ….

Example — a variant's number is read with cast

Source

module ex_enum_cast .

enum color do
  red .
  green .
  blue .
end .

fn code input c color . output u8 . do
  return cast u8 c. . .
end .

fn code_of_blue output u8 . do
  return code blue. . .
end .

fn same output u8 . do
  return cast u8 eq green. green. . . .
end .

Output

code_of_blue() = 3 · same() = 1

Rejected example — giving an enum value as a number

Source

module ex_enum_as_number .

enum color do
  red .
  green .
end .

fn code input c color . output u8 . do
  return c. .     rem a variant's number is read with `cast u8 c. .`
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-RETURN

Example — turning a number read back into an enum and filtering by range

Source

module ex_enum_from_number .

enum item do
  weapon_start 10 .
  sword .
  stick .
  weapon_end .
  tool_start 20 .
  hammer .
  tool_end .
end .

rem 1 = weapon · 2 = tool — values of the same enum are compared by magnitude
fn kind_of input n u8 . output u8 . do
  let it item cast item n. . .
  if and gt it. weapon_start. . lt it. weapon_end. . . do return 1 . end .
  if and gt it. tool_start. . lt it. tool_end. . . do return 2 . end .
  return 0 .
end .

enum hangul do
  ga 11 .
  na .
  da 101 .
  ra .
end .

fn round_trip input n u32 . output u32 . do
  return cast u32 cast hangul n. . . .
end .

Output

kind_of(12) = 1 · kind_of(21) = 2 · round_trip(102) = 102

Rejected example — comparing values of different enums

Source

module ex_enum_cross_order .

enum color do
  red .
  green .
end .

enum size do
  small .
  large .
end .

fn f input c color . input s size . output bool . do
  return lt c. s. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-KIND

Rejected example — doing arithmetic with an enum

Source

module ex_enum_arith .

enum level do
  low .
  high .
end .

fn next input l level . output u8 . do
  return add l. 1 . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-KIND

6.2.17 What holds characters — a string is not a type§

1 This language has no string type. What holds characters is a piece of bytes (slice u8), and that is all.

2 So the length is the truth. A mark that signals the end of a string (a null byte) is not part of the value, and scanning the bytes to count the length does not happen.

In plain words

A null-terminated string is a design that hides the length inside the value. So to know the length one has to scan, and if the mark is missing while scanning, what lies beyond is read — in practice the most common memory accident came from here. A piece carries its length outside, so that accident has no place to stand.

3 The null-terminated shape is needed only where C is met (§6.9.2). It is a shape built at the boundary, not a type this language has.

4 Handling characters — cutting · searching · joining · Unicode checks — is the work of libraries (§9.5). The processor knows only pieces and bytes.

6.2.18 Type names without meaning§

1 Some names are accepted but have no meaning. If such a name is written in a signature, the processor warns (W-NOT-YET). Those names are gathered in annex A.9 (2) — names such as byte · char · str · string · bytes_view · dyn · atomic.

2 Having no meaning means that the processor does not know the shape of the values that name holds. Neither the size, the layout, nor what can be done with the value is fixed, so checks that rely on that name do not stand.

Caution —

This is why this place shall speak. Translation passes and answers come out, so the checks that did not stand show nowhere. What silently dropped out, nobody finds — so the processor says so.

3 There are two ways to give a name meaning: write the underlying type directly, or declare that name (type str option u64 .). Once declared, it has a meaning, so there is no warning.

Example — a name without meaning is warned about but not refused

Source

module ex_name_only .

rem `str` is not built in — without a meaning it gets W-NOT-YET. Here it is given a meaning by declaring it.
type str option u64 .

fn f input s str . output u8 . do return 1 . end .

Output

f() = 1

6.2.19 Variants that carry values — building and taking apart§

1 The variants of an enum can carry values. When writing a variant, the names and types of what it carries are written with it.

2 The shape that builds such a value is <enum>.<variant>, and it gives all of what the variant was declared to carry. If something is missing it is refused (E-ENUM-ARITY) — a half-built value is not made silently.

3 There are two ways to handle an enum that carries values.

Table 25 — The two ops that handle variants carrying values

opShapeWhat it does
isaisa <value> <variant>asks whether it is that variant now
payloadpayload <value> <variant> <name>reads what that variant carries

4 Naming a variant that does not exist is refused (E-ENUM-NOVARIANT), and trying to read a name that variant does not carry is refused (E-ENUM-NOFIELD). Both are decided at translation — the variant and the name are written in the source, so there is no reason to wait for execution.

5 In an enum with several variants, payload can be used only after it has been narrowed to that variant (E-ENUM-UNCHECKED). What narrows is isa, usually written as guard isa <value> <variant> else ….

5a Using payload at a place already narrowed to another variant is refused too (E-ENUM-VARIANT) — at that place it has already been proven to be another variant, so that payload reads the wrong thing.

5b The type of what a variant carries is written as one word (E-ENUM-PAYLOAD). A variant line has no mark that separates fields, so for types written as several words (slice u8 · option t · owned t · ref t) where they break would not be fixed. This is a restriction of spelling, not of size — the size of the carried value is not asked about. A multi-word type is written by naming a struct that has it as a field and using that name.

In plain words

What (5) does is prevent «reading what is not there». Reading without narrowing is right only when the value is that variant, and when it is not it reads unrelated bytes — that is not an answer being wrong but an answer that is right by chance, the shape this language dislikes most.

Example — a multi-word type is carried through a named struct

Source

module ex_enum_named_payload .

struct bytes do
  b slice u8 .
end .

struct pt do
  x u64 .
  y u64 .
  z u64 .
end .

enum msg do
  data d bytes .
  dot p pt .
  blank .
end .

fn size_of_data input m msg . output usize . do
  guard isa m. data . else return 0 . .
  let d bytes payload m. data d . .
  return len field d. b . . .
end .

export fn f input s slice u8 . output usize . do
  let m msg msg.data lit bytes do b s. . end . . .
  return size_of_data m. . .
end .

export fn g output u64 . do
  let m msg msg.dot lit pt do x 1 . y 2 . z 7 . end . . .
  guard isa m. dot . else return 0 . .
  let p pt payload m. dot p . .
  return add add field p. x . field p. y . . field p. z . . .
end .

Output

f([1,2,3]) = 3 · g() = 10

6 If an enum carries itself as a value, its size is not fixed, so it is refused (E-ENUM-INFINITE). A recursive structure is built by putting something in between.

In plain words

A variant that carries values is not a new construct. There is just one field that indicates the variant and an aggregate holding what the variant carries; building is building an aggregate, and reading is reading a field. So this feature added not a single machine instruction — it lowers to what already existed, so it ran on both back ends by itself.

6.2.20 Seeing scattered pieces as one — segments§

1 Several pieces that are not contiguous can be seen with one eye. It consists of two things: the bytes lying behind, and a descriptor that says where in them and how much.

Table 26 — The ops that handle scattered pieces

opShapeWhat it does
view_segmentsview_segments <bytes> <descriptor>binds the two into one eye
segview.piece_countsegview.piece_count <eye>counts how many pieces there are (standard module segview)
segview.piece_atsegview.piece_at <eye> <number>gives the piece with that number as a piece (slice) (standard module segview)

1a An eye is an aggregate holding the fields back (the bytes behind) and descs (the descriptor). The number of pieces and the piece of a number are written by reading and cutting those fields, so they are not operations of the language but ops of the standard module segview (§9.3).

2 A descriptor is a piece holding pairs of «from where» and «how much». So these three add neither a new type nor a new machine instruction — they lower to building an aggregate, reading fields and cutting.

In plain words

Without a way to handle scattered pieces, the user has no choice but to gather them into one place. Gathering is copying, and copying where it need not be done is the cost this language sets out to remove. Given an eye, the copying disappears.

6.2.21 Fetching ahead — prefetch§

1 prefetch <piece> <number> tells the machine that the bytes at that place will be used soon.

2 This is only a hint. It leaves no value and does not change the meaning of the program, and if the machine ignores it, the answer is the same.

Caution —

A hint is a hint only if being wrong does not make the program wrong. Something that changes the meaning is not a hint but a command, and such things do not come in this place.

6.2.22 What it means for types to match§

1 In every place where a value is put, the type of the value shall be the same as the type that place states.

Table 27 — Places where types do not match

DiagnosticWhere
E-TYPE-LETbinding with let — the value given differs from the type written
E-TYPE-VARbinding with var — the same mismatch, at a different place
E-TYPE-SETrewriting — the new value differs from the type of that name
E-TYPE-RETURNreturning — what is returned differs from what the op states
E-TYPE-ARGpassing — an argument differs from the parameter
E-TYPE-FIELDmaking — a field that does not exist is given, a field’s value differs from its type, or a field is missing
E-TYPE-TRYtry — a value with nothing to strip off

2 Aggregates and enums are declared with struct <name> do … end . · enum <name> do … end .. Trying to declare them in another shape is refused (E-TYPE-DECL).

3 Even with the same field layout, different names are different aggregates (E-TYPE-STRUCT, §8.9).

In plain words

The seven in this table are all different places of the same sentence — the source wrote “the type of what comes here is this”, and the processor compares what is written with what actually comes. There is a separate diagnostic for each place because where it mismatched is the fact the person fixing it needs most.

6.2.23 The shape of writing fields§

1 A field of an aggregate is a name followed by a type (x u64 .). Anything not in that shape is refused (E-FIELD-FORM).

2 A field is read with field <value> <name> …. The old spelling that reads with an attached dot does not exist in this language (E-FIELD-GLUED).

3 A vec and a mask are values themselves, and pointing or borrowing markers cannot be attached to them (E-VEC-QUAL). Their lanes are positions inside a word, not positions in memory.

Rejected example — a field is a name followed by a type

Source

module ex_field_form .

struct p do
  x .              rem there is no type
end .

fn f output u8 . do return 1 . end .

Diagnostic: E-FIELD-FORM

6.2.24 Integers of arbitrary width — bits§

1 type <name> bits <number> . makes an integer type with that many bits. The width is from 1 to 64.

2 The width need not be a power of 2 — bits 3 and bits 10 both stand.

Caution —

If the width does not match the machine word, speed is not guaranteed. What this language guarantees is correctness — bits 10 gives the answer computed in ten bits, but it does not say that this is faster than a sixteen-bit computation. To avoid making places whose cost is invisible, what is not guaranteed shall be written down as not guaranteed.

6.3 Expressions and operations§

1 An expression is something that produces a value. A literal is an expression, a name is an expression, and the application of an operation is an expression.

6.3.1 Prefix notation is the default§

1 Expressions are written in prefix notation — the name of the operation comes first and the arguments follow.

2 Prefix notation has no precedence rules. What is computed first is said entirely by the dots and the order — a form is opened by a name and closed by its own dot (§6.1.5), and the inner form closes first.

2a Nesting calls needs no parentheses — in add 1 mul a. b. . . the first dot closes mul and the second closes add. The end of a form is decided by that form’s dot. The number of arguments an operation takes does not decide the end — whether the number of arguments is right is looked at separately after the form has closed (E-IR-ARITY if not).

2b Parentheses are decoration that makes a grouping stand out — add 1 (mul a. b. .) . is the same expression as that of (2a). A form inside parentheses closes with its own dot too, and the parentheses do not change the meaning.

2c The order in which operands are computed is a free choice (§4.3). The processor may compute the operands of an operation in any order. Core ops · builtin ops and ops the author declares are the same. But the following are fixed.

  • The operation is done after all its operands have been computed.
  • Each operand is computed only once.
  • Operands that produce effects are computed in the order written, from the left. Here an effect means an effect other than panic (§7) —

being able to stop does not by itself bind the order.

  • and and or are the exception — they compute the first operand first, and if that alone decides the result, they do not compute

the second operand (§6.3.3 (4)).

Free choice — FC-0001 — the order in which operands are computed

The processor may compute, in any order, those operands of an operation that produce no effect. That order is the only range of choice — computing all operands before the operation, computing each once, and computing effect-producing operands in the order written are fixed. In most programs the answer is the same whatever the order. There is one time the difference shows — when two operands stop for different reasons, which stop occurs. If that difference matters, put what is to be computed first into a name with let — statements are executed in the order written.

Example — prefix notation

Source

let a u32 add 1 2 . .
let b u32 add 1 mul 2 3 . . .
let c bool lt a. b. . .

In plain words

Someone seeing 1 + 2 * 3 for the first time has to memorise whether * or + comes first. add 1 mul 2 3 . . has nothing to memorise — the dots say it as it is. That is why this language takes prefix as the default.

6.3.2 Infix notation — the expr island§

1 Long arithmetic is hard to read, so at a place that begins with expr infix notation can be used. This place is called an island.

2 The notation inside an island turns into exactly the same meaning as prefix. An island is only a projection of notation and has no execution cost.

3 Precedence exists only inside islands. The following table is all of it.

Table 28 — Precedence inside an expr island (strongest at the top)

LevelOperationAssociativityPrefix with the same meaning
5( ) grouping—only a grouping mark, not a value
4* /leftmul · div
3+ -leftadd · sub
2eq ne lt le gt gecannot be chainedthe prefix operation of the same name
1bandleftand (short-circuit)
1aorleftor (short-circuit)

4 and binds more strongly than or. That is, expr a or b and c is the same as or a. and b. c. . ..

5 Comparisons cannot be chained. expr a lt b lt c is refused.

6 The table above is all of the island. This set is closed and does not grow. Operations not in the table — including bit operations (§6.3.5) · the remainder mod · the minimum min and maximum max — are written in prefix even inside an island.

7 There are no unary operations in an island either. To flip a sign, write expr 0 - a, or wrap a prefix form in parentheses (expr (not b. .) or c. .).

Caution — The island is small not because it lacks something

The only value of infix notation is readability, and that holds only when nobody has to look up the precedence. The twelve in the table are the same as the arithmetic learned at school, and everyone reads them the same way — nobody disputes that multiplication binds more strongly than addition. Bit operations are exactly the opposite. In C, a & b == c does not group as (a & b) == c but as a & (b == c). When a · b · c are all 6 the former is true and the latter false — the answers diverge. This is a trap that catches even people who have written C for a long time, and many languages fix this precedence differently from one another. So putting bit operations in the island would not make it easier to read but would increase what has to be looked up. Prefix notation has no precedence at all (§6.3.1), so the parentheses say the answer — it gets longer but cannot be wrong. This language chooses not the shorter side but the side that cannot be wrong (§1.3).

In plain words

A notation projection exists to make things easier to read, not to give every operation a second grammar. If there are two ways of writing the same meaning, a reader has to know both — that cost is greater than what is gained by shortening.

Example — infix notation

Source

module ex_infix .

export fn score input a u32 . input b u32 . output u32 .
  requires le a. 1000 . .
  requires le b. 1000 . .
do
  return expr a. + b. * 2 . .
end .

Rejected example — comparisons cannot be chained

Source

module ex_chain .

proc p input a u32 . input b u32 . input c u32 . output bool . effects none .
do
  return expr a. lt b. lt c. . .     rem two comparisons cannot be chained
end .

Diagnostic: E-EXPR-CHAIN

Caution — Why comparisons cannot be chained

In mathematics a < b < c means «a is less than b, and b is less than c». But in many languages that notation is read with a different meaning (first a < b is computed to get a truth value, and that is compared again with c). If the same characters mean different things to people and to machines, that is a place that silently goes wrong. So this language refuses it outright.

6.3.3 Core ops and builtin ops§

1 The operations whose meaning the language fixes are of two kinds. A core op is called anywhere by its name alone and shares one namespace with the names the author makes. A builtin op is called only after call_builtin and has its own namespace ((1c) · (1f)). For both kinds the list of names is fixed, and the whole of it is in annex D. Annex D also states, for each operation, what it takes and what it gives, when translation is refused and when execution stops.

1a Not all the names of core ops have the same rule. Most cannot be used as local names (§6.1.3), but some are operations only within a context, and outside it they can be the author’s names. Which is which is stated by annex D.

1b What is listed below shows the basic ones by kind; it is not all of them. The full list and the meaning of each operation are in annex D.

1c A builtin op stands only after the head word call_builtin: call_builtin <name> <operand>*. Its names form a closed set, and that is the only place it is called. Which names are such is stated by annex D. Those that reach the outside world — files · the network · processes (file_* · dir_* · path_remove · path_rename · link_type · net_* · proc_*), standard input and output (write_out · read_in), arguments and the environment (arg · env_get), clocks (time_now · time_local · time_sleep), terminals (tty_raw · tty_read · tty_size), random numbers (random_bytes), the reactor (reactor_new · r_read · r_write) — take a capability as their first operand: call_builtin net_send <capability> <handle> <bytes>.

1c-1 Builtin ops that take no capability are computations — floating-point functions (§6.3.9), the sum of a piece (§6.3.7.1), and hashing · cryptography · carry-less multiplication · random sequences (hash_bytes · crc32 · sha256 · sha384 · sha512 · aes_round · aes_round_last · aes_ctr · ghash · aes_gcm · chacha20 · poly1305 · chacha_poly · clmul_lo · clmul_hi · mul_hi · rng_next).

1c-2 The standard library provides ops that wrap these builtin ops (§9.3 (1a)) — stdio · args · env · clock · tty · random · reactor · math and so on. A wrapper passes its operands on as they are, so the meaning is the same.

1f The namespace of builtin ops is separate from the author’s namespace. The word directly after call_builtin is always read as the name of a builtin op, and the same spelling in any other place is always read as the author’s name. So the author can give a spelling the same as a builtin op to their own op · parameter · local, and can use both together in one unit. Since the place where a word stands decides which it is, a spelling never has two meanings in one place.

In plain words

Builtin ops are not meant for ordinary programs to use. They are doors the processor selects and opens for what cannot be written as a library — computations that go down to machine instructions, work that reaches the outside world, things only the processor knows. Those who use these doors are the people building system libraries and the people building processors; application programs call the ops of the standard library that wrap them (§9). That is also why the namespace is separate — a library must be able to provide its wrappers under natural names such as sha256.

In plain words

Why confine them to a place? If an operation a program uses once enlarges everyone’s vocabulary, the names a reader must memorise grow. That cost is paid not by the one who writes but by everyone who reads. For the same reason pipe stage names have meaning only inside pipe (§6.12), and type words stand only in their own places.

1d If a word called by its bare name is not among the author’s names and has the spelling of a builtin op, the processor regards it as having left out call_builtin and refuses it (E-BUILTIN-BARE). Putting a word outside that closed set after call_builtin does not conform either (E-BUILTIN-NAME) — that place does not silently pass over to the author’s names.

Rejected example — a builtin op cannot be called by its bare name

Source

module ex_bare .

proc p input s slice u8 . input o mut slice u8 . output u64 . effects none .
do
  return sha256 s. o. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-BUILTIN-BARE

Example — written to fit its place, the same thing stands

Source

module ex_call .

proc p input s slice u8 . input o mut slice u8 . output usize . effects none .
do
  return call_builtin sha256 s. o. . .
end .

Example — the author's sha256 and the builtin op sha256 stand together in one unit

Source

module ex_both .

fn sha256 input x u64 . output u64 . do
  return add x. 1 . .
end .

proc p input s slice u8 . input o mut slice u8 . output u64 . effects none .
do
  let n usize call_builtin sha256 s. o. . .
  return sha256 cast u64 n. . . .
end .

1e There are three ways of keeping names whose meaning the language fixes out of the global vocabulary.

  • Head — the name position of call_builtin <name> ((1c)). Builtin ops are such.
  • Prefix — a group of ops shares the same leading part: bitset_* (sets of small numbers, §6.2.12) ·

lane_* and reduce_* (lanes) · atomic_* · wrap_* · sat_* · chk_* · narrow_*. Prefixed names are global names, but since the group does not occupy short words (union · remove · select · any …), the author can use those words as their own names.

  • Place — words that have meaning only inside a fixed context. The stages and terminals of pipe are such (§6.12):

the terminals any · all are words inside pipe, and the lane predicates are lane_any · lane_all. An op has one spelling. When a name is moved, the old spelling is not left behind as a synonym.

2 The arithmetic operations are add sub mul div mod. The spelling of the operation that gives a remainder is mod, and rem is the word that opens a comment (§6.1.2) — the same spelling is not used for two meanings.

2a mod is the remainder of integers. The remainder of floating-point numbers is fmod (§6.3.9), and using mod on floating-point operands is refused at translation (E-TYPE-KIND, 2026-11-02 — before that it passed the check and stopped during execution).

3 The comparison operations are eq ne lt le gt ge, and their result is bool.

3a A comparison between integers follows the same rule as arithmetic — the two operands shall be comparable by widening, and if they cannot be, translation is refused (E-TYPE-SIGN, §6.3.4 (1)). The processor does not secretly change signs for a comparison. To compare the mathematical values of two integers even when their signs or widths differ, call cmp_eq · cmp_lt · cmp_le of the standard library intops by name (§9) — intops.cmp_lt i64. u64. a. b. . is true when a is −1 and b is 0. Those ops take any pair of integer types (usize · idiff · 128 bits included).

4 The logical operations are and or not, and their operands and results are all bool. and and or short-circuit — if the first part alone decides the result, the second is not computed.

5 The slice operations are len idx (§6.2.6).

6 The conversion operations are widen narrow (§6.2.5).

7 The bit operations are in §6.3.5.

Free choice — FC-0002 — when NaN takes part in min and max

In min · max of floating-point numbers, if one operand is NaN, the processor gives the value of the side that is not NaN, or gives NaN. Which of the two is chosen by the processor. If neither operand is NaN, the result is uniquely fixed. Machine min · max instructions answer differently at this place, so nailing it to one side would slow down what one instruction could finish.

8 neg flips the sign. The operand shall be a signed integer or a floating-point number — an unsigned integer has no negative numbers, so it is refused at translation (E-TYPE-SIGN). The type of the result is the type of the operand.

8a neg is arithmetic too. If the result does not fit its width, it stops (§6.3.4 (3)) — the smallest value of a signed type is that case (−128 of i8).

In plain words

The names may look unfamiliar, but the rule is one — words instead of symbols. + is add, < is lt (less than), == is eq (equal). Symbols are not used for two reasons: symbols differ subtly in meaning from language to language (for example ^ is exponentiation in some places and exclusive or in others), and a notation that can be read out as words carries over as it is to voice input and reading aloud.

6.3.4 The rules of arithmetic§

1 The two operands of binary arithmetic shall be comparable by widening (§6.2.5). If they are not comparable, translation is refused. If only their widths differ, they are computed at the wider one.

1a Even with mixed signs, it can be computed if there is a widening that keeps the value. It is enough that the unsigned side fits in the result type without losing its value — u8 and i16 can be mixed.

1b If there is no such widening, translation is refused. u32 and i32 have the same width, so the unsigned side cannot fit in the signed side, and they cannot be mixed.

2 The type of the result is the wider of the two operands. The processor does not invent a third type that is neither of the two operands.

3 Arithmetic happens at the declared width. If the result does not fit that width, it traps.

4 Dividing an integer by 0 traps. Dividing a floating-point number by 0 gives infinity, as IEEE 754 specifies.

5 div and mod are interpreted with the declared sign.

5a div truncates the quotient towards 0 — div -7 2 . is −3.

5b mod is the remainder of floored division. The sign of the remainder follows the divisor — mod -7 2 . is 1, mod 7 -2 . is −1. So when the divisor is positive the result is always 0 or more and less than the divisor: if 0 < n then 0 ≤ mod i n < n, even when i is negative.

5c So div and mod are not a pair. (div a b) × b + (mod a b) equals a only when the two numbers have the same sign or divide evenly. The remainder that pairs with the quotient truncated towards 0 is written sub a. mul div a. b. . b. . ..

5d mod of the smallest value of a signed type by −1 is 0. div stops at the same place (the quotient is not in that width), but the remainder 0 is within that width.

In plain words

Why does mod follow the sign of the divisor? The place where a remainder is used is almost always choosing a position — the slot of a ring buffer, the slot of a hash table, the day of the week. The property needed there is «the result is 0 or more and less than the size», and only the remainder of floored division keeps it even for negative numbers. C’s % gives −1 for -7 % 2, so that property breaks.

Example — even with mixed signs, a widening that keeps the value is enough

Source

module ex_mixsign .

rem `u8` fits in `i16` without losing its value.
fn ok_widen input a u8 . input b i16 . output i16 . do return add a. b. . . end .

rem A wider place may be chosen for the result.
fn ok_wider input a u8 . input b i16 . output i32 . do return add a. b. . . end .

Output

ok_widen(200, -100) = 100 · ok_wider(200, -100) = 100

Rejected example — without a widening that keeps the value it is refused

Source

module ex_sign .

proc p input a i32 . input b u32 . output i32 . effects none .
do
  return add a. b. . .     rem `u32` does not fit in `i32` — the widths are the same
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-SIGN

Caution — Overflow does not wrap silently

In many languages adding 1 to 255 of u8 gives 0 (wrapping). Lowent traps. If wrapping is needed, use an operation written as such, like wrap_add — whether wrapping was wanted or was a mistake shall remain in the source.

In plain words

“It stops when it overflows” may look inconvenient. But if computation continues with an overflowed value, that program’s answer is already wrong; it merely becomes unknown where it went wrong. Stopping is the failure that can be fixed.

Note (informative)

Why traps do not hurt performance is in §6.4.6. If a contract proves the range, that check disappears at translation time — that is, writing honestly makes it faster.

6.3.5 Bitwise operations§

1 Bit operations treat a value as a sequence of bits. Operands and results are integers, and signed integers can be handled too.

1′ If the two operands of bit_and · bit_or · bit_xor are of the same type, the result is of that type too; if one side is a literal, it is of the other side’s type. The result of bit_not and of shifts · rotations (shl · shr · rotl · rotr) is of the type of the (left) operand. Putting usize · idiff and a fixed-width integer in one operation is refused at translation (E-TYPE-NOMINAL, §6.2 (3-2)) — the shift count is not covered by this rule.

1″ If the two operands of bit_and · bit_or · bit_xor are integers that differ only in width or sign, they follow the same rule as arithmetic (§6.3.4 (1)–(2)): if they are comparable by widening, the result is of the wider type; if not, translation is refused (E-TYPE-SIGN). The result of count_ones · leading_zeros · trailing_zeros is of the type of a.

1a The right shift shr means different things depending on the sign. For unsigned integers 0 comes into the vacated places (logical shift); for signed integers the sign bit comes in (arithmetic shift). So a negative number stays negative when shifted right.

2 The bitwise logical operations are bit_and bit_or bit_xor bit_not. The first three take two values and bit_not takes one.

3 The shifts are shl (left) and shr (right). The rotations are rotl and rotr, in which the bits pushed out come back in on the opposite side.

3a The count of a rotation may be of any integer type, and it rotates by the remainder after dividing by the width — rotating a type of width 8 by 9 is the same as rotating it by 1. Rotations do not stop.

4 The shift count shall be less than the width of the type. Otherwise it traps.

4a The count of shl · shr · wrap_shl · wrap_shr is an unsigned integer type (any width). Putting a value of a signed integer type as the count is refused at translation (E-SHIFT-SIGNED) — a negative count has no single fixed meaning. To use a signed value as the count, convert it explicitly to an unsigned type. A literal takes the type of its place, so it is used as it is (negative literals: (9)).

5 wrap_shl and wrap_shr wrap the shift count into the width before using it. On a type of width 8, 9 places are read as 1 place.

Rejected example — the shift count is an unsigned integer type

Source

module ex_shift_signed .

fn up input a u8 . input n i8 . output u8 . do return shl a. n. . . end .

Diagnostic: E-SHIFT-SIGNED

6 The counting operations are count_ones (the number of 1 bits), leading_zeros (the number of consecutive 0s from the front) and trailing_zeros (the number of consecutive 0s from the back).

7 byte_swap reverses the order of the bytes. It is used when exchanging values with a place of different endianness.

Example — bitwise logic and inversion

Source

module ex_bitlogic .

rem 12 = 0000_1100 · 10 = 0000_1010
fn mask input a u8 . input b u8 . output u8 . do return bit_and a. b. . . end .
fn both input a u8 . input b u8 . output u8 . do return bit_or  a. b. . . end .
fn diff input a u8 . input b u8 . output u8 . do return bit_xor a. b. . . end .
fn flip input a u8 . output u8 . do return bit_not a. . . end .

Output

mask(12,10) = 8 · both(12,10) = 14 · diff(12,10) = 6 · flip(12) = 243

In plain words

flip(12) is 243 because it is u8 — inverting 0000_1100 gives 1111_0011, and that is 243. With a type of a different width the answer is different too. The answer of a bit operation is always bound to the width of the type.

Example — shifts and rotations

Source

module ex_bitshift .

fn up   input a u8 . input n u8 . output u8 . do return shl  a. n. . . end .
fn down input a u8 . input n u8 . output u8 . do return shr  a. n. . . end .
fn spin input a u8 . input n u8 . output u8 . do return rotl a. n. . . end .
fn back input a u8 . input n u8 . output u8 . do return rotr a. n. . . end .

Output

up(3,2) = 12 · down(12,2) = 3 · spin(129,1) = 3 · back(3,1) = 129

In plain words

spin(129, 1) being 3 shows the difference between rotating and shifting. 129 is 1000_0001, and rotating it one place left brings the leading 1 round to the end, giving 0000_0011 = 3. Shifting the same value with shl throws that 1 away.

Caution — A shift count of the width or more traps — a place that differs from C

Shifting a value of width 8 by 9 places stops the processor (E-VM-SHIFT). In C the same thing is undefined behaviour; the program keeps running and the machine decides the answer. Here it is a contract. If wrapping is wanted, say it by name — wrap_shl · wrap_shr. At width 8, 9 places are read as 1 place (wrap_shr(12, 9) = 6). Wrapping silently and writing that you want wrapping are different things (§1.3).

Example — the sign changes the meaning of a shift

Source

module ex_signed_shift .

rem Signed integer — the sign bit comes into the vacated places (arithmetic shift). The count is an unsigned type ((4a)).
fn s_shr input a i32 . input n u32 . output i32 . do return shr a. n. . . end .

rem Unsigned integer — 0 comes into the vacated places (logical shift).
fn u_shr input a u32 . input n u32 . output u32 . do return shr a. n. . . end .

rem Bitwise logical operations handle signed integers too.
fn s_and input a i32 . input b i32 . output i32 . do return bit_and a. b. . . end .
fn s_not input a i32 . output i32 . do return bit_not a. . . end .

Output

s_shr(-8, 1) = -4 · u_shr(4294967288, 1) = 2147483644 · s_and(-8, 12) = 8 · s_not(0) = -1

In plain words

-8 and 4294967288 are the same bit pattern in 32 bits. But shifting them one place right, the answers diverge — the former gives -4, the latter 2147483644. Being a bit operation does not mean the type is not looked at. The sign decides the meaning.

Example — counting and byte reversal

Source

module ex_bitcount .

fn ones input a u8 . output u8 . do return count_ones a. . . end .
fn lead input a u8 . output u8 . do return leading_zeros a. . . end .
fn tail input a u8 . output u8 . do return trailing_zeros a. . . end .
fn endian input a u32 . output u32 . do return byte_swap a. . . end .

Output

ones(7) = 3 · lead(1) = 7 · tail(8) = 3 · endian(1) = 16777216

In plain words

lead(1) being 7 is also because of the width — in u8, 1 is 0000_0001, so there are seven leading zeros. endian(1) is 16777216 because reversing the bytes of 0x00000001 gives 0x01000000.

8 Bit operations take only integers — bits are a property of machine words, so for values without that property they have no meaning. This mismatch is caught at the place of execution (E-BITOP-TYPE).

Caution —

Here is a place that cannot be caught at translation. It is written down because, if it were not, readers would believe that translation protects them — letting people believe that something is protected when it is not is the worst thing a norm can do (§8.6).

9 If the shift count is proven not to be less than the width of its type, it is refused (E-SHIFT-RANGE).

6.3.6 Choosing by name what to do on overflow§

1 add · sub · mul stop on overflow (§6.3.4). If another disposition is needed, use the op whose name states that disposition. That is, a disposition is not a setting but a word.

Table 29 — Arithmetic whose disposition is chosen by name

DispositionopWhat happens on overflow
stop (default)add · sub · mulit traps — it does not go on with a wrong value
wrapwrap_add · wrap_sub · wrap_mulit wraps round within the width
saturatesat_add · sat_sub · sat_mulit stops at the end value of the width
as a valuechk_add · chk_sub · chk_mulit returns the overflow as a value (⟦option⟧)
no checkdiv_nzdivision whose divisor is guaranteed by its type not to be 0. No check remains at that place

2 Division by 0 is the same. At a place where the divisor is guaranteed by its type not to be 0, use div_nz, and no check remains at that place.

2a The way to obtain that guarantee is nonzero_of. nonzero_of <value> reports as a value that the value is not 0 (⟦option⟧) — none if it is 0. Once obtained, it is not asked again at each place that divides.

In plain words

Instead of checking «not divided by 0» every time, proving it once and carrying it as a value is the same as what this language does with contracts. Removing a check is not ignoring the danger; it is confirming in one place that there is no danger and carrying that fact in the type.

2b To receive an overflowing carry · borrow as a value instead of discarding it, use the standard library intops (§9) — add_carry_<width> returns the sum and the carry (0 or 1), sub_borrow_<width> the difference and the borrow, and mul_wide_<width> the lower half lo and the upper half hi of the product, as a struct (for signed widths mul_wide_s_<width> — lo is unsigned and hi signed). The widths are unsigned u8 · u16 · u32 · u64 · usize and signed i8 · i16 · i32 · i64 · idiff. All are written in Lowent (§9.3 (5)).

3 Reducing a width also chooses its disposition by name.

Table 30 — The ops that change width

opShapeWhat it does
widenwiden <type> <value>widens. The value is preserved, so no disposition is needed
narrownarrow <type> <value>reduces. If it does not fit, it stops
narrow_wrapwraps if it does not fit
narrow_satstands at the end value if it does not fit
narrow_tryreports as a value if it does not fit (⟦option⟧)

In plain words

Why is the disposition a name and not a build setting? As a setting, the same source gives a different answer for each build, and which answer it is cannot be known by reading the source. As a name, whether wrapping was wanted or was a mistake remains at that place — the same principle this language repeats in contracts and effects.

6.3.7 Ops that handle lanes§

1 A vec (§6.2.11) does arithmetic per lane separately, but crossing lanes is done by separate ops.

Table 31 — Ops that handle lanes

opShapeWhat it does
splatsplat <value>fills every lane with one value
loadload <piece> <position>reads lanes from memory
storestore <piece> <position> <value>writes lanes to memory
load_masked · store_masked＋ a mask and a defaultreads or writes only the lanes whose mask is on
lane_reverselane_reverse <value>reverses the order of the lanes
lane_rotatelane_rotate <value> <places>rotates the lanes by that much
reduce_add · reduce_mulreduce_add <value>adds · multiplies all lanes
reduce_min · reduce_maxreduce_min <value>the smallest · largest of the lanes
lane_avglane_avg <value> <value>the rounded average per lane
native_lanesnative_lanes <type>asks at translation time how many lanes this machine handles at once

Caution —

The value of lane_avg is fixed as (a+b+1)>>1. If the lane is narrow, a+b overflows that lane, so the processor shall add in a wider place and then narrow — the two back ends of this edition do that. A processor that later wants to lower this to one machine instruction shall check that the instruction gives exactly this value. x86′s PAVGB/PAVGW and ARM’s VRHADD/URHADD give this value, but for RISC-V’s vaaddu the rounding mode depends on the vxrm register, so that cannot be said. If the same name gives different values on different machines, that drops something this language has decided to keep.

1a splat takes the lane count from the declared type. So the only place it can be used is the value position of a binding that writes a vector type: var lim vec 4 u32 splat 5 . .. Writing it directly inside an expression does not conform (E-VEC-SPLAT) — nothing in that place says the lane count.

1b lane_avg belongs to integer lanes (the value above is an integer computation). Using it on floating-point lanes is refused at translation (E-TYPE-KIND, 2026-11-02).

2 An op that crosses lanes gathers as many as the lane count into one. The type of the result is the type of the lanes. Signed lanes are added and multiplied by sign, and if the value of reduce_add · reduce_mul does not fit that type, it stops (§6.3.4 (3)).

Note (informative)

Earlier, the front of this table listed sum and sum_fast as «adds all lanes». No op did that under those names — what adds lanes is reduce_add, and the name sum was being used in the processor for the floating-point sum of a piece (§6.3.7.1). One name meant two things, so the names were separated (2026-09-17).

6.3.7.1 Adding up a piece§

1 There are two builtin ops that add up all the floating-point numbers held in a piece (⟦slice⟧), and the name says how it adds. The standard library math wraps them as ops of the same names (math.sum_neumaier · math.sum_seq).

1a sum_neumaier <piece> adds while collecting the lost digits separately and giving them back. So its error is not bound to the number of terms.

1b sum_seq <piece> adds once from front to back. It is fast, and its error is proportional to the number of terms.

Rejected example — returning a floating-point sum in an integer place

Source

module ex_slice_sum .

use math .

fn total input b slice u8 . output u64 .
do
  var xs slice f64 view_array f64 b. . .
  return math.sum_neumaier xs. . .      rem the result is a floating-point number, but the head says `u64`
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-RETURN

2 The result of both ops is a floating-point number. Returning it as it is to a place written as an integer is refused (E-TYPE-RETURN).

In plain words

Why does the name say the method? Adding [1e16, 1.0, -1e16] from front to back with sum_seq gives 0, and adding it with sum_neumaier gives 1 — neither is a wrong answer; they are different promises. If the name were only sum, the caller could not know which promise it got, and switching to the faster one would become silently changing the answer. Cost and error are what the name carries in this language.

Caution —

native_lanes is not a value that changes the answer. The lane count is part of the type (§6.2.11), so the answer is the same whether the number obtained by this question or a number written by hand is used. But choosing a number that fits the machine computes more at once.

6.3.8 Asking the size — size_of§

1 size_of <type> gives the number of bytes one value of that type occupies, at translation time. The type of that number is usize — so are the alignment align_of gives and the number of lanes native_lanes gives.

2 It cannot be used on a type whose size cannot be known.

In plain words

Without this question, code that takes a type as a parameter would have to assume the widest width. Then a container holding one-byte things uses eight times as much. Being able to ask the size lets code that takes parameters produce its costs honestly.

6.3.9 Transcendental functions§

1 The builtin ops sin · cos · exp · log · pow · sqrt · floor · ceil · round · fmod are floating-point only. The standard library math wraps them as ops of the same names (math.sqrt x.). Application programs call those ops.

1a abs takes floating-point numbers and signed integers. The type of the result is the type of the operand. At the smallest value of a signed integer it stops — its absolute value is not in that width (§6.3.4 (3)).

2 These require no capability and can be used only on machines with an operating system.

Open issue — M-0007 — the last bit of the results of transcendental functions

This document does not fix what value the results of sin · cos · exp · log · pow are, down to the last bit. But which kind of processor discretion it is (§4.3) is not marked. Reading A: leave it a free choice — write the range of choice as something like «one of the two floating-point numbers nearest the true value». Reading B: make it implementation-defined — the processor documents the math library it uses. Reading C: fix it as the correctly rounded value. The processor currently gives the value the machine’s math library gives.

Caution —

What value the results of these functions are, down to the last bit, is not fixed by this document. The machine and the math library under it decide. If a computation depends on the last bit, use them knowing that.

6.3.10 A truth value is not a number§

1 and · or · not take only truth values. Passing an integer is refused (E-TYPE-LOGICAL).

2 Places where a condition comes (§6.5) likewise take only truth values (E-TYPE-COND).

3 That is, there is no promise that 0 is false and everything else true. What is being asked shall be written at that place — as in gt a. 0 ..

In plain words

In languages that let an integer stand in a condition position, a reader guesses from context whether if x means “x is not 0”, “x exists” or “x is true”. A place that needs guessing is also a place where the guess goes wrong. Making people write what is asked removes that place.

Rejected example — an integer is not a condition

Source

module ex_cond .

fn f input a u8 . output u8 .
do
  if a. do return 1 . end .     rem write what is being asked — `gt a. 0 .`
  return 0 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-COND

6.3.11 Widths and lanes are part of the type§

1 Two vecs with different lane counts are different types and do not mix (E-TYPE-LANES).

2 Only a mask can come in a place that takes a mask (E-TYPE-MASK).

3 Even with the same field layout, different names are different types (E-TYPE-WRAP) — the same layout does not make the same type (§8.9).

4 Putting a value outside a narrowed range (§6.2.14) in that place is refused (E-TYPE-RANGE). So is writing a range that no value can satisfy — that is not a condition but a lie.

Rejected example — two with different lane counts are different types

Source

module ex_lanes .

fn f input a vec 4 u8 . input b vec 8 u8 . output vec 4 u8 .
do
  return add a. b. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-LANES

6.3.12 The rules inside an expr island§

1 Each piece inside an expr island (§6.3.2) is one form — a. if it is a name, a form closed by its own dot if it is a call (expr twice a. . + 1 .). Since a form has its own end, calls need not be wrapped in parentheses. The island is closed by its own dot.

2 Inside an island there are only infix operators. There is no one-place operator attached in front (E-EXPR-UNARY) — to flip a sign, call the op written as such (neg a. .).

Rejected example — there is no operator attached in front inside an island

Source

module m .
fn flip input a i32 . output i32 . do
  return expr - a. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-EXPR-UNARY

In plain words

The island exists to let arithmetic be read in the familiar order, not to bring in a second grammar. So what can be done inside an island is narrow, and because it is narrow, the meaning inside does not waver.

6.4 Declarations and contracts§

6.4.1 Declaring an op§

1 An op is a unit of execution with a name and a contract. Ops are divided into two kinds.

pure op (fn)

An op that produces no effect at all. It always gives the same result for the same input. Since it does not touch the outside world, executing it in a different order gives the same result.

effectful op (proc)

An op that produces effects. It shall write in its own contract which effects it produces (§7.1), and if it produces more effects than written, translation is refused.

2 The kind is always written. There is no default — whether it is fn or proc is written in front of the name.

3 The declaration of an op consists of, in order, the name, inputs, output, effects, contract and body. Inputs are written with input, the output with output. The order is laid out so that what comes first is used by what comes after:

  • A named promise (satisfies) comes directly after the name — it says first what this op is.
  • Translation-time inputs (comptime) come next — the types of the following inputs and output use those names.
  • Capability inputs come before data inputs — who permitted it is seen before what it receives.
  • The output comes after the inputs — the output type may use the inputs’ type parameters.
  • Effects come after the output and before the contract — they write what is done with the capabilities received earlier.
  • The contract is in the order input conditions (requires) · output promises (ensures) · failures (errors) · tests (tests).

3a The clauses of the head are written in one order only. From front to back: satisfies · lowdoc · vector · priority · comptime inputs · capability and region inputs (whose type is cap … · region …) · use · data inputs · output · effects · link · variadic · asm · absorbs · reference · why · access · inplace · invalidates · parallel · reduce · requires · ensures · errors · tests · schedule. Clauses in the same position keep the order in which they are written. The method signatures of a trait (§6.11.2) and procs inside an actor follow the same order. Violating this order is refused at translation (E-CLAUSE-ORDER). The order of inputs is also the order of the caller’s arguments — so capabilities are always passed before data.

3b The formatter (--fmt) moves clauses that are not inputs, and use clauses, into this order. It does not move inputs among themselves — moving inputs would change the meaning of calls.

3c Each clause of the head is closed by its own dot — the same as statements (§6.1.6 (2)). A clause word does not close the previous clause, and neither does a newline. Meeting the next clause word or do without a dot is refused at translation (E-DOT-MISSING). The list of clauses is the head, so clauses begin with the first word after the name — even if the name has the same spelling as a clause word (proc link …), it is read as the name.

3d The meanings of the clause words that appear in the order of (3a), and where they are defined, are as follows.

  • satisfies — a named promise this op keeps (§6.4.5 · §6.11.2). lowdoc — a description for people to read (§6.4.3).
  • vector · priority — make this op an interrupt handler: the number of the interrupt it receives and its urgency (§7.7).
  • comptime inputs — parameters fixed at translation time (§6.8). Capability inputs — the right to produce effects (§7.2).
  • use — the allocator this op uses (§8.14).
  • link · variadic — the name on the C side · arguments whose number is not fixed (§7.4).
  • asm — the place that writes machine instructions directly (§6.9.1).
  • absorbs · reference · why — the declaration that the spread of unsafe stops here, and the pure version and assumptions that ground it (§7.2.1).
  • access · inplace · invalidates — what it touches and in which mode · writing in place · what it invalidates (§6.4.3 · §8.12).
  • parallel · reduce — a loop that runs divided, and the place where the steps gather (§6.5.10).
  • requires · ensures · errors · tests — the clauses of the contract (§6.4.3).
  • schedule — comes only in the head of a test (test). schedule explore_interleavings . runs every order in which messages are delivered

and checks that the result is the same for each order. With limit <number> it runs only up to that number. If the result differs by order, the test fails (E-SCHED-NONDET). The meaning is specified by §6.4.13 (4).

3e The output can be named — output <name> <type> .. That name is a local of the body and starts at 0: 0 · 0.0 for numbers, false for bool, none for option, and for a struct the 0 of each field. The body writes to that name and returns it with return <name> . (as with other ops that return a value, every path shall end with return — §6.5.5 (3)).

  • Distinguishing: there is one namespace (§6.10), so if the first word after output is a type name, the whole output is a type;

if it is not a type name and what follows is one type, that word is the name of the result.

  • A type without a fixed zero (slices · result · owned · enums · newtypes · generic parameters, and structs with such fields)

cannot be named (E-RESULT-NOZERO). Such an output is written without a name and returns a value.

  • An op without a body (extern · the method signatures of a trait) cannot name its result (E-RESULT-NAMED).
  • An output that returns an array (output [<name>] array <length> <type> .) has its place provided by the caller — §6.2.6 (1c).
  • ensures still refers to the result as ret (§6.4.12). The name of the result is visible only inside the body.

Example — a named result starts at 0

Source

module ex_named_result .

struct span do
  lo u64 .
  hi u64 .
  seen bool .
end .

export fn bounds input xs slice u64 . output s span .
do
  for x xs. do
    if or not field s. seen . . lt x. field s. lo . . . do set field s. lo . x. . end .
    if gt x. field s. hi . . do set field s. hi . x. . end .
    set field s. seen . true .
  end .
  return s. .
end .

Rejected example — a name given to a type without a fixed zero

Source

module ex_named_nozero .

fn first input xs slice u8 . output r slice u8 .
do
  return xs. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-RESULT-NOZERO

Example — declaring an op

Source

module ex_op .

export fn twice input n u32 . output u32 .
  requires le n. 2147483647 . .
do
  return mul n. 2 . .
end .

Rejected example — the output written before the input

Source

module ex_clause_order .

fn twice output u32 .
  input n u32 .
  requires le n. 2147483647 . .
do
  return mul n. 2 . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-CLAUSE-ORDER

Rejected example — a head clause without a dot

Source

module ex_clause_dot .

fn twice input n u32 output u32 .
do
  return mul n. 2 . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-DOT-MISSING

In plain words

export is the mark that lets other modules use this op. Without it, it is used only inside its module.

6.4.2 What a contract is§

1 A contract is the promise an op writes for itself about its input and output. A contract is not a comment but a statement that is checked.

2 Contracts are used in three places.

3 a) The processor uses them as facts — on the premise that the contract is true, it removes checks (§6.4.5).

4 b) They are enforced during execution — a contract the processor could not prove is confirmed during execution, and if broken it traps. But whether the remaining checks are actually kept is decided by the build mode (§6.4.8).

5 c) They speak to readers — what must be kept to call this op is visible without opening the body.

6 There are two places where a contract breaks, and the diagnostic separates whose fault it is.

Table 32 — When a contract is broken

ClauseWhen it is checkedWhose fault if broken
requireson entrythe caller’s — it did not keep the condition it had to keep
ensureson exitthis op’s — it broke its own promise (the caller was told a lie)

In plain words

This distinction really pays off. When a program stops, the diagnostic says at once “did I call it wrongly, or was that function built wrongly”.

Caution — A contract is an asset, not a cost

It is easy to think “writing checks makes it slower”. In Lowent it is the opposite. When a contract is written, the processor uses it as a fact and removes checks during execution. If it is not written, the processor does not know, so the check remains — that is, writing honestly is faster.
