6.4.3 The six clauses of a contract§

1 A contract consists of the following six clauses. Not every clause is required.

precondition (requires)

A condition that shall be true at the moment of entering the op. The caller is responsible for keeping it. It is checked on entry, and if it is broken it traps.

postcondition (ensures)

A condition that is true when the op ends normally. The maker is responsible for keeping it. The value returned can be referred to by the name ret.

error condition (errors)

When the op fails, and the error value then. It states which error comes under which condition. The condition is read with the values on entry to the op — ⟦(1c)⟧.

effect declaration (effects)

The list of effects this op produces (§7.1). If it produces no effect at all it is effects none.

access declaration (access)

What this op touches, and how — written access <name> <mode> .. The modes are the seven in the table below and no more; any other word is refused (E-CONTRACT-MODE).

Table 33 — The modes of access — a closed set of seven

ModeEnforced nowMeaning
shared_readyesdoes not write to this place. Writing is refused
write_onlyyesdoes not read this place. Reading is refused
sequentialnogoes through in order
randomnotouches any place
streamingnopasses once and does not look again
tilednogoes through in blocks
read_mostlynomostly reads and rarely writes

examples (tests)

Examples that show how this op is used. The processor can run them as tests.

1a The clause tests <name>… writes one or more names of ops that test this op. The names written shall be ops visible at that place; if not, it is refused (E-CONTRACT-UNDEF). The name of a test block (§6.4.13) is not an op, so it cannot be written here. The tests clause neither defines nor runs tests — it only points at what already exists. If an op pointed at disappears, the head reports it.

1b The lowdoc clause writes the description of this op. One line is lowdoc "<text>" .; several lines are opened with lowdoc text <end mark>, and the text runs up to the line on which that mark stands alone. This text is read by the document output (--doc) and does not affect the meaning of the translated program.

1c The condition of the errors clause is read with the values on entry to the op.

1c1 If the body never raises that error, the processor reports it (W-ERRORS-UNRAISED). The clause is read as the promise «this op can fail this way», and the caller builds branches by looking at it; a branch that cannot be taken becomes dead code that the reader cannot tell apart from live branches. An op that passes on someone else’s result as it is is regarded as raising that error (try · return <call>).

1d So the only names that can stand in that condition are those that are the same on entry and on exit — namely inputs that do not change and module constants. Writing in the condition a name the body can change (an actor’s state field · a module var · an argument in a mut position) is refused (E-ERRORS-STATE).

In plain words

Why on entry? In the table of ⟦(6)⟧ errors stands on the same side as requires — it is a clause that says what the caller did wrong. What the caller did is only what it handed over, so the only values that can measure that mistake are what was handed over. Read with the values on exit, a successful execution would accuse itself — an op that subtracted 30 from a balance of 50 and succeeded would, because the condition «more than the balance» is read again on exit, become “it did not raise the error it should have”.

Caution —

(1d) refuses generously. A slice in a mut position does not change length, so a condition that reads only len is in fact the same on entry and on exit, but the processor does not distinguish which part of the value a condition reads. Rather than pretending to distinguish what cannot be distinguished, it is better to draw the line thickly and write that down — write it with guard inside the body.

2 The conditions used in a contract shall be pure expressions — something that produces effects cannot be written in a contract.

3 shared_read and write_only are disciplines of reading and writing, so the processor enforces them now. That is, writing to a place declared shared_read is refused, and reading a place declared write_only is refused (E-ACCESS-MODE). The other five are hints about usage, and the processor says that it does not use them yet (W-NOT-YET). It does not silently ignore them.

4 Not being enforced does not mean they may be written carelessly. A mode is a claim about what this op does, and the processor may someday question that claim.

6.4.4 How to write contracts§

1 Conditions are written in prefix notation. Comparison and logical operations, arithmetic, len and idx can be used.

2 If there are several requires, all shall be true.

3 A condition about every element of a slice is written with the following four. elem_lt <slice> <value> means “every element is less than that value”, and elem_le · elem_gt · elem_ge likewise.

4 These four exist because a contract cannot write going through one by one. A condition is a pure expression and a loop is a statement, so to speak about all the elements there has to be a word that says it.

Example — a contract

Source

module ex_contract .

rem This op reads two bytes from a slice and makes one larger number.
export fn read_pair input data slice u8 . output u32 .
  requires ge len data. . 2 . .
  ensures le ret. 65535 . .
do
  let hi u32 widen u32 idx data. 0 . . .
  let lo u32 widen u32 idx data. 1 . . .
  return add mul hi. 256 . lo. . .
end .

In plain words

How to read the example above: “this function shall receive a row of bytes of length 2 or more (requires), promises that the value it returns is at most 65535 (ensures), and touches nothing in the outside world (effects none).” — this much can be known without opening the body.

6.4.5 Giving a contract a name§

1 If several ops require the same condition, that condition can be given a name. It is written with contract.

2 An op writes that name with satisfies and so takes on that contract.

Example — a named contract

Source

module ex_named .

rem If several ops require the same condition, that condition can be given a name.
contract nonneg do
  requires ge a. 1 . .
end .

fn half satisfies nonneg . input a u8 . output u8 .
do
  return div a. 2 . .
end .

In plain words

If the same condition is repeated by hand in several places, one gets fixed and another forgotten. Giving it a name makes one place to fix.

6.4.6 Contracts remove checks§

1 The processor takes contracts as facts and narrows the range a value can have. If that range shows safety, it removes that check.

2 The checks that can be removed are these — overflow, division by 0, narrowing, slice bounds.

3 If a contract is not enforced, it is not used as a fact. That is, nothing is believed without confirmation. Believing without confirmation and removing checks is not being faster but being wrong.

4 The processor can report where checks it could not remove remain. A performance model that does not report is itself knowledge outside the source (§1.3).

Example — contracts remove checks

Source

module ex_elim .

rem Without a contract, the overflow check remains.
export fn bare input a u8 . output u8 .
do
  return add a. 1 . .
end .

rem With a contract, the processor proves it and removes the check.
export fn proven input a u8 . output u8 .
  requires le a. 200 . .
do
  return add a. 1 . .
end .

Note (informative)

The two ops have the same body. The only difference is one line of contract, and that one line removes one check during execution. This is the largest thing a contract does in this language.

Diagram — a contract becomes a fact at entry and removes the overflow check

 proven 250  ──▶ requires le a 200   entry check fails → E-VM-CONTRACT (caller's fault)
 proven 150  ──▶ requires le a 200   passes → from here on a ∈ [0, 200] is a fact
                 add a 1             a + 1 ∈ [1, 201] ⊂ u8 [0, 255] → overflow check removed
 bare 255    ──▶ add a 1             no fact about a → the check stays → E-VM-OVERFLOW

6.4.7 The build mode decides the remaining checks§

1 The build mode is written in the source as build <mode> .. A proved contract leaves no check in any mode. What the mode decides is the disposition of contracts that could not be proved.

1a The modes are a closed four — debug · test · release_safe · release_fast. Any other word is refused (E-BUILD-MODE).

2 debug keeps the remaining checks and, if one breaks, stops saying what broke.

2a test keeps checks like debug. What runs tests (§6.4.13) is not this mode but a request to run tests — that request runs tests in any mode, and even in test mode an ordinary execution does not run tests. If an expect inside a test is false, that test fails (E-TEST-FAIL) — it is not the processor’s error but the claim of the person who wrote it being wrong.

3 release_safe keeps the remaining checks and stops if one breaks. What it reports when it stops is a short fixed message naming the kind of check that broke — the contract’s text (the source expression) is not put in the executable. What differs from debug is that, as in (4), it removes contracts of debug grade.

4 release_fast removes the checks of requires that could not be proved — both those written on the op and those of named contracts taken in with satisfies. release_safe removes the requires checks of debug grade. No mode removes the exit checks of ensures.

4a A contract whose checks the mode removed is not a fact. The processor does not remove other checks on the ground of that contract — the op runs as if that contract had not been written. So if a call that broke the contract overflows or goes out of range inside that op, the check of that computation stops it. This is for the same reason that assume is not a fact (§6.4.9).

4b The checks the processor puts at each computation — overflow · division by 0 · MIN / -1 · indices · shift counts · narrowing · tags — are not removed in any mode. Those checks disappear only when they are proved.

5 That is, release_fast is the same as opening one more trust boundary (§4.4) — a person promises that the contracts are true. If the promise is wrong, the program runs the same as a program written without the contracts. It does not go into undefined behaviour.

Rejected example — the build modes are a closed four

Source

module ex_build_mode .

build nosuchmode .

fn f output u8 . do return 1 . end .

Diagnostic: E-BUILD-MODE

6.4.8 Names and scopes§

1 Names nest in four layers — the module, the op, the block, and blocks inside it.

2 In one scope a name points at exactly one entity. A name already alive cannot be hidden by declaring it again. Doing so is refused at translation.

3 The things that cannot be hidden are these four — names in the module, the names of core ops (§6.1.3), the parameter names of the same op, and local names still alive in an outer block.

4 Declaring the same name twice in the same block does not conform either. The second declaration does not replace the first — the same characters would merely point at two entities.

5 On leaving a block, the names declared in that block disappear. So two side-by-side blocks each declaring the same name conforms — the two names are not alive at the same time.

Rejected example — a module name cannot be hidden

Source

module ex_shadow .

let g u32 7 .

proc p output u32 . effects none .
do
  let g u32 1 .     rem hides the module's `g`
  return g. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-NAME-SHADOW

Rejected example — a parameter cannot be hidden

Source

module ex_shadow_param .

proc p input n u32 . output u32 . effects none .
do
  let n u32 1 .     rem hides the parameter `n`
  return n. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-NAME-SHADOW

Rejected example — the same name cannot be declared twice in the same block

Source

module ex_shadow_twice .

proc p output u32 . effects none .
do
  let a u32 1 .
  let a u32 2 .     rem does not replace the first
  return a. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-NAME-SHADOW

Rejected example — an inner block cannot hide an outer name

Source

module ex_shadow_inner .

proc p input n u32 . output u32 . effects none .
do
  let a u32 1 .
  guard gt n. 0 . else do
    let a u32 2 .   rem the outer `a` is still alive
    return a. .
  end .
  return a. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-NAME-SHADOW

6 The order of looking up names is local → module → imported modules. If imported names collide, which one is meant shall be written out.

6.4.9 Grades of contracts§

1 A grade can be attached to one contract clause to fix when and with what that condition is handled — it comes directly after the clause word, as in requires <grade> <condition> .. The grades are the following three and no more.

grade (등급)

A mark attached to one contract clause that fixes when, and who, is responsible for that condition. At translation the processor proves it (static), at run time it is checked (debug), or a person promises that it is true (assume).

Table 34 — Grades of contracts — a closed set of three

GradeWhen it is looked atMeaning
staticat translationthe processor shall prove it. If it cannot, translation fails
debugat run timelooked at only while the build mode keeps checks
assumenot looked atonly written down. The processor does not use it as a fact

2 If no grade is written, the processor decides — it proves what it can prove, and what it cannot it keeps as the build mode (§6.4.7) decides. That is, a grade is a mark that overturns the default disposition, not something needed to write a contract.

3 A condition that is assumed is not used as a fact. The processor neither keeps it nor relies on it to remove other checks — that is, that condition is something said to the reader.

4 This is normative because the opposite does not hold. If a condition that is not kept were taken as a fact, then when that condition is false the processor would believe it is right while having removed checks it should not have removed. That is, to use a contract as a fact it shall be enforced, and what is not enforced is not a fact.

Table 35 — The same condition, different dispositions

WrittenAt entryFollowing checks
requires le a 200checkedremoved — because it became a fact
requires assume le a 200not checkedremain — because it is not a fact

In plain words

Then what is assume for? It is the place to write down what the processor cannot prove yet but a person knows. Written down, the reader knows it, and when the processor grows later that place can be raised to static. Not written down, that knowledge stays only in a person’s head.

Example — the same program ends differently by mode — debug

Source

module ex_mode_debug .

build debug .

fn bump input a u8 . output u8 .
  requires le a. 200 . .
do
  return add a. 1 . .
end .

Output

bump(10) = 11 · bump(250) → E-VM-CONTRACT (trap)

Example — the same program — release_fast

Source

module ex_mode_fast .

build release_fast .

fn bump input a u8 . output u8 .
  requires le a. 200 . .
do
  return add a. 1 . .
end .

Output

bump(10) = 11 · bump(250) = 251 (does not stop)

Example — the same mode — where the contract kept the overflow away

Source

module ex_mode_fact .

build release_fast .

fn bump input a u8 . output u8 .
  requires le a. 254 . .
do
  return add a. 1 . .
end .

Output

bump(10) = 11 · bump(255) → E-VM-OVERFLOW (trap)

Caution — release_fast is outside the safe subset

The same program ends differently by mode. Breaking the contract requires le a. 200 . . by calling with a as 250 stops in debug (E-VM-CONTRACT), and in release_fast does not stop and gives 251. In the third example the contract was the ground for proving that add a. 1 . does not overflow. When the mode removes the contract, that ground goes too, so the overflow check comes back, and bump(255) stops instead of giving 256 (§6.4.7 (4a)). This is one of the reasons this document limits its claim of having removed undefined behaviour to the safe subset (§4.4). Removing checks for speed is something that may be chosen, but the fact of choosing shall remain in the source — that is why the mode is not a command-line flag but a build statement.

6.4.10 Violations that can be decided now§

1 If a call site breaks the callee’s requires and both sides are constants, that violation is decided at translation, without waiting for execution. It is then refused (E-CONTRACT-IMPOSSIBLE).

2 This does not make contracts stricter; it brings forward when the answer comes. Before, it was translated green and caught a trap during execution.

In plain words

If a contract whose answer comes at translation is postponed until execution, that program becomes a program that is known to be wrong only by running it. Programs carrying a bit budget that did not fit really were shipped. If the answer comes now, it is said now.

Rejected example — both sides are constants, so it is decided now

Source

module ex_impossible .

fn g input a u8 . output u8 . requires lt a. 10 . .
do
  return a. .
end .

fn f output u8 .
do
  return g 200 . .       rem 200 is never less than 10
end .

Diagnostic: E-CONTRACT-IMPOSSIBLE

6.4.11 What cannot be a name§

1 Apart from what cannot be hidden (§6.4.8), there are characters that cannot be a name in the first place.

Table 36 — What cannot be used as a name

WhatDiagnosticWhy
keywordsE-NAME-KEYWORDa keyword is a mark by which the reader tells construct from value at a glance
names of core opsE-NAME-BUILTINthe namespace is flat (no hiding), so that name would mean two things
a lone _E-NAME-WILDCARDa _ standing alone is a mark that means anything, not a name
names beginning with __E-NAME-VENDOR-RESERVEDa place reserved for those who make processors and libraries
characters outside ASCIIE-NAME-ASCIItext · strings · rem comments hold any character, but names are ASCII

1a Here «core op» is not only those named in §6.3. Anything called by its bare name belongs to that set — the table of core ops in annex D is all of it. The names of builtin ops that stand after call_builtin (files · the network · processes · standard input and output · clocks · random numbers · the environment · terminals · floating-point functions …) are not included — they are in their own namespace (§6.3.3 (1f)). That the names of core ops are taken is normative — using one as a local name is refused.

In plain words

It may look strange to rule «names in that list cannot be used» without writing the list. But the two questions are different — what exists is answered by the counting side (the ledger), and whether it occupies the namespace is answered by the norm. The former grows and shrinks, but the latter is always true, and only what is always true is written in this document.

2 Two imported names being bound to the same name is refused too (E-NAME-COLLISION). Importing does not overwrite a name that already exists.

3 In a dotted name <front>.<back>, the front shall be a declared type (E-NAME-QUALIFIER). That shape puts the op in the namespace of that type; it does not point at a module.

In plain words

Why ASCII? This language lets text and comments hold any character while keeping only names narrow. Names are not only read by people but moved, attached and compared by tools, and characters that cannot be told apart by eye becoming different names makes mistakes that cannot be found.

Caution —

What reserving __ protects is not the processor but the person writing. Once it is fixed that that place always means «something internal», when reading someone else’s code you know from the name alone that it is not yours.

6.4.12 What contracts refer to§

1 The names a contract clause calls shall be visible at that place. Calling a name that does not exist is refused (E-REQ-UNDEF · E-ENS-UNDEF).

2 A contract that calls something the processor cannot bring to that place is refused too (E-REQ-UNSUP · E-ENS-UNSUP). It does not pretend to judge what it cannot judge.

3 If a named contract (§6.4.5) points at something not declared, it is refused (E-CONTRACT-UNDEF).

4 Declaring an error that cannot happen is refused (E-CONTRACT-DEAD) — if requires already filters out that case, that error branch never occurs.

4a If two requires on the same inputs cannot be true together, it is refused (E-CONTRACT-UNSAT) — whatever arguments it is called with, it stops at entry, so the body of that op never runs. The same place as (4): code that does not run is written in the signature.

In plain words

What (4) prevents is not a wrong program but a wrong picture. If a failure that never occurs is written in the signature, the caller writes code to handle it, and that code never runs. Code that does not run is not tested, and untested code is wrong someday.

4b A contract clause the processor cannot enforce reports that fact (W-CONTRACT-IGNORED). The entry check (requires) and the exit check (ensures) each know certain shapes; other shapes are stopped by nobody during execution, and interval analysis does not learn from them either. Such a clause stands only in documentation.

In plain words

(4b) pairs with (2). (2) refuses what cannot be brought, and (4b) reports shapes that can be brought but cannot be set up. Both keep the same one thing — a promise that is not checked is not left looking as if it were checked. On the ensures side this report did not exist until 2026-09-16, so such clauses were silently discarded (defect note 11).

5 If the type passed to a translation-time parameter does not provide what that place requires, it is refused (E-BOUND-UNSAT, §6.11.2).

6.4.13 Test blocks§

test block (시험 블록)

A body without inputs or outputs, written test <name> do … end .. It is executed only when a request to run tests is received.

assertion (단언)

expect <condition> . — a condition a test claims to be true. If it is false, that test fails.

1 Test blocks come at the top level of a module. The head has the name and, if present, a schedule clause (§6.4.1 (3d)); the body has the forms that come in the body of an op. The body is checked by the same type rules as the body of an op — being inside a test does not let let x u8 300 . pass.

1a The name of a test is in the same namespace as the names of ops · types · modules. If tests in one module, or a test and another declaration, use the same name, it is refused (E-NAME-DUP, §6.4.8 (2)). Tests are reported by name and called by name, so if names overlap it cannot be said which is meant.

2 The condition of expect <condition> . shall be bool. Giving a number is refused (E-TYPE-COND) — this language has no rule that reads non-zero numbers as true (§6.2.16 (4)).

2a If, when tests are run, the condition of an expect is false, that test fails (E-TEST-FAIL). This is not a contract violation — a contract violation is code breaking its own promise; a test failure is the claim of the person who wrote it differing from the code. The place to fix is different.

2b The processor does not remove expect in any build mode (§6.4.7). A vanished assertion is a test that did not run.

2c expect comes only inside test blocks. Writing it in the body of an op or anywhere else is refused (E-EXPECT-PLACE). Promises about inputs or results are written with requires · ensures (§6.4.3), and stopping deliberately with panic (§7.1).

3 Tests are executed only when a request to run tests (lowentc --test) is received. Ordinary execution and checking (--check) do not run tests, and checking reports that fact (W-TEST-NOT-RUN) — so that passing the check is not read as passing the tests.

3a The tests of a unit that translation refuses do not run. A request to run tests also goes through the same checks as --check first. If a refused program reports «pass», that green is false.

3b The result of running tests is the number of tests run · the number passed · the number failed. If there are no tests at all, it says so. If even one test failed, that request ends in failure.

4 A test with the clause schedule explore_interleavings [limit <number>] . is run repeatedly while changing the order in which messages are delivered. Without limit it runs up to a bound the processor decides. If it runs through every order before the bound, the result is reported as «exhaustive»; if it stops at the bound, as «up to the bound». Stopping at the bound is not reported as exhaustive.

4a If it passes in every order, the test passes. If it passes in some orders and fails in others, the test fails (E-SCHED-NONDET) — the result depends on the order of delivery, and the answer from running it once is only the answer for that one order. If it fails in every order, it is an ordinary test failure (E-TEST-FAIL).

Example — test blocks and assertions

Source

module ex_test .

fn clamp8 input v u64 . output u8 .
do
  return narrow_sat u8 v. . .
end .

test clamp_keeps_small do
  expect eq clamp8 7 . 7 . .
end .

test clamp_saturates do
  expect eq clamp8 1000 . 255 . .
end .

Rejected example — the condition of an assertion is bool — a number is not true

Source

module ex_test_num .

test counts do
  expect 1 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-COND

Rejected example — two tests in one module shall have different names

Source

module ex_test_dup .

test same do
  expect eq 1 1 . .
end .

test same do
  expect eq 2 2 . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-NAME-DUP

Rejected example — tests and ops share one namespace

Source

module ex_test_ns .

fn check output bool . do return true . end .

test check do
  expect check. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-NAME-DUP

Rejected example — expect comes only inside test blocks

Source

module ex_expect_place .

fn half input n u8 . output u8 .
do
  expect lt n. 200 . .
  return div n. 2 . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-EXPECT-PLACE

6.5 Statements and control flow§

1 A statement is something that is executed. An expression makes a value; a statement does work.

1a So using as a statement a form that only gives a value and changes nothing — a pure computation (add s 100 . · eq s 7 .) or an fn call — does not conform (E-VALUE-DISCARDED). That value goes nowhere. What was usually meant is set s. add s. 100 . .. A proc call is a statement, since its effect is its work.

Rejected example — a form that only gives a value cannot be used as a statement

Source

module ex_discard .

fn bump input a u64 . output u64 .
  requires lt a. 100 . .
do
  var s u64 a. .
  add s. 100 .   rem what was meant is set s (add s 100) .
  return s. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-VALUE-DISCARDED

6.5.1 Statements that make names§

1 let makes a name that does not change. A value once fixed does not change.

2 var makes a name that can change. Its value is changed with set.

2a Parameters can be set too. A parameter received by value is a local copy within this op, so changing it does not change the caller’s value. Writing a field of a struct parameter (array fields included) is the same — it writes to the local copy. The only writes that reach the caller are mut · mut_ref positions (§8.4).

2b So what requires · ensures say through a parameter name is the value on entry. Even after the body sets that name, what the contract says is the value on entry, unchanged.

binding      ::= ( "let" | "var" ) name [ alloc-clause ] [ type ] term [ fail-clause ] "."
alloc-clause ::= ( "use" | "keep" ) name "."
fail-clause  ::= "else" leave "."
               | "else" block
               | "else" "error" name block
leave        ::= "return" [ term ] | "break" [ number ] | "continue" [ number ] | "panic" term

3 The statement that makes a name is let <name> <type> <value> . (var the same) — the type stands after the name and before the value. If the value does not fit that type, translation is refused. The type is not guessed: it is let x u64 300 ., not let x 300 . (E-LET-NOTYPE). Guessed types let bare literals slip past the width check. If the value begins with lit, the type is written after that lit, so it may be left out in front (let p lit pt do … end . .).

3a No word is inserted between the name and the type. The be of the old shape let <name> be <type> <value> . is not a word of this language — writing it is refused at translation (E-LET-BE).

3b A type ends by its characters alone: it is a constructor of the closed vocabulary (u64 · slice u8 · result u64 perr · array 4 u8), one name, or parentheses. User types that take arguments, and forms whose number of arguments is not fixed (such as result u64 without an error type), are wrapped in parentheses — let b (box u64) mk. . · let r (result u64) ok k. . ..

3c The allocator clause stands directly after the name, before the type — use <allocator>. gives back at the end of the block, and keep <allocator>. does not give back even on leaving the block (§6.2.6 (7e)(7f) · §8.14). An allocator is a variable, so it is closed with a dot: var v use al. option (vec u32 allocs.bump_bytes) vecgen.new al. 8 . .. The old shapes using <allocator> be · using <allocator> keep be are refused (E-USING-OLD). A binding whose type contains owned (let n use al. option (owned node) lit node … .) does not give back at the end of the block even when written with use — §8.5 (10)(11).

4 After the type there shall be a value. Closing without a value is refused at translation (E-LET-NOVALUE). This language has no way of making a name and giving it a value later.

4a The value is one form and is closed by its own dot (§6.1.5). After it comes the statement’s closing dot, or else — let n u64 find k. . . · let n u64 find k. . else return 0 . .. The failure clause stands before the statement’s closing dot: writing else after the statement has been closed is refused at translation (E-STMT-ELSE).

5 The failure clause — in let <name> <type> <value> else <leave> . ., if the type of the value is option t · result t e and the type written is the contents t, then when there is a value its contents are bound to the name, and when it is empty (none · an error) what follows else is executed. What follows else shall leave (return · break · continue · panic) — falling through is refused (E-GUARD-FALLTHROUGH). So after it the name always has contents. var is the same.

5c The failure clause has three shapes. else <leave> . is one leaving statement — that statement is closed by its own dot. else do … end is several statements — every path of the block shall leave. else error <name> do … end binds the error value of a result to <name> and looks at it inside the block — <name> lives only inside that block, and every path of the block shall leave. If the value is an option, there is no error to receive, so else error <name> is refused (E-BIND-ELSE).

5d After the failure clause comes the statement’s closing dot — after a leaving statement too (… else return 0 . .), and after a block too (… else do … end .). If that dot is missing, translation is refused (E-DOT-MISSING).

5a If the value is not an option · result, or the type of the contents differs from the type written, it is refused (E-BIND-ELSE). The type of the value is read from the output of the op called · the declaration of the name · the output of an actor handler — for any other expression, first bind it with its type written.

5b Binding an option · result value to its contents type without else is refused (E-TYPE-LET) — a place that forgot it can fail. If stopping is acceptable, write else panic "…" . (the effect panic follows).

Caution —

What this rule catches is not only the mistake of leaving a blank. If let x f64 .5 . is written, .5 is not a floating-point literal (§6.1.4 (2)), so that dot closes the form, and after the name only the type remains. A person believes they wrote a value while the processor saw none, so the diagnostic names that trap.

Note (informative)

It matters that the default is let. A name that does not change needs to be checked only once by the reader, but a name that can change makes them ask again at every place it is used “what value is it here”. That question is semantic entropy (§1.3).

Rejected example — no value after the type

Source

module ex_let_novalue .

fn f output u8 .
do
  let a u8 .        rem 0 is not put in silently
  return a. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-LET-NOVALUE

Rejected example — the type is not guessed

Source

module ex_let_notype .

fn f output u8 .
do
  let x 300 .
  return x. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-LET-NOTYPE

Rejected example — be is not a word of this language — tests (3a)

Source

module ex_let_be .

fn f output u64 .
do
  let x be u64 7 . .
  return x. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-LET-BE

Rejected example — the failure clause comes before the statement's closing dot — tests (4a)

Source

module ex_else_dot .

fn find input k u64 . output option u64 .
do
  if eq k. 0 . do return none . end .
  return some k. . .
end .

fn f input k u64 . output u64 .
do
  let n u64 find k. . . else return 0 .
  return n. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-STMT-ELSE

Rejected example — the allocator clause is use · keep — tests (3c)

Source

module ex_using_old .

actor exact do
  state do n u64 . end .
  proc reserve input k u64 . output option mut slice u8 . effects state . do
    return none .
  end .
end .

proc f output u64 . effects state .
do
  var e exact spawn actor exact . .
  let b using e. mut slice u8 lit array 4 u8 _ . else return 0 . .
  return len b. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-USING-OLD

Example — the three failure clauses — tests (5)(5c)

Source

module ex_fail_clause .

enum perr do bad . empty . end .

fn parse input s u64 . output result u64 perr .
  errors empty eq s. 0 . .
  errors bad gt s. 100 . .
do
  if eq s. 0 . do return error empty . . end .
  if gt s. 100 . do return error bad . . end .
  return ok s. . .
end .

export fn one input s u64 . output u64 .
do
  let n u64 parse s. . else return 0 . .
  return add n. 1 . .
end .

export fn many input s u64 . output u64 .
do
  let n u64 parse s. . else do
    return 99 .
  end .
  return add n. 1 . .
end .

export fn why input s u64 . output u64 .
do
  let n u64 parse s. . else error e do
    if eq e. bad. . do return 1000 . end .
    return 2000 .
  end .
  return add n. 1 . .
end .

Output

one(5) = 6 · one(0) = 0 · many(200) = 99 · why(0) = 2000 · why(500) = 1000

6.5.2 Conditions — if§

Syntax

if-statement ::= "if" term block { "else" "if" term block } [ "else" block ] "."
block        ::= "do" { statement } "end"

Constraints

1 The type of the condition (term) shall be bool (§6.2.3).

2 An if-statement is a statement. It cannot come in a place where a value goes.

Dynamic semantics

3 The condition is computed in turn.

4 If its value is true, the statements of the first block are executed in the order written.

5 If its value is false and there is an else if, (3) is done again with that condition; if there is an else, the block after it is executed.

6 If its value is false and there is no else, nothing is executed.

Diagnostics

Table 37 — Diagnostics of the if statement

ViolationDiagnostic
(1) — the condition is not boolE-TYPE-COND
(2) — if written in a value positionE-IF-VALUE
syntax — one more dot written after the condition closed (if c. . do)E-CLOSER-EXTRA
syntax — no closing dot at the end of the chainE-DOT-MISSING
syntax — something other than do … end written in the block positionE-CTRL-NODO
syntax — else written inside the blockE-STMT-ELSE

Examples

Example — three branches — tests (4)(5)

Source

module ex_if .

export fn sign input a i64 . output u64 .
do
  if gt a. 0 . do
    return 1 .
  end else if eq a. 0 . do
    return 0 .
  end else do
    return 2 .
  end .
end .

Output

sign(5) = 1 · sign(0) = 0 · sign(-3) = 2

Rejected example — if gives no value — tests (2)

Source

module ex_if_value .

fn pick input a u64 . output u64 .
do
  let x u64 (if gt a 1 do 5 . end else do 6 . end) .   rem set or return in each branch
  return x. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-IF-VALUE

Notes

In plain words

Why must the body be a block? The condition ends at its own dot (if gt a. 3 . do) — the condition never swallows the next word. Still, if the body could be written as one statement, the same if would have two shapes, and the moment a second statement is added, whether it is inside or outside the if would depend on indentation. Since the body is always do … end, end says where the body ends, and the dot after it where the statement ends.

Note (informative)

That the head’s expression ends at do and the body is written as a block holds for while (§6.5.3) · the four loop heads (§6.5.3.1) · match (§6.6) too. If the head’s expression is a value that holds a block (if eq p. lit pt do x 1 . end . . do), the head’s body is the last block — for the reader’s sake, wrap such a value in parentheses.

Rejected example — one more dot after the head's expression

Source

module ex_ctrl_dot .

fn f input a u64 . output u64 .
do
  if gt a. 3 . . do
    return 1 .
  end .
  return 0 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-CLOSER-EXTRA

Note (informative)

To get a different value per branch, set a name or return in each branch.

6.5.3 Loops — while§

1 while <condition> do <statements> end . repeats the block while the condition is true. The condition is computed on each iteration, before entering the block — if it is false from the start, the block does not run even once.

1a The condition is one form and is closed by its own dot (while lt i. n. . do … end .). Writing one more dot after it is refused at translation (E-CLOSER-EXTRA). The statement ends at the dot after end (§6.1.6 (2b)).

2 break leaves the loop, and continue moves to the next iteration. Without a number, both apply to the innermost loop. break carries no value.

2b With a number after the word, they apply to the loop counted that many outward. break 2 . leaves both the innermost loop and the loop that encloses it. continue 2 . leaves the innermost loop and goes to the next iteration of the loop that encloses it. 1 is the same as writing no number. The number is a plain decimal literal, 1 or more, and cannot exceed the number of loops enclosing that statement (E-LOOP-COUNT). Loops are not given names (labels).

2c What is counted are the five loop heads (while · for · repeat · range · cycle). The blocks of if and match are not counted. What a loop passed on the way out opened is closed as when that loop is left normally.

Note (informative)

Wrapping or unwrapping a loop makes the same number point at a different loop. Then count again.

2a These two are used only inside loops. Writing them outside a loop does not conform (E-LOOP-OUTSIDE) — there is no loop to leave.

3 The condition of a loop shall be bool too. Writing a number in a condition position is refused at translation (E-TYPE-COND).

3a while is a statement and gives no value. It has no else branch (E-STMT-ELSE).

Example — leaving nested loops at once

Source

module ex_break_count .

export fn first_zero input want u32 . output usize .
do
  let g lit slice array 3 u32 lit array 3 u32 4 7 1 . lit array 3 u32 9 0 5 . . .
  var hit usize 99 .
  repeat r usize len g. . do
    repeat c usize 3 do
      if eq idx idx g. r. . c. . want. . do
        set hit. add mul r. 3 . c. . .
        break 2 .
      end .
    end .
  end .
  return hit. .
end .

Rejected example — two loops enclose it, but it tries to leave three

Source

module ex_break_far .

export fn f output u64 . do
  var n u64 0 .
  repeat a u64 3 do
    repeat b u64 3 do
      set n. add n. 1 . .
      break 3 .
    end .
  end .
  return n. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-LOOP-COUNT

Example — conditions and loops

Source

module ex_ctl .

export fn count_big input n u32 . output u32 .
  requires le n. 100 . .
do
  var total u32 0 .
  var i u32 0 .
  while lt i. n. . do
    if gt i. 5 . do
      set total. add total. 1 . .
    end .
    set i. add i. 1 . .
  end .
  return total. .
end .

6.5.3.1 Loops that go in order — for · repeat · range · cycle§

for-stmt    ::= "for"    name [ "mut" ] term [ filter ] block "."
repeat-stmt ::= "repeat" name type term [ filter ] block "."
range-stmt  ::= "range"  name type term term [ "step" term ] [ filter ] block "."
cycle-stmt  ::= "cycle"  name type term "while" term "next" term [ filter ] block "."
filter      ::= "if" term

1 The four statements repeat the block putting the values the head decides into the name in turn. Each head word does one thing — for goes over the elements of a source, repeat n times from 0, range between two ends, and cycle over the next values the author writes. There are no other shapes.

Table 38 — Loop heads

HeadShapeWhat the name takes in turn
elementsfor x <slice> dothe elements of the slice — from the front
element placesfor x mut <slice> dothe slots of the slice themselves — can be read and written
countingrepeat i <type> n do0, 1, …, n−1
rangerange i <type> a b do · range i <type> a b step k dofrom a to b, both ends included
recurrencecycle i <type> v while c next e dostarting at v, then e on each iteration

1a Each expression of the head is one form and is closed by its own dot — repeat i u64 len xs. . do · range i u64 0 sub n. 1 . do · cycle i u64 1 while lt i. n. . next mul i. 2 . do. The ending words (do · if · while · next · step) are reserved, so they cannot come as names inside an expression. Writing one more dot after a closed expression is refused at translation (E-CLOSER-EXTRA).

1b The old shapes for i count <type> n . do · for i range <type> a b . do · for i be <type> v . while c . next e . do and the filter . where c . are refused (E-FOR-OLD). for does only one job, going over elements.

2 Elements. The source shall be a slice. If it is not a slice, translation is refused (E-TYPE-ITER). The type of the name is the element type of the slice. String literals (slices of bytes) and list literals (lit slice u16 10 20 30 .) are sources too. If there are no elements, the block does not run even once.

2a The source ends at do (or at the filter’s if) — whether it is one name (for x xs. do) or an expression (for x view_array u16 b. . do), it is the same.

3 Element places. In for x mut buf. do … end ., x is that slot of buf: reading it gives the slot’s value, and set x. v. . writes that slot. buf shall be mut (E-TYPE-MUT). During the loop the whole of buf is borrowed, so reading or writing buf inside the block is refused at translation (E-FOR-HEAD) — the elements are touched only through x.

3a In for x <slice> do written without mut, x is a copy holding that iteration’s element value. x cannot be set inside the block (E-FOR-HEAD) — to change the slots, write mut in the head.

4 Counting. repeat i <type> n do puts 0 to n−1 into i in turn. If n is 0 or less, it does not run even once. <type> is the type of the count and is an integer type that is always written — if it is not written, or is not an integer type, translation is refused (E-FOR-STEP). n shall fit <type> (E-TYPE-WIDTH).

5 Range. range i <type> a b do goes from a to b with both ends included. The two ends decide the direction — range i u64 3 5 is 3, 4, 5, and range i u64 5 3 is 5, 4, 3. If the two ends are the same, it runs once. Both ends shall fit <type> (E-TYPE-WIDTH).

5a With step k, the next value is current + k and the sign of k decides the direction. If the start has already passed the end in that direction, it does not run even once (range i i64 1 10 step -1). If the next value passes the end, it ends there — range i u8 250 255 step 3 is 250, 253. step 0 is refused at translation (E-FOR-STEP), and if a k not known at translation is 0 during execution, it stops.

5b The count does not overflow. range i u8 0 255 runs 256 times and ends at 255 — it never goes beyond the type while computing what comes after the last value.

6 Recurrence. cycle i <type> v while c next e do … end . starts i at v. Before entering an iteration it looks at c; if true, it runs the block, and when the block ends it puts the value of e into i. If c is false from the start, it does not run even once. c shall be bool (E-TYPE-COND), and v and e shall fit <type> (E-TYPE-WIDTH). The computation of e is ordinary arithmetic — it stops if it overflows (§6.3.4). Both clauses while · next shall be present, in this order (E-FOR-HEAD). A continue inside the block goes through next — it puts the value of e into i and looks at c again.

7 Filtering. if c can be added at the end of the head, before do. It means the same as writing if not c. . do continue . end . at the start of the block — the condition is computed on each iteration. It attaches to any of the four head words. The if inside the head is not a statement but a clause — no statement can come inside the head, so they do not overlap.

8 What the head read is frozen during the loop. The end · step · source are computed once on entering the loop. The count name (the name of repeat · range · cycle) and the names the head read cannot be set inside the block (E-FOR-HEAD). Changing the contents of slice slots is allowed.

9 The name of a loop lives only inside the block. When the block ends, that name is gone. A name that already exists in the same block cannot be used again (E-NAME-SHADOW).

10 break and continue are used inside the four loops just as in while (§6.5.3 (2)). None of the four gives a value.

Example — a head that counts numbers

Source

module ex_for_count .

export fn evens output u64 .
do
  var acc u64 0 .
  range i u64 10 1 step -2 do
    set acc. add acc. i. . .
  end .
  return acc. .
end .

Output

the sum of the even numbers from 1 to 10

Example — going through a slice

Source

module ex_for .

export fn total_of input xs slice u8 . output u64 .
do
  var acc u64 0 .
  for x xs. do
    set acc. add acc. widen u64 x. . . .
  end .
  return acc. .
end .

Output

adds up all the elements

Example — element places · recurrence · filtering

Source

module ex_for_more .

fn bump input b mut slice u64 . output u64 .
do
  for x mut b. do
    set x. mul x. 2 . .
  end .
  var acc u64 0 .
  for y b. do
    set acc. add acc. y. . .
  end .
  return acc. .
end .

export fn powers output u64 .
do
  var n u64 0 .
  cycle i u64 1 while lt i. 100 . next mul i. 2 . do
    set n. add n. 1 . .
  end .
  return n. .
end .

export fn evens output u64 .
do
  var acc u64 0 .
  repeat i u64 10 if eq mod i. 2 . 0 . do
    set acc. add acc. i. . .
  end .
  return acc. .
end .

Output

powers() = 7 · evens() = 20

Rejected example — the count name cannot be changed inside the block

Source

module ex_for_head .

fn f output u64 .
do
  repeat i u64 3 do
    set i. 0 .
  end .
  return 0 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-FOR-HEAD

Rejected example — for goes only over elements — tests (1b)

Source

module ex_for_old .

fn f output u64 .
do
  var acc u64 0 .
  for i count u64 3 do
    set acc (add acc i) .
  end
  return acc. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-FOR-OLD

Caution —

in is not a word of this language. Writing for x in xs is refused (E-VOCAB-REMOVED). What is gone through comes directly after the name.

6.5.4 A condition that leaves — guard§

1 guard leaves on the spot if the condition is not true. What comes after else shall leave on every path.

0 guard <condition> else <leave> . . — the condition is one form and is closed by its own dot. The leaving statement after else is closed by its own dot too, and after it comes the dot that closes guard (guard lt i. 9 . else return 0 . .). The same holds when what follows else is a block (guard c. else do … end .).

1a After else there may come one statement or a block. If it is a block, every path of that block shall leave — the leaving statements are return, break, continue and panic.

2 If some path of else does not leave but continues downward, translation is refused.

3 Once past a guard, its condition is guaranteed to be true. The processor uses that fact in the code after it.

Rejected example — else does not leave

Source

module ex_guard_bad .

proc p input n u32 . output u32 . effects none .
do
  guard le n. 5 . else set n. 0 . .   rem continues downward without leaving
  return n. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-GUARD-FALLTHROUGH

Caution — guard is not another name for if not

What guard says is “if not this condition, it ends here”. So the code below guard lives only in a world where the condition is true. If else does not leave, that promise breaks, and the code below wrongly believes it is true — so the language refuses it.

Example — guard

Source

module ex_guard .

export fn safe_head input data slice u8 . output u8 .
do
  guard ge len data. . 1 . else return 0 . .
  return idx data. 0 . .
end .

Example — the type is written before the value, and guard else may be a block

Source

module ex_infer_guard .

rem The type is written after the name and before the value — it is not guessed.
fn inferred output u64 .
do
  let a u64 7 .
  return a. .
end .

rem `else` may be a block. The rule is "does every path leave".
fn guarded input n u8 . output u8 .
do
  guard gt n. 5 . else do
    let x u8 1 .
    return x. .
  end .
  return 9 .
end .

Output

inferred() = 7 · guarded(3) = 1 · guarded(9) = 9

Rejected example — a path of else that does not leave is refused

Source

module ex_guard_fall .

fn f input n u8 . output u8 .
do
  guard gt n. 5 . else do
    let x u8 1 .
  end .
  return 9 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-GUARD-FALLTHROUGH

6.5.5 Going back — return§

1 return ends the op and returns a value. The type of the value returned shall be the same as what is written in output.

1a This rule is the same wherever return is — the one statement after the else of a guard, and all statements inside an else block, receive the same check. So an op with output result … returns only with ok <value> · error <variant> · an expression that gives a result, and returning a bare value is refused (E-TYPE-RETURN). Conversely, an op with a bare type written returning ok <value> is refused too.

Rejected example — a bare value returned in a result place

Source

module ex_bare_under_result .

enum short do
  too_short .
end .

fn head input b slice u8 . output result u8 short .
errors too_short .
do
  guard ge len b. . 2 . else return 0 . .   rem neither `ok 0` nor `error too_short`
  return ok idx b. 0 . . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-RETURN

In plain words

This place was outside the check for a while (until 2026-09-25) — the else of a guard was attached as one lump, not as a list of statements, so the checker did not go down into it. So the op above passed translation and stopped during execution the moment the caller asked is_ok. The rarer the path (input too short), the later it shows.

1b An op whose output is named (§6.4.1 (3e)) returns that name — return <name> .. That name is a local that starts at 0, so there is a value to return even if the body never writes it. Another value may be returned too (the name is only a local). That every path shall end with return is the same as for unnamed outputs (paragraph 3).

2 An op that returns no value (output void) can end early by writing just return.

2a An op that returns no value may run to the end of its body without return. There is no value to return, so where it ends is where it goes back.

3 An op that returns a value shall end returning a value on every path. If there is a path that ends without a value, it does not conform.

4 The following statements are regarded as ending by returning a value — return, an if ⋯ else both of whose branches return a value, and a match all of whose branches return a value. Whether a match covers every case is checked separately (§6.6).

5 guard · while · for have paths that leave, so by themselves they are not regarded as returning a value.

6 An op whose body is machine instructions (§6.9) gives its value through out reg, so it is not the subject of this clause.

In plain words

Without this rule, on a path that ends without a value the processor would put 0 in instead. That 0 is nowhere in the source, so the result cannot be known even after reading the whole program (§1.3). So this language does not translate such paths at all.

Example — an op that gives no value may end without return

Source

module ex_void .

proc hold input n u8 . output void . effects none .
do
  let x u8 n. .
end .

In plain words

There is no value to return, so where it ends is where it goes back. Had it been an op that returns a value, the same shape would be refused (§6.5.5 paragraph 3).

6.5.6 Blocks and indentation§

1 A block is opened with do and closed with end. Indentation has no meaning — it is for the reader.

2 A name made inside a block is visible only inside that block. Reading that name after the block has closed does not conform (E-NAME-SCOPE) — on a path that did not enter, no value was ever put in that name, and the processor does not put 0 in instead (§6.5.5 (3)).

Rejected example — reading a name made inside a block from outside the block

Source

module ex_name_scope .

fn pick input a u64 . output u64 .
do
  if gt a. 1 . do
    let big u64 mul a. 2 . .
  end .
  return big. .        rem on the path that did not enter there is no `big`
end .

Diagnostic: E-NAME-SCOPE

In plain words

Some languages divide blocks by indentation. This language does not, because if invisible characters (spaces and tabs) change the meaning of a program, what is seen and what is meant can diverge. do and end are visible.

6.5.7 Three ways of saying failure§

1 There are only three ways of saying failure, and their uses differ.

Table 39 — The three failure channels

WhatWhat it saysWhat the caller does
result t ea failure that can be fixedasks which side and handles it. If it does not handle it, it passes it upward
option tno valueasks whether there is one and takes it out, or gives a value to use instead
the panic effecta contract was brokencannot handle it. The program stops

2 The variants that go into the error type of a result shall be the same as those the op’s errors clause writes. Returning an error not written, or writing one and not returning it, does not conform.

3 panic does not unwind. The program ends at the place where it stops — there is no way of catching it midway and carrying on.

Example — the three channels answer in different shapes

Source

module ex_channels .

enum io_error do
  too_big .
end .

rem ① a failure that can be fixed — result.
fn halve input a u8 . output result u8 io_error .
  errors too_big gt a. 200 . .
do
  guard le a. 200 . else return error too_big . . .
  return ok div a. 2 . . .
end .

rem ② no value — option.
fn lookup input k u8 . output option u8 .
do
  guard lt k. 3 . else return none . .
  return some mul k. 10 . . .
end .

rem ③ a broken contract — if the caller breaks the promise, it stops.
fn strict input a u8 . output u8 .
  requires le a. 200 . .
do
  return add a. 1 . .
end .

Output

halve(100) = ok 50 · halve(250) = err too_big · lookup(2) = some 20 · lookup(7) = none · strict(10) = 11 · strict(250) → trap

In plain words

The question that separates the three is “what can the caller do”. A missing file — another file can be tried, so it is result. What is searched for not being in a list is simply absent, so it is option. A broken contract means the caller has already broken the promise, and that is not something that can be fixed — so it stops.

6.5.8 Handling failure — try and the consuming forms§

1 When calling an op that returns a result, try takes out the value on success and on failure passes that error on upward as it is.

2 An op that uses try shall itself be able to return that error. Otherwise it is refused (E-TRY-NORESULT) — because the failure has nowhere to go. If a tail is attached to change the channel ((4)), or it is handled here by the means of (3), that op need not return a result.

3 The ways of consuming result and option are closed to the following — try (including with a tail, (4)) · asking (is_ok is_error is_some is_none) · taking out (ok_value error_value some_value) · giving a value to use instead (value_or).

4 A tail can be attached after try to change the channel. The value stays the same; only the container changes.

Table 40 — Changing channels

ShapeFrom what to whatWhat is lost or gained
try <expr> else_noneresult → optionthe error is discarded — it no longer says why it failed
try <expr> else_error <variant>option → resulta name is given to the absence — it says why there is none

5 Changing channels changes the type. The type of the place where try <expr> else_none is written is option, not the value inside it. Writing a value type and then using a channel change does not conform.

Note (informative)

This is normative because once it was not so. The processor did not see the tail word, so an op written output u64 returned some 7 ., and still --check was green. If the norm does not speak about the place where the channel changes, nobody notices when a tool forgets that place.

6 Taking out is a partial operation (§6.2.8). Taking out the absent side traps.

7 The default of value_or is evaluated only when there is no value (lazy evaluation). So in the default position an expensive computation, or one that can fail, may be written — if there is a value, it does not run.

Note (informative)

value_or some 7 . div 1 0 . . is 7. There is a value, so the default side does not run. That place is evaluated only when there is no value, and then the trap really happens. ☞ Until 2026-08-29 it was not so — the tool evaluated the default first, so a program that had a value ended in a failure it need not have had. The canon (§6.2.8 · DECISION-0003 C1) had fixed it as lazy evaluation, and the tool was fixed to match the canon. The values do not change; what changes is which traps and effects can happen.

In plain words

Writing the code that checks for failure by hand every time makes it long, and long code gets things left out. try reduces that repetition to one word while leaving in the source the fact that the failure goes upward — no failure is silently ignored.

6.5.9 Stopping — panic§

1 panic stops the program at once (§5.4). It is an effect, so an op that uses it shall write that effect in its contract.

2 A pure op (fn) cannot use panic. But the trap the processor raises when a contract is broken is separate from this, and it is not something the op did.

6.5.10 Loops that run divided — parallel§

1 Attaching the clause parallel <piece> split . to a loop declares that the loop may be run by several together, dividing the piece.

2 The processor does not believe that word but checks it. What it checks is that the steps of the loop do not rely on one another, and there are three conditions.

Table 41 — The conditions for a loop that can run divided

ConditionIf violatedWhat went wrong
reads only its own shareE-PAR-READif it reads someone else’s place, whether that value is still old or already new depends on who runs first
writes only its own shareE-PAR-WRITEif two steps write the same place, the value that remains depends on the order
does not write to a place that lives across stepsE-PAR-CARRYsuch a place binds the steps together. If they must gather, declare it with reduce <accumulating place> <operation> .

3 If parallel names a piece but there is no loop that runs over the length of that piece, it is refused (E-PAR-NOLOOP) — because it cannot be known what is to be divided.

4 The gathering operation shall give the same answer even if the order of grouping changes. Operations that do not are refused (E-PAR-ASSOC). Addition of floating-point numbers does not, so it is blocked separately (E-PAR-FLOAT).

4a The starting value of the accumulating place shall be the identity of that operation. Otherwise it is refused (E-PAR-IDENTITY). When run divided, each piece starts again from that starting value, so a value that is not the identity enters the computation as many times as there are pieces — that is, the divided answer differs from the answer run one by one (the promise of (2) above breaks). The identity is 0 for add · bit_or · bit_xor, 1 for mul, and 0 for max of an unsigned width.

In plain words

If running divided is left only as a declaration, then when that declaration is wrong the program usually runs fine and one day runs differently. So this language checks what is declared — if it cannot be checked, it does not simply not divide; it refuses translation. «Fast but sometimes wrong» is not something this language sells.

Caution —

Even if it does not run divided, the answer is the same. The conditions above are conditions for being divisible, not clauses that change the meaning. So the divided answer and the one-by-one answer are always the same — that is exactly why E-PAR-FLOAT exists.

6.5.11 What a body shall have§

1 An op that says it gives a value shall give a value on every path (E-RETURN-PARTIAL). If any one path reaches the end without a value, it does not conform.

2 The heads of declarations (fn · proc · on · test …) shall have a do … end body (E-STMT-NODO).

3 What comes at the outermost level shall begin with the head of a declaration (E-TOPLEVEL).

4 If something that cannot come inside a form comes, it is refused (E-FORM-UNEXPECTED). So is a name that is not an op coming in the head position (E-HEAD-NOT-AN-OP).

In plain words

Without (1), the declaration «it gives a value» becomes something true only on some paths. The caller trusts it and uses the value, and on that path there is no value. A declaration is a promise about every path, so breaking it on even one path means it is not a promise.

Rejected example — no value on some path

Source

module ex_partial .

fn f input a u8 . output u8 .
do
  if gt a. 5 . do return 1 . end .
end .                      rem on the path where `a` is 5 or less there is no value

Diagnostic: E-RETURN-PARTIAL

6.5.12 Only the errors written occur§

1 The errors an op raises shall be within what its errors clause writes (E-ERR-UNDECLARED).

2 An errors clause writing a name not in the op’s error type is refused too (E-ERR-UNDEF).

3 If another op’s failure is passed upward — with try <op>, or by returning that result as it is with return <op> … — that op’s failure becomes this op’s failure. So the errors the called op can raise shall be within this op’s errors clause (this op’s error type if there is no clause) (E-ERR-UNDECLARED).

3a The errors a called op can raise are those its errors clause writes. If there is no clause, they are the whole error type of that op — not writing a clause means «any error of this type can occur», and the caller reads it with exactly that meaning.

Caution —

The two rules are two sides of the same sentence — what is written and what is raised shall be the same. If one side overflows, the caller gets a failure it did not see; if the other side overflows, it handles a failure that will not come (for the same reason as §6.4.12 (4)).

Rejected example — raising an error not written

Source

module ex_err_undeclared .

enum e do
  bad .
end .

fn f output result u8 e .
do
  return error bad . .   rem `errors bad …` was not written
end .

Diagnostic: E-ERR-UNDECLARED

Rejected example — passing on someone else's failure with return

Source

module ex_err_handed_on .

enum parse_error do
  bad_digit .
end .

enum load_error do
  too_long .
end .

fn read_digit input c u8 . output result u8 parse_error .
errors bad_digit .
do
  guard le c. 9 . else return error bad_digit . . .
  return ok c. . .
end .

fn load_byte input c u8 . output result u8 load_error .
errors too_long .
do
  guard le c. 200 . else return error too_long . . .
  return read_digit c. . .     rem `bad_digit` is not in `load_byte`'s promise
end .

Diagnostic: E-ERR-UNDECLARED

Rejected example — an op without a clause passes on a failure it received

Source

module ex_err_clauseless .

enum parse_error do
  bad_digit .
end .

enum load_error do
  too_long .
end .

fn read_digit input c u8 . output result u8 parse_error .
errors bad_digit .
do
  guard le c. 9 . else return error bad_digit . . .
  return ok c. . .
end .

fn digit_or_fail input c u8 . output result u8 parse_error .
do
  return read_digit c. . .     rem no clause — anything of `parse_error` can occur
end .

fn load_byte input c u8 . output result u8 load_error .
errors too_long .
do
  guard le c. 200 . else return error too_long . . .
  let v u8 try digit_or_fail c. . . .     rem `parse_error` is not in `load_byte`'s promise
  return ok v. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-ERR-UNDECLARED

6.6 Telling variants apart — match§

1 match looks at which variant an enum value is and executes the matching block.

2 match shall cover every variant. If even one is missing, translation is refused.

3 match can divide not only enums but integers too. Then one branch is a single value or a range, and a range is written <first> to <last> and includes both ends.

4 When dividing integers too, every value shall be covered. Either the ranges cover the values the type can have without gaps, or there is a branch that takes the rest (_).

5 For one branch to take several values, join them with or.

6 A later branch writing again what an earlier branch already covered is refused (E-MATCH-REDUNDANT). That branch never runs, and code that does not run being there is a sign that the writer’s meaning and the program’s meaning have parted.

7 A bare name written in case is always a variant. If it is not a declared variant, it is refused (E-MATCH-UNDEF). To catch anything, use _.

In plain words

Before 2026-09-03, a bare name that was not a variant was read as a name that binds the value, so a typo in a variant name, far from being caught, became a branch that catches everything and even satisfied the missing-branch check of (2). Adding one more variant said nothing either — that typo branch swallowed the new variant too. A place where the processor fails to catch the easiest mistake to catch is not a rule but a hole.

7a If more names follow a name, it is destructuring — unpacking a variant that carries values (§6.2.19) or binding the fields of an aggregate. The first name in that position need not be a variant (it may be the name of an aggregate).

7b If the name in case error <name> is a declared variant of that error enum, it takes only that variant. If it is not a declared variant, the whole error value is bound to that name (that branch takes every error). This applies to this place, unchanged, the discipline (7) fixes for the whole of match — a bare name that is a variant is a variant.

In plain words

Before 2026-09-16, the name in this place was always read as a binding. So case error not_digit . also took the empty error, a match that wrote only one of two variants passed the missing-branch check, and writing one per variant had the second refused as «already handled» (defect note 52). Writing a variant name and having another variant come into that branch is exactly the mistake (7) set out to prevent.

8 When dividing a variant that carries values (§6.2.19), the number of binding names shall equal the number of things that variant carries (E-MATCH-ARITY).

8a Putting else do … end in the last place of a match makes it the branch that takes the rest — it does the same as case _ . (the grammar of annex A.7 already writes that place). The missing-branch check counts it as covering, and writing more branches after it is caught by (6).

8b When the pattern of a branch is nested (case ok (some x) · case ok none), its outer tag counts as covered when the inner pattern is itself exhaustive. The inner being exhaustive means that the inner has _ or a binding name, or the inner wrote both some and none, or the inner branches wrote every variant of that enum.

In plain words

Before (8b), nested branches were not counted at all, so a match that divided exhaustively was required to have a _. A useless _ says nothing even when variants grow later, so forcing it was making people switch off the missing-branch check of (2).

9 For branches joined with or to bind values, every branch shall have the same name in the same place (E-MATCH-ORBIND). Otherwise that name would point at different things depending on which branch it came from.

In plain words

If (2) · (4) prevent gaps, (6) prevents overlaps. The two are the front and back of the same discipline — dividing branches is dividing the space of values without gaps and without overlaps, and keeping only one side is not dividing but merely laying things out.

Example — dividing by ranges

Source

module ex_range_match .

fn band input a u8 . output u8 .
do
  match a. do
    case 0 to 9 do return 1 . end .
    case 10 to 255 do return 2 . end .
  end .
end .

Output

band(5) = 1 · band(200) = 2

Rejected example — if there are values not covered, it is refused

Source

module ex_range_gap .

fn band input a u8 . output u8 .
do
  match a. do
    case 0 to 9 do return 1 . end .
  end .
end .

Diagnostic: E-MATCH-INEXHAUSTIVE

In plain words

Leaving a gap is refused at translation, so the author always has to say “what is the rest”. In other languages this place silently does nothing or becomes an exception, and neither can be known by reading the source.

Example — match covers every variant

Source

module ex_match .

enum color do
  red .
  green .
end .

export fn code input c color . output u32 .
do
  match c. do
    case red do return 1 . end .
    case green do return 2 . end .
  end .
end .

Rejected example — a match with a variant missing

Source

module ex_match_bad .

enum color do
  red .
  green .
end .

export fn code input c color . output u32 .
do
  match c. do
    case red do return 1 . end .
    rem `green` was not handled
  end .
end .

Diagnostic: E-MATCH-INEXHAUSTIVE

In plain words

The same can be done by chaining conditionals. The one advantage of match is completeness — when one more variant is made, the processor finds every place that does not handle it. A chain of conditionals does not find those places.

Note (informative)

Adding a new variant is common. If a person has to remember “where all must this variant be handled”, they will certainly miss something. match hands that memory over to the processor.

6.7 Tests — test and expect§

1 A test block is the place to write the tests of that module. The processor can run it.

2 expect is the assertion of a test. If the condition is false, that test fails.

3 The processor does not remove expect by optimisation. A vanished test is a test that did not run.

4 Only translating does not run tests. Translation having passed does not mean “the tests passed”.

Example — a test

Source

module ex_test .

export fn twice input n u32 . output u32 .
  requires le n. 100 . .
do
  return mul n. 2 . .
end .

test twice_works
do
  expect eq twice 5 . 10 . .
end .

Caution — A contract violation and a test failure are different

A contract violation is the code breaking its own promise, and a test failure is a test saying that the code is wrong. The two come out as different diagnostics — because what needs fixing differs.

6.8 Translation-time parameters — comptime§

1 A parameter marked comptime shall have its value fixed at translation time. Types can be received that way too.

2 This is the way to use the same op for several types. The processor makes a concrete instance for each combination used.

3 That making is visible in the source. There are no angle brackets and no inference — which combinations were made is written at the call sites.

4 Giving a comptime position a value not known at translation time does not conform.

4-1 Values known at translation time are integer literals, module constants, comptime <expr> (the computation of §6.8.1), and local lets in the same op whose value is computed as in §6.8.1 (1a). vars and run-time values are not. A parameter used as a lane count (vec <t> <n>) is given only integer literals and module constants — because a concrete instance is made for each such place.

4a Leaving empty a comptime position that takes a type does not conform either (E-MONO-NOTYPE). It is not traced back from the types of the arguments — as (3) says, what is made shall be written at the call site.

5 A concrete instance is made for each combination used, so the number of combinations is the amount of code made. This cost can be counted at the call sites.

Example — receiving a translation-time value

Source

module ex_comptime .

fn twice input comptime n u8 . input a u8 . output u8 .
do
  return add a. n. . .
end .

fn call_it output u8 .
do
  return twice 3 4 . .
end .

Output

call_it() = 7

Rejected example — a value not known at translation time cannot be given

Source

module ex_comptime_rt .

fn twice input comptime n u8 . input a u8 . output u8 .
do
  return add a. n. . .
end .

fn call_it input k u8 . output u8 .
do
  return twice k. 4 . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-COMPTIME-ARG

Note (informative)

It overlaps with what generics do in other languages, but here nothing is implicit. What is made shall be knowable from the source alone, and that is why this language does not use inference.

6.8.1 It shall be computable at translation time§

1 An expression in a comptime position shall produce its value at translation. If the current processor cannot compute it, it is refused (E-COMPTIME-NONCONST).

1a What the current processor computes is integer literals, module constants, local lets whose value came out at translation time, machine.*, native_lanes <type>, config <name>, and pure integer builtins (add · sub · mul · div · mod · neg · comparisons · min · max · bit operations · shl · shr · logic). A local let’s value coming out at translation time means that its value is a literal · one such name · native_lanes <type> · comptime <expr>. vars and inputs (comptime inputs too — the concrete instance of §6.8 is one, and the value is handed over at execution) are not computed. If a computation goes beyond the range of values (overflow of signed 64-bit · unsigned 64-bit, division by 0, mixing unsigned values and negative numbers), that expression is not computed — an expression that would stop at execution is not turned into a different number at translation.

1c Calling a pure fn is computed too. The processor actually runs that fn at translation and puts the answer in that place. A proc, which can produce effects, is not run. The inputs are one integer or bool, slices of numbers · bools, structs, and slices of structs. Integer arguments shall be computable as in (1a), and slice arguments shall have their bytes fixed at translation time — quoted strings · text literals (as written) · files read at translation (1g) · lit array · lit slice whose elements are all constants · array or slice module constants (1d)(1e). Struct arguments shall be structs with a byte layout (§6.2.6 (9)), and either a lit s do … end in which each field has a value computable as in (1a) (inner structs written the same way) or a struct answer (1f) — a module constant or a call in that place. The fn receives that struct as it would at the boundary of the program. In a 128-bit field, write one number literal. A slice-of-structs argument is a lit slice s … of such literals or an array · slice answer of structs (1d)(1e) — if it is the answer of a struct with slice fields, the processor hands over the bytes those fields point at as well. mut inputs are not accepted. If that fn overflows, breaks a contract or does not end within the fixed number of steps, or an argument cannot be known at translation time, it is refused with E-COMPTIME-NONCONST, and the message says why.

1c-1 The fixed number of steps is a number the processor states, and the build settings change it — build comptime_steps <n> . in the manifest, and above that the command line --comptime-steps <n>. An answer run at translation can be kept and used again in the next translation; a kept answer is used only when that fn’s definition · input bytes · processor edition · target · step budget are all the same. With or without keeping, the answers and refusals are the same.

1d An fn whose answer is an array (§6.2.6 (1c)) is run too. The elements are sized numbers · bool · rows of such elements (arrays inside arrays) · structs (§6.2.6 (9) · (9-1)). The processor provides the place of the result, runs that fn, and the bytes after running become read-only bytes fixed in the program — the same place as a list whose elements are all constants (§6.2.6 (7) ⓑ). That answer is given a name with the same length and element type as the result of f, as in let x array n t comptime f … .. It may be a let inside a body or a module constant. Elements cannot be written through that name — set · copy · swap are refused (E-TYPE-MUT · E-IMMUTABLE for set on a module constant), and passing it to a mut position is refused (E-TYPE-ARGMUT). Binding it with var, binding it to a name of a different length or element type, or putting it in the middle of an expression is refused (E-RESULT-PLACE). Whether two answers with the same contents are the same place is, as in ⓑ, a free choice (FC-0003).

1d-1 Only pure fns run at translation, so such an fn builds no new storage — the place of the answer is provided from outside (by the processor), and slice fields inside the answer point at the fn’s inputs or at constant bytes of the program. The processor puts the bytes pointed at into the read-only place of (1d) and rewrites the addresses of the fields to that place. If a field points elsewhere (at bytes in that fn’s frame), it is refused. If the number of answers cannot be known in advance, write a generous length and count how many were filled separately (the example below).

1e An fn whose answer is a slice of numbers · bools · structs (output slice t .) is run too. The bytes of the answer are copied and kept as the same read-only bytes as (1d) — whether that fn gave an answer pointing at a string or a piece of its input. If the elements are structs with slice fields, the addresses of the fields are rewritten as in (1d-1). The name is given with let x slice t comptime f … .; it may be a let inside a body or a module constant, and writing is refused as in (1d). A slice of rows (output slice (array m t) .) is not run (E-COMPTIME-NONCONST) — answers of rows are given as arrays (1d).

1f An fn whose answer is a struct is run too. The answer is a value of that struct — the same as a lit s do … end that writes each field’s run value as a constant (not a window view s). The fields are numbers · bool · arrays and slices of numbers (their bytes are put in the read-only place of (1d)) · structs with only such fields. A 128-bit field goes in as it is, up to its upper half. If there is a field holding option · result · a reference, it is not run (E-COMPTIME-NONCONST).

1g include_text "<path>" is the bytes (slice u8, read-only) of the file at that path, read at translation. The path is looked up from the folder of the source file containing this text and stays inside that folder — absolute paths and .. are refused, and so is reaching outside that folder by following links. This shape stands only in the input position of an fn run at translation (1c). In other places it is refused (E-INCLUDE-PLACE) — because it would read as if the file were read at execution. If the file cannot be read, points outside the folder, or exceeds the size the processor states, that comptime place is refused saying why. Those bytes are the input bytes of (1c-1) — if the file changes, a kept answer is not used. In a build where the processor pins dependencies with a lock file, files read are also pinned by content, like dependencies: the entry’s name is include:<the path seen
from the manifest folder>, and a different content is refused with E-DEP-HASH, a missing entry with E-DEP-UNLOCKED. The pinning entries are written by --lock-write.

1b A computed answer is a constant. The receiving place measures that answer like a literal — if it does not fit the type of the place, it is refused like a literal (E-TYPE-WIDTH). The types of the names used in the answer do not become the type of the answer.

2 A let at module level is a named constant. Its value too shall come out at translation time (E-CONST-NOTCOMPTIME). The value is an integer literal or comptime <expr>.
