That expression can read other module constants, and it is the same whether the constant read is before or after it. If they read each other and no value comes out, it is refused.

Caution —

What (1) says is not «this expression is not a constant» but «today’s processor cannot compute this». The two are different, and this document does not hide the difference — if what cannot be done is written as if it were impossible in principle, nobody notices when it becomes possible later.

Rejected example — no value comes out at translation

Source

module ex_comptime .

fn f input a u8 . output u8 .
do
  let b u8 comptime a. . .    rem `a` is fixed only at execution
  return b. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-COMPTIME-NONCONST

Example — computing a constant from constants

Source

module ex_comptime_names .

let KIB u64 1024 .
let PAGE u64 comptime mul 4 KIB. . . .    rem reads another module constant

fn f output u64 .
do
  let extra u64 4 .
  return comptime add PAGE. extra. . . .
end .

Output

f() = 4100

Example — running a pure function at translation

Source

module ex_comptime_fn .

fn fact input n u64 . output u64 .
do
  var r u64 1 .
  var i u64 1 .
  while le i. n. . do
    set r. mul r. i. . .
    set i. add i. 1 . .
  end .
  return r. .
end .

fn f output u64 .
do
  return comptime fact 10 . . .    rem at execution it is one number
end .

Output

f() = 3628800

Example — building a table at translation

Source

module ex_comptime_table .

fn crc_table output r array 256 u32 .
do
  var n u32 0 .
  while lt n. 256 . do
    var c u32 n. .
    var k u32 0 .
    while lt k. 8 . do
      if eq bit_and c. 1 . 1 . do set c. bit_xor 3988292384 shr c. 1 . . .
      end else do set c. shr c. 1 . . end .
      set k. add k. 1 . .
    end .
    set idx r. cast usize n. . . c. .
    set n. add n. 1 . .
  end .
  return r. .
end .

let CRC array 256 u32 comptime crc_table . . .    rem at execution it is one read-only table

fn f output u32 .
do
  return idx CRC. 255 . .
end .

Output

f() = 755167117

Example — trimming text at translation

Source

module ex_comptime_slice .

fn trim input s slice u8 . output slice u8 .
do
  var a usize 0 .
  var b usize len s. . .
  while and lt a. b. . eq idx s. a. . 32 . . do set a. add a. 1 . . end .
  while and gt b. a. . eq idx s. sub b. 1 . . 32 . . do set b. sub b. 1 . . end .
  return subslice s. a. b. . .
end .

let NAME slice u8 comptime trim "  hi  " . . .    rem read-only bytes "hi"

fn f output usize .
do
  return len NAME. . .
end .

Output

f() = 2

Example — building a message table at translation — without allocation

Source

module ex_comptime_messages .

struct msg do
  ko slice u8 .
  en slice u8 .
end .

fn parse input txt slice u8 . output r array 4 msg .
do
  var row usize 0 .
  var start usize 0 .
  var comma usize 0 .
  var i usize 0 .
  while le i. len txt. . . do
    if or eq i. len txt. . . eq idx txt. i. . 10 . . do
      if gt i. start. . do
        set idx r. row. . lit msg do ko subslice txt. start. comma. . . en subslice txt. add comma. 1 . i. . . end . .
        set row. add row. 1 . .
      end .
      set start. add i. 1 . .
    end else do
      if eq idx txt. i. . 44 . do set comma. i. . end .
    end .
    set i. add i. 1 . .
  end .
  return r. .
end .

let MSGS array 4 msg comptime parse "hi,hello\nthx,thanks" . . .    rem the fields point at pieces of the input text

fn f output usize .
do
  return len field idx MSGS. 1 . en . . .
end .

Output

f() = 6

Example — passing a struct and receiving a slice of structs

Source

module ex_comptime_structs .

struct pt do
  x u16 .
  y u16 .
end .

fn scale input p pt . input k u16 . output pt .
do
  return lit pt do x mul field p. x . k. . . y mul field p. y . k. . . end . .
end .

fn from input ps slice pt . input k usize . output slice pt .
do
  return subslice ps. k. len ps. . . .    rem a piece of the input — builds no new storage
end .

let P pt comptime scale lit pt do x 3 . y 4 . end . 10 . . .
let REST slice pt comptime from lit slice pt lit pt do x 1 . y 2 . end . lit pt do x 5 . y 6 . end . . 1 . . .

fn f output u16 .
do
  return add add field P. x . field P. y . . field idx REST. 0 . x . . .
end .

Output

f() = 75

Rejected example — a file read at translation is an input of a function run at translation

Source

module ex_include_place .

fn f output usize .
do
  return len include_text "words.txt" . . .    rem it would read as if read at execution
end .

Diagnostic: E-INCLUDE-PLACE

Rejected example — the computed answer does not fit the receiving place

Source

module ex_comptime_width .

let K u64 6 .

fn f output u8 .
do
  return comptime mul K. 100 . . .    rem 600 does not fit `u8`
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-WIDTH

6.9 Places that cannot be checked — unsafe and extern§

1 unsafe marks a place that does what the language cannot check. It is an effect, so it shall be written in the contract (§7.1), and it spreads upward to the callers.

2 extern marks a place that meets something written in another language (§7.4).

3 These two places shall be narrow. Widening them widens by as much the area the language’s guarantees do not reach.

In plain words

The word «unsafe» does not mean “anything goes”. It is a mark that says from here on, not the language but a person is responsible, and that mark remains in the source so it can be found later.

6.9.1 Writing machine instructions directly — asm§

1 The body of an op can be written as machine instructions. Write an asm <machine> . clause in the declaration, and in the body write that machine’s assembly.

2 The processor cannot read what is inside. The assembler reads it. That is, every invariant this language keeps can be broken inside, and the processor cannot see that. What cannot be seen is confined — all of the following four shall be present.

Table 42 — What an op that uses asm shall have

To haveWhy
the unsafe markit marks honestly what cannot be seen
cap machineit receives the right to issue machine instructions — not an authority floating around
effects unsafecarried in the signature, so the caller knows
the machine nameassembly is not portable. The processor does not pretend it is

3 The machine name comes from the closed set of §5.6. Other names are refused (E-ASM-TARGET), and assembly for a machine different from the one being built for is refused too — the processor neither silently drops that op nor hands another machine’s instructions to the assembler.

4 Operands are <register> <name> for inputs and out <register> <name> for outputs. Writing only a register without what to carry is refused (E-ASM-OPERAND).

5 The clobber clause writes what these instructions destroy, and the options clause the promises given to the processor.

Caution —

asm is a clause word, so it cannot come in a value position. That is why the name of the capability is not cap asm but cap machine — a name collision is not discovered and fixed but removed by design.

6 The body is the assembly itself — one text literal, and nothing else can come there (E-ASM-BODY). Machine instructions and lowered code cannot be mixed.

7 The processor cannot read inside the template, but the operand list and the {name}s of the template are two expressions that say the same thing, so whether they match is checked both ways.

Table 43 — Places where asm is refused

DiagnosticWhat went wrong
E-ASM-UNBOUNDthe asm clause never declared a name the template calls. The assembler refuses it, or worse, simply reads whatever register was there
E-ASM-UNUSEDthe template does not use a declared operand. It burns a register and says something the assembly does not — an unchecked duplicate rots into a lie (⟦§1.1⟧)
E-ASM-BINDan input operand points at something that is not a value (a name that is neither a parameter nor a local). If it is not a value, whatever was in that place is carried
E-ASM-BODYthe body is not one text literal
E-ASM-OPERANDonly a register was written, without what to carry
E-ASM-NOUNSAFE · E-ASM-NOCAP · E-ASM-NOEFFECTone of the three things to have in the table above is missing
E-ASM-TARGET · E-ASM-TARGET-UNKNOWNthe machine name differs from the machine being built for, or is outside the closed set of ⟦§5.6⟧
E-ASM-OPTLIEoptions pure disagrees with the effects line

8 options pure says “this assembly has no side effects”, and trusting that, the processor may remove, hoist, or run once instead of twice these instructions. But if the effects line says it touches input/output · state · devices, both cannot be true. The processor then trusts options, so the disagreement is refused (E-ASM-OPTLIE).

Caution —

When what the processor trusts and what the processor checks differ, the dangerous side is always what it trusts. options is a promise that cannot be checked, so if that promise disagrees with a declaration elsewhere, it is stopped on the spot.

6.9.2 Where C is met — the boundary of extern§

1 An op marked extern has its body in C. So writing a body on this side is refused (E-FFI-BODY) — two bodies are not a program but a question nobody can answer.

1a An extern op whose body is in C is a block declaration. As a struct holds its fields in do … end, it holds its clauses in do … end: extern proc <name> do <clause>* end .. Each clause is closed by its own dot (§6.4.1 (3c)). Only clauses come in the block — if a statement comes, there are two bodies (E-FFI-BODY). The shape that lists clauses after the name without do and closes with end is refused (E-STMT-NODO) — end closes only its own do (§6.1.6 (2a)).

Example — an op whose body is in C — its clauses go in do … end

Source

module ex_extern_block .

unsafe extern proc c_abs do
  input k cap c .
  input a i64 .
  output i64 .
  effects unsafe .
  link "llabs" .
end .

Rejected example — clauses listed without do and closed with end

Source

module ex_extern_nodo .

unsafe extern proc c_abs input k cap c . input a i64 . output i64 .
  effects unsafe .
  link "llabs" .
end

Diagnostic: E-STMT-NODO

2 export extern is the opposite direction. C calls us, so the body is on this side, and without a body it is refused (E-FFI-BODY).

2a The name seen from C is lw followed, for each step of the path, by _<length><step>. The length is decimal and may have several digits. The path of an op in a module is [module, name], of an op belonging to a type [module, type, name], and of an op in a file without a module declaration [name] — inside of module listlit is lw_7listlit_6inside, and rect.area of module shapes is lw_6shapes_4rect_4area. A name in a module is unique whatever its kind (§6.10.3), so the path fixes the name to one, and since the length is written, underscores inside names are not read as step boundaries. Names with a digit after lw_ belong to this rule; names with a letter after lw_ belong to the processor itself.

2b If link "<C name>" . is written in the head, that name becomes the C name as it is. That name shall be acceptable to C — ASCII letters · digits · underscores, not starting with a digit, not a reserved word of C or C++, and not starting with lw_, __ or _ + a capital letter. Otherwise it is refused (E-LINK-NAME). If two exports in one translation unit have the same C name, it is refused (E-ABI-NAME-DUP).

Rejected example — a C reserved word written as the C name

Source

module ex_link_keyword .

export fn inside input a u64 . output u64 .
  link "inline" .
do
  return add a. 1 . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-LINK-NAME

3 An op that calls C shall have all of the following three. If even one is missing, it is refused.

Table 44 — What an op that calls C shall have

To haveIf missingWhy
the unsafe markE-FFI-NOUNSAFEinside that function everything this language keeps can be broken, and the processor cannot see it. What cannot be checked shall at least be marked
input k cap c .E-FFI-NOCAPentering C is a right that is handed over — the same as the heap (⟦§8.4⟧) · devices · machine instructions (⟦§6.9.1⟧). A door floating around is not a door
an effects line (at least unsafe)E-FFI-NOEFFECTthe effects line is how the caller learns what it takes on. C hidden behind a clean signature is exactly the hidden cost this language sets out to remove
link "<symbol>" .E-FFI-LINKa C name is a promise made to another language, and the promise is the author’s. If it were made up from the op name, the moment the op is renamed it would call another function, and that fact would be nowhere in the source

3a The right is judged by kind. Receiving a right other than cap c (allocators · file systems …) does not open this place (E-FFI-CAPKIND). Not having one but what it is makes the authority.

4 The types that cross the boundary are limited to what C’s ABI can express. Anything else (⟦option⟧ · ⟦result⟧ · vectors · containers) is refused (E-FFI-TYPE). C has no such things, and the processor does not pretend it does.

4a Only pieces (slices) are mapped automatically. slice <type> becomes two arguments: «a pointer to <type>» and «a count». The width is known — slice u32 is uint32_t*, not a byte pointer. Everything else is written by hand.

4a-1 Integer arguments cross at their declared width — 8 · 16 · 32-bit integers are the C integer types of that width and sign (int8_t · uint8_t · int16_t · uint16_t · int32_t · uint32_t), and other integers are long long. The result of an extern is at its declared width too. The header the processor emits writes those prototypes, so if the C side includes that header, a mismatched prototype becomes a translation error.

4b Rights do not cross. A cap is not a value but an authority, so there is nothing to hand to C.

4c A struct with a byte layout (§6.2.7 — all fields sized numbers · arrays · such structs) crosses by value. What is passed when calling C, and what is received and returned when C calls us, is a C struct with the same field order and widths. Whether a large struct is returned through a hidden pointer is decided by the C compiler according to the target’s conventions — this language does not expose that in the signature. A struct with a 128-bit integer field does not cross (E-INT128-PLACE, §6.2.2 (3b-2)).

4d If an input of an op that gives a name for C to call (export) is an ⟦option⟧ · a vector · a bitset or a struct without a layout, or its result is one of those · a piece, the processor does not emit the C name and says so (W-EXPORT-NOSYM) — the header does not promise that name either. It never writes another type in the C signature and discards the value.

5 There is one ABI name, c. sysv · win64 · aapcs64 are not made into words — what they ask is “what is C on this machine”, and the machine being built for (§5.6) already fixes that answer. Adding words would make two ways of saying the same thing.

6 Variadic arguments go one way only. Variadic C functions can be called, but C cannot call our variadic ones — that would be work done without knowing the number and types of arguments at run time, with no place for a contract to stand.

7 There is no new convention for callbacks. An export extern op can already be called by C, so a callback is passing the address of that op (unsafe_fn <op>) as a value. The boundary contract is checked at the place where the door is entered.

7a So what unsafe_fn can point at is only an export extern op. Pointing at any other op is refused (E-FN-NOTEXPORT) — handing over the address of a door C cannot call is not an address but a question.

7b An op used as a callback cannot require capabilities (E-FN-CAP). What enters a callback is C, and C has no capabilities to hand over. Work that needs capabilities is done outside the callback, and inside the callback there is only computation.

7c After unsafe_fn there shall come the name of an op (E-FN-VALUE), and an op of that name shall actually exist (E-FN-UNDEF).

Caution —

A capability is handed over by the caller (§7.2), and the caller of a callback is C. So if a callback requires a capability, that place becomes a place nobody can fill. Rather than discovering it empty at run time, that shape is refused at translation.

8 Passing owned <type> to an extern moves the responsibility to C. This side’s duty ends at that point, and giving it back afterwards is C’s job. Whether that is kept is not verified — inside the door we are responsible, outside the door C is.

9 If a raw pointer (§8.5) mismatches at the boundary, it is refused (E-UPTR-TYPE).

10 An exported type cannot take its alignment from the machine (E-ABI-TARGET-ALIGN). Writing something like align machine.cache_line makes the layout differ by machine, while the header the C side reads assumes there is one layout — the two part without a word. Give exported types alignment with a written number (align N .).

In plain words

What this clause does is not make C convenient to call, but make the places that call C narrow and visible. The mark (unsafe) · the right (cap c) · the effects line each tell the same fact to people, to the processor and to the caller. With only one of the three it «can still be called» — but then this language can no longer say what it has lost.

6.9.3 Places that use instructions that differ by machine — unsafe target§

1 Some ops have meaning only when a particular instruction set is present. Such ops write that fact with unsafe target <instruction set>.

2 An instruction-set name the processor does not know is refused (E-TARGET-ISET). The names form a closed set.

3 If the name is known but the machine being built for cannot provide it, it is refused (E-TARGET-INTRIN).

3a What this clause blocks is the place where that op stands, not each op individually. Which leaves must be inside this fence is decided only when the meaning of that leaf depends on the machine — an op written to give the same value on every machine is not inside this fence.

Note (informative)

There was a clause in this place that tied avg down as «machine-bound». It was deleted (2026-09-17) — §6.3.7 wrote it as an ordinary lane op, and both back ends of this edition emitted it not as a machine instruction but as widened integer arithmetic. That is, what it was blocking was a machine dependency that did not even exist. Where the text and the tool part, follow the text.

In plain words

This place is unsafe because the meaning of that op depends on the machine. What this language keeps shall be the same whatever the machine, and here that promise is let go — so the fact of letting go shall be written in the source.

Caution —

Among the library leaves that reach the host, some today go down only to POSIX. Trying to build such a leaf for a machine without it is refused (E-TARGET-LEAF) — what is not there is not sent down pretending it is.

6.10 Modules§

1 One source file is one module. A module writes its own name on its first line.

2 A module name need not be the same as the file name.

6.10.1 What is exported§

1 Only what is marked export can be used by other modules. What is not marked is used only inside that module.

2 That is, hidden is the default. The exporting side shall write it.

2a export attaches to declarations: fn · proc · struct · enum · type · newtype · actor · trait. It does not attach to module constants (let) · module variables (var) · test blocks (E-TOPLEVEL) — a constant is not part of a module’s surface (give it out through an op), and a test belongs to the module that has it.

2b If the signature of an exported op uses a named type that is not exported (struct · enum · newtype · actor), the processor reports it (W-EXPORT-HIDDEN) — the importing side cannot write that type, so it cannot make a name to receive the value, and that export cannot be used from outside. The program itself conforms, so it is a report, not a refusal. Transparent aliases (type) are not included here, since the underlying type can be written. Why it is not a refusal: in a unit made of a single module there is no «outside», so nobody exists who cannot use that export. The processor cannot know whether this unit will be used as a library or end as a program, so it does not refuse what has not yet done harm.

Example — exporting

Source

module ex_export .

rem Only what is marked `export` is used by other modules.
export fn visible input n u32 . output u32 .
do
  return mul n. 2 . .
end .

rem This is used only inside this module.
fn hidden input n u32 . output u32 .
do
  return add n. 1 . .
end .

In plain words

Some languages are the other way round — public by default, with a mark to hide. This language makes hiding the default because what is visible from outside is a promise. Hiding something by mistake is easier to fix than promising something by mistake.

6.10.2 What is imported§

1 use imports another module. Where to import it from is written too.

2 The things inside are called through the name of the imported module.

3 If the module is not at the place to import from, translation is refused.

Grammar shape — importing — the form that writes the place

module <my module name> .

use <module to import> from "<the place of that module>" .

4 There is also a form that does not write the place. Then that module shall be in the same translation unit — when several files are handed over as one unit, it is looked for among them.

Grammar shape — importing — within the same unit

module <my module name> .

use <module to import> .

4a use <module> [from "<place>"] as <alias> . renames how that module is called. The alias stands in every place where a module name stands — the place that qualifies an op (<alias>.<op>), and the place that writes a type (<alias>.<actor>), alike.

4b Once an alias is written, the original module name does not stand within that unit (E-USE-ALIASED). An alias is not an additional name but a renaming. If both names stood, there would be code that calls the same module with two spellings, and the reader would have to check whether the two are the same — the alias was written because the original name was inconvenient, and if that name kept standing, the alias would have done nothing.

In plain words

A discipline this language repeats stands here too: one meaning, one spelling. If there are two ways of writing the same thing, a reader has to know both, and someone fixing it has to fix both. The use line itself is the exception — that is exactly the place where the original name is written.

Grammar shape — importing — under another name

module <my module name> .

use <module to import> as <alias> .

5 If a name whose place is not written is not in the unit, the processor shall say so. The processor of this edition says so with a warning (W-USE-EXTERNAL) — there is no search path for looking for modules, so whether that name exists cannot be known from this unit alone. If that name is actually used, it is refused then.

Note (informative)

Having no search path is the point of this design. If only a name were written and a tool fetched it from somewhere, what the program relies on would become knowledge outside the source — the same source could import different things on different machines. Either write the place, or hand them over together as one unit, one or the other.

Note (informative)

This place is written as shapes instead of examples that actually run. Importing translates only if the file actually exists, so an example that runs anywhere cannot be made. What the checker compiles is only #ex, and shapes are not its subject.

Note (informative)

Writing in the source where something is imported from connects with the discipline of this language — if only names were written and the tool found on its own where they come from, what the program relies on would become knowledge outside the source.

6.10.3 When names collide§

1 The same name cannot be declared twice in one module. Doing so is refused at translation.

1a The top-level names of a module are in one namespace — modules · fn · proc (including extern) · type · newtype · struct · enum and its variants · actor · trait · module let/var · test. Even of different kinds, the same name is refused (E-NAME-DUP). Fields · ops belonging to a type (<type>.<name>) · the state and handlers of an actor · the ops a trait requires are inside their container, and in different containers the same name can be used.

2 If imported names collide with one another, which one is meant shall be written out at the place of the call.

Rejected example — the same name declared twice

Source

module ex_dup .

fn f output u32 . do return 1 . end .
fn f output u32 . do return 2 . end .

Diagnostic: E-NAME-DUP

Rejected example — even of different kinds it is one namespace — a module let and an fn

Source

module ex_dup_kinds .

let limit u64 3 .
fn limit output u64 . do return 1 . end .

Diagnostic: E-NAME-DUP

6.10.4 The order of declarations§

1 The top-level declarations in a module do not depend on order. An op declared later can be called earlier, and ops that call each other can be used.

2 Inside an op body it is not so — local names shall be declared before they are used.

In plain words

The two rules differ because the ways of reading differ. A whole file is read by skimming, but a function body is read from top to bottom. If a name that has not appeared yet is used in a body, the reader has to go back.

6.10.5 Ops inside an op — local ops§

1 fn · proc declarations can be put directly below an op body (in the statement positions of the body’s do … end). These are called local ops. Putting them inside if · loops · blocks or inside another local op, or declaring types · actors · traits · tests · modules inside a body, does not conform (E-LOCAL-PLACE).

2 The name of a local op is visible only inside the body of that op. Inside it, order does not matter as with top-level declarations — it can be called before its declaration, and local ops of the same op can call each other and call back (§6.10.4 (1)). Other ops do not see that name. Different ops can use the same local name.

3 A local op does not see the outer op’s parameters · locals · regions · borrows · capabilities. Using such names does not conform (E-LOCAL-CAPTURE). Values that are needed are passed as parameters. A local op has the same head (clauses) and body as an ordinary op, and the same rules of effects · contracts · errors · ownership. The effects of a local proc are the effects of the place that calls it (§7).

4 The name of a local op does not hide the name of the outer op, its parameters · locals, or names the module already has (E-NAME-SHADOW). Local ops of one op cannot have the same name (E-NAME-DUP), and the names of core ops cannot be used (E-NAME-BUILTIN).

5 A local op cannot carry export · extern · unsafe (E-LOCAL-EXPORT) — to use it from outside, declare it at the top level.

Example — a helper op put close to where it is used

Source

module ex_local .

export fn count_selected input xs slice u8 . input mask u8 . output u64 .
do
  fn has_bits input x u8 . input bits u8 . output bool .
  do
    return eq bit_and x. bits. . bits. . .
  end .
  return pipe xs. do
    filter has_bits with mask. .
    count .
  end . .
end .

Rejected example — a local op does not see outer names

Source

module ex_local_capture .

export fn f input n u64 . output u64 .
do
  fn g input k u64 . output u64 .
  do
    return add k. n. . .
  end .
  return g 1 . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-LOCAL-CAPTURE

Rejected example — a local op is put only directly below the body

Source

module ex_local_place .

export fn f input n u64 . output u64 .
do
  if gt n. 1 . do
    fn g input k u64 . output u64 .
    do
      return k. .
    end .
  end .
  return n. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-LOCAL-PLACE

Note (informative)

A local op is not a closure. Only the range in which its name is visible is narrow; it takes nothing secretly from outside, so the processor treats it like an ordinary op — there is no object made at run time and no hidden argument.

6.11 Type-associated ops and traits§

6.11.1 Attaching an op to a type§

1 If a type name and a dot are put in front of an op’s name, that op becomes attached to that type.

2 The first input of an attached op is a value of that type.

6.11.2 Traits§

1 A trait is a list of ops a type shall provide. It writes the name and signature of each op.

In plain words

When there are several shapes (rectangle · square …), the method of finding the area differs by type, but the promise “it tells its area” is the same. A trait gives that promise a name. Then an op that takes “anything that tells its area” can be written once — the same op need not be copied for each type, and a type that does not keep the promise is caught at translation. The call site need not know what the type is. This language has no inheritance, so this is the way to handle several types under one name.

Example — what a trait is for — one op takes several types

Source

module ex_trait_why .

rem Each shape finds its area differently. But the promise «it tells its area» is the same.
trait shape do
  area input s self . output u64 .
end .

struct rect do
  satisfies shape .
  w u64 .
  h u64 .
end .

struct square do
  satisfies shape .
  side u64 .
end .

fn rect.area input s rect . output u64 .
do
  return mul field s. w . field s. h . . .
end .

fn square.area input s square . output u64 .
do
  return mul field s. side . field s. side . . .
end .

rem This op takes **any shape** — `requires shape t` pins it to «only types that tell their area».
fn double_area input comptime t type . input s t . output u64 .
  requires shape t. . .
do
  return mul 2 method s. area. . . .
end .

fn demo output u64 .
do
  let r lit rect do w 2 . h 3 . end . .
  let q lit square do side 4 . end . .
  return add double_area rect. r. . double_area square. q. . . .
end .

Output

demo() = 44

1a A trait can write several ops. A signature begins with the op’s name and that op’s clauses follow, and the next name opens the next signature. It is customary to write one signature per line. The clauses of a signature follow the same order as an op head (§6.4.1 (3a)) — inputs · output · effects. There is no limit on the number of ops written.

1b fn · proc are not written in a signature. What that op can do is decided by the signature’s effects line.

  • Without an effects line, it is an op with no effects. It can be provided by an fn, or by a proc that writes its effects.

A proc always writes an effects line (§7.1 without one, E-EFFECT-MISSING) — a proc that provides an effect-free signature writes effects none ..

  • With an effects line, the providing side is a proc that writes those effects or fewer, or an fn if there are no effects.

2 Inside a trait, self refers to the type that will provide it.

3 If a type writes a trait with satisfies, the processor checks that the ops of that list exist with exactly those signatures. If even one is missing or mismatched, translation is refused.

4 There are five kinds of mismatch.

Table 45 — Places that fail to provide a trait

DiagnosticWhat went wrong
E-TRAIT-UNDEFsatisfies calls a trait that is not declared — a claim that points at nothing checks nothing
E-TRAIT-MISSINGan op of the list is missing
E-TRAIT-SIGthe op exists but the number of parameters differs — a trait that does not fit exactly cannot carry a contract
E-TRAIT-EFFECTthe providing side has effects the trait does not write. The caller judges by the trait’s contract, so doing what is not in that contract makes the judgment wrong
E-TRAIT-RECVan op wrote that it provides a trait — what provides is a type

5 If an op of a trait writes via self in its effects line, the providing side may write allocation-family effects — alloc · heap · lock · atomic — for that op beyond the trait. Writing any other additional effect is still E-TRAIT-EFFECT. The additional effects are carried by a generic op that calls through via <type parameter> up to its callers (§7.1.1 (6)).

In plain words

What a trait does is make «this type knows how to do these things» something the caller can trust. So if only the names match and the effects differ, that trust breaks — only when the effects match too does a trait carry a contract.

Example — providing a trait

Source

module ex_trait .

rem A trait is the list of ops a type shall provide.
trait shape do
  area input s self . output u64 . effects none .
end .

rem Writing `satisfies` has it checked whether that list is provided.
struct rect do
  satisfies shape .
  w u8 .
  h u8 .
end .

fn rect.area input s rect . output u64 .
do
  return mul widen u64 field s. w . . widen u64 field s. h . . . .
end .

Rejected example — writing that it provides and not providing

Source

module ex_trait_bad .

trait shape do
  area input s self . output u64 . effects none .
end .

struct rect do
  satisfies shape .     rem it wrote that it provides
  w u8 .
  h u8 .
end .

rem `rect.area` was not made

Diagnostic: E-TRAIT-MISSING

Example — a trait with several ops

Source

module ex_trait_many .

rem One line per signature — it begins with the name, then inputs · output · effects.
trait shape do
  area input s self . output u64 .
  perimeter input s self . output u64 .
  grow input s self . input k u64 . output self .
  checked_area input s self . output u64 . effects panic .
end .

struct rect do
  satisfies shape .
  w u64 .
  h u64 .
end .

rem A signature without an effects line is provided with an `fn`.
fn rect.area input s rect . output u64 .
do
  return mul field s. w . field s. h . . .
end .

fn rect.perimeter input s rect . output u64 .
do
  return mul 2 add field s. w . field s. h . . . .
end .

fn rect.grow input s rect . input k u64 . output rect .
do
  return lit rect do w add field s. w . k. . . h add field s. h . k. . . end . .
end .

rem A signature that writes effects is provided with a `proc` that writes those effects.
proc rect.checked_area input s rect . output u64 . effects panic .
do
  if eq field s. w . 0 . do panic "empty rect" . end .
  return mul field s. w . field s. h . . .
end .

Rejected example — trying to provide an effect-free signature with a proc without an effects line

Source

module ex_trait_proc_noeff .

trait shape do
  area input s self . output u64 .
end .

struct rect do
  satisfies shape .
  w u64 .
  h u64 .
end .

proc rect.area input s rect . output u64 .
do
  return mul field s. w . field s. h . . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TRAIT-EFFECT

In plain words

A trait is a way of writing “this type can do these things” in a form that is checked. Written as a comment, it stays the same even when the code changes, but with satisfies, translation is refused the moment it fails to provide.

Caution — It is not inheritance

A trait makes no hierarchy among types. That a type provides a trait is only the fact “it has those ops”; it inherits nothing from another type. This language has no inheritance.

6.11.3 Calling an attached op — method§

1 method is the form that calls an op attached to a value. Its shape is method <value> <step> … <name> <argument> … ..

2 Steps and the name are told apart by following the type. Going down one step at a time from the receiver’s type, if that type has an attached op of that name, that is the name, and everything after it is arguments. So method o inner area calls area attached to inner of o, and method s plus 5 calls plus attached to s with 5.

3 The receiver need not be a name. It may be the result of another form, and then the receiver’s type is that form’s output type — so method (method x grow 2) area holds.

4 If the receiver’s type cannot be decided, the processor refuses the program. Attached ops are found by type, so if the type is not known, the op to call is not decided either.

Example — calling an attached op

Source

module ex_method .

struct rect do
  w u64 .
  h u64 .
end .

fn rect.area input s rect . output u64 .
do
  return mul field s. w . field s. h . . .
end .

export fn twice_area input s rect . output u64 .
do
  return mul 2 method s. area. . . .
end .

Note (informative)

The infix notation (s..area) does not exist. It was the only place in the language read from right to left, and there was no way to write a multi-step receiver. Unified as a form, a call is always name first.

6.11.4 Where attached ops go wrong§

1 method requires both a receiver and a name (E-METHOD-RECV).

2 If the receiver’s type has no attached op of that name, it is refused (E-METHOD-UNDEF) — it shall be declared as fn <type>.<name>.

Caution —

An attached op is a device that divides the namespace, not inheritance (§6.11.2). So calling a name that does not exist does not search upward; it is refused right there.

Rejected example — that type has no attached op of that name

Source

module ex_method_undef .

struct p do
  x u8 .
end .

fn f input s p . output u8 .
do
  return method s. nosuch. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-METHOD-UNDEF

6.12 Flowing in one stream — pipe§

1 pipe is a statement that takes the elements of a source one at a time, passes them through stages in turn, and ends with one terminal. Its shape is as follows.

Grammar shape — the pipe statement

pipe <source> do
  <stage> .        rem may be absent. There may be several.
  <terminal> .     rem exactly one.
end

In plain words

A loop that goes through a slice almost always has the same skeleton — keep an index, check whether it is the end, take the element out and test a condition, accumulate something, increase the index. Writing that skeleton by hand buries what is meant to be done («keep only the digits and count them») between the index and the counter, and that is where off-by-one mistakes happen. pipe has the language take care of the skeleton and lets people write only what is meant to be done, one word per line. At the same time it goes through only once and makes no intermediate array, exactly like a loop written by hand (§6.12.1). The two ops below give the same answer.

Example — the same work as a hand-written loop and as a pipe — both go through once

Source

module ex_pipe_why .

fn is_digit input c u8 . output bool . do return and ge c. 48 . le c. 57 . . . end .

rem A hand-written loop — a person matches up the counter · index · condition · increment one by one.
fn digits_loop input s slice u8 . output u64 .
do
  var n u64 0 .
  var i usize 0 .
  while lt i. len s. . . do
    if is_digit idx s. i. . . do set n. add n. 1 . . end .
    set i. add i. 1 . .
  end .
  return n. .
end .

rem The same work with pipe — «keep only digits, and count». What is done shows as one word per line.
fn digits_pipe input s slice u8 . output u64 .
do
  return pipe s. do
    filter is_digit .
    count .
  end . .
end .

2 There are seven stages: filter · map · take · skip · scan · zip · enumerate. There are five terminals: collect into · fold · count · any · all. Using a word outside this list in a stage position does not conform (E-PIPE-STAGE).

2a The place of collect into <place> shall be able to hold the flowing values without losing them. Putting wider values into a narrower container does not conform (E-TYPE-COLLECT) — a conversion that loses values does not happen implicitly (§6.2.5 (1)). To narrow, do it in writing with map.

2b The meaning of each word is as follows. «The flow» is the order in which the source’s elements reach this place after passing the earlier stages, and «op» is a named op (4). The element type is written t, and the accumulator type a.

  • filter <op> (stage, op is t → bool) — sends on only the elements for which op is true. Elements for which it is false are discarded.
  • map <op> (stage, op is t → u) — calls op on each element and sends its answer on. The element type may change to u.
  • take <n> (stage) — sends only the first n and stops the traversal (§6.12.2). If there are fewer than n elements, as many as there are.
  • skip <n> (stage) — discards the first n and sends the rest.
  • enumerate <op> (stage, op is (u64, t) → u) — gives the ordinal i (from 0) and the element to op and sends its answer.
  • zip <source2> <op> (stage, op is (t, t2) → u) — gives the element and the element at the same position of source2 to op and sends its answer. When the shorter one ends, the flow ends.
  • scan <initial> <op> (stage, op is (a, t) → a) — sets the accumulator to initial, updates it for each element as accumulator = op(accumulator, element), and sends the accumulator after the update. The initial value itself is not sent.
  • collect into <place> (terminal) — puts the elements of the flow into <place> (mut slice) from the front and gives the number put in (u64). The place shall be long enough to hold the whole flow (2c).
  • fold <initial> <op> (terminal, op is (a, t) → a) — accumulates like scan but gives one final accumulator (a). If the flow is empty, it is the initial value.
  • count (terminal) — gives the number of elements of the flow (u64).
  • any <op> (terminal, op is t → bool) — true if there is an element for which op is true. False if the flow is empty. It stops at the first true.
  • all <op> (terminal, op is t → bool) — true if op is true for every element. True if the flow is empty. It stops at the first false.

2c When the receiving place is full, it does not stop silently. If it is known at translation time that the flow is longer than the receiving place — the two lengths are written in the head’s contract (requires eq len x. . N ., which array T N inputs become) and no stage in between has an unknown count (filter · zip) — translation refuses it (E-COLLECT-FULL). If it cannot be known, during execution, the moment an element arrives that cannot be held, it stops saying the receiving place is full (E-VM-BOUNDS; natively a panic with the same sentence). To put in only as many as fit, write take.

2d The op of filter · any · all is a predicate — its output shall be bool. Giving an op that gives a number does not conform (E-PIPE-PRED). This language has no rule that reads non-zero numbers as true (§6.2.16 (4)).

Rejected example — putting five into three slots — both lengths are known at translation time

Source

module ex_collect_full .

fn dbl input a u8 . output u8 . do return wrap_add a. a. . . end .

proc over input xs array 5 u8 . input out mut array 3 u8 . output u64 . effects none . do
  return pipe xs. do
    map dbl .
    collect into out. .
  end . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-COLLECT-FULL

Rejected example — an op that gives a number given in a predicate position

Source

module ex_pipe_pred .

fn as_flag input a u8 . output u8 . do return a. . end .

fn nonzero input xs slice u8 . output u64 . do
  return pipe xs. do
    filter as_flag .
    count .
  end . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-PIPE-PRED

3 A pipe statement has exactly one terminal. Ending without a terminal, or having two, does not conform.

4 The argument of a stage is a name that points at a named op. This language has no nameless functions (lambdas), so a function made on the spot cannot be given as a stage argument.

5 A stage op takes only one element — it has exactly one parameter and takes no capability. Using any other op as a stage does not conform (E-FOLD-OP). If a context (7) is written, there is one more parameter.

5a The op of fold and scan has two parameters, the first being the accumulator and the second the element. One step is accumulator = op(accumulator, element), so the type of the first parameter and the output type shall be the same. Writing them as different types does not conform (E-FOLD-ORDER).

6 The count of take and skip shall be a non-negative value fixed at translation time (§6.8). Giving a value known only at execution time does not conform.

7 Stages and terminals that call an op — filter · map · any · all · scan · fold · zip · enumerate — can have a context with <expr> after the op name. The context is passed as the op’s last argument: filter · map · any · all take (element, context), fold · scan (accumulator, element, context), zip (element, partner element, context), and enumerate (ordinal, element, context). If the number of parameters or the type of the context does not match, or the context parameter is mut · owned, it does not conform (E-PIPE-CONTEXT-ARG). Writing a context on take · skip · count · collect, which call no op, or a word other than with coming after the op name, or what follows with not being one expression, does not conform (E-PIPE-WITH). with is only a marker of this place, not a keyword (annex A.1).

7a A context is a copied value without references. The type of the context parameter — including struct fields, the contents of every variant of an enum (unused variants too), the underlying type of aliases · newtypes, the elements of arrays, and the contents of option · result — shall not hold slices, references (ref · mut_ref), pointers, capabilities (cap), regions, owned values or actors (E-PIPE-CONTEXT-TYPE). An expression that uses a capability may make a context, but a capability cannot be put inside the context and handed over. If the context parameter is of an array type (array n t), its value crosses pointing at storage, so it cannot be a context (E-PIPE-CONTEXT-TYPE). An array that is a struct field is copied with the struct (§6.2.7 (4a)), so that struct can be a context.

7b The context expression is evaluated once, before the traversal, and its value is passed as the same value to every call. Even if the op changes its own copy, the next call receives the stored value. It is evaluated even if the source is empty. A pipe with a context evaluates the source and then the operands of each clause (the partner of zip, the initial value of scan · fold, the expression of with, the receiving place of collect into) in clause order, from the left. If preparation fails, the later preparations and the traversal do not happen. The effects of the context expression are the effects of the outer op (§7). The order of a pipe without a context is as before — the source, the terminal’s operands, then the stages in order.

Example — a threshold fixed at run time, as a context

Source

module ex_with .

fn above input x u8 . input limit u8 . output bool .
do
  return gt x. limit. . .
end .

export fn count_above input xs slice u8 . input base u8 . output u64 .
do
  return pipe xs. do
    filter above with wrap_add base. 1 . .
    count .
  end . .
end .

Rejected example — a context is a value without references

Source

module ex_with_slice .

fn over input x u8 . input s slice u8 . output bool .
do
  return gt x. idx s. 0 . . .
end .

export fn f input xs slice u8 . output u64 .
do
  return pipe xs. do
    filter over with xs. .
    count .
  end . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-PIPE-CONTEXT-TYPE

Rejected example — a terminal that calls no op takes no context

Source

module ex_with_count .

fn above input x u8 . input limit u8 . output bool .
do
  return gt x. limit. . .
end .

export fn f input xs slice u8 . output u64 .
do
  return pipe xs. do
    filter above with 3 .
    count with 1 .
  end . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-PIPE-WITH

Note (informative)

A context is not a lambda. A stage op is still a named op, and it does not secretly read outer names — it sees only the value passed as the context. References are blocked to close the way of copying only an address and watching outer storage throughout the traversal. If a large table has to be handed over, write an ordinary loop or an op call.

6.12.1 Fusion is meaning, not optimisation§

1 A pipe statement is executed in one pass. No intermediate collection is made between stages. This is not an optimisation the processor may try but the definition of this statement.

2 So the processor shall not make intermediate collections. A processor that makes them does not conform.

Note (informative)

Why make it a definition? If fusion were an optimisation, “how far is it fused” would differ between processors, and people writing code would meet a cliff — the place where changing one line suddenly creates an intermediate array and makes it slow. That cliff cannot be seen from the source. Here, operations that cannot be fused are not in the stage vocabulary at all, so there is no way to fall off the cliff by accident.

6.12.2 Reading only as much as needed§

1 any stops the traversal at the first element that gives true, and all at the first element that gives false. Elements after the stop are not read, and the stages for those elements are not executed.

2 take n stops the traversal after letting n elements through.

Note (informative)

This property is what lets pipe handle sources without an end. If stopping were not written in the meaning, a source without an end would keep the program from ever stopping.

Example — two stages and one terminal — one pass

Source

module ex_pipe .

fn over2 input a u8 . output bool . do return gt a. 2 . . end .

fn dbl input a u8 . output u8 . do return wrap_add a. a. . . end .

rem [1,2,3,4,5] → only those greater than 2 → doubled → put into out. No intermediate array is made.
proc fm input xs slice u8 . input out mut slice u8 . output u64 . effects none . do
  pipe xs. do
    filter over2 .
    map dbl .
    collect into out. .
  end .
  return 0 .
end .

In plain words

pipe is a statement in order to make fusion a promise. Were it an expression, stages could be detached as values and passed around, and then where one stream begins and ends would not be visible in the source. do … end nails that boundary down visibly.

6.12.3 map · filter · fold outside pipe — ops of a library§

1 Outside pipe, map · filter · fold are not core ops. The author can use the three names as the names of ops · parameters · locals. The same words inside pipe are stages and terminals (§6.12 (2)).

2 Moving a slice into another slice, filtering it or folding it into one is done by the ops map · filter · fold of the standard module collection (§9.3). The element type brings the work along — the element type provides the traits collection.mappable (apply) · collection.keepable (keep) · collection.foldable (combine) (§6.11.2), and the op takes that type as a translation-time argument. Giving a type that does not provide them is refused at translation.

2a collection.map and collection.filter write into the receiving place (mut slice) and give the number of elements written (u64) — the same as collect into. When the receiving place is full, execution stops the moment it tries to write.

2b The names that were core ops up to language revision 1.12 are six — map · filter · fold · count · seg · segs. From revision 1.13 all six are the author’s names. Calling such a name without a definition is refused like any other name (E-IR-UNDEF), and the processor says where to move as well — for count, bitset_count · stack_count · len (§6.2.12 (4a)); for seg · segs, segview.piece_at · segview.piece_count; for prefix map · filter · fold, the collection of (2) above.

Example — the element type brings the work along

Source

module ex_collection .

use collection .

struct twice do
  satisfies collection.mappable .
  v u8 .
end .

fn twice.apply input x twice . output twice . do
  return lit twice do v wrap_add field x. v . field x. v . . . end . .
end .

proc twice_all input xs slice u8 . input out mut slice u8 . output u64 . effects none . do
  return collection.map twice. view_array twice xs. . view_array twice out. . . .
end .

Output

twice_all([1,2,3], [0,0,0]) = 3

In plain words

These three were once core ops (in the shape map <receiving place> <op> <source>). The names of core ops cannot be the author’s names, so three common words were blocked. This language has no way of passing an op as a value, so library generics look for the work in the element’s type — the same way sorting finds comparison. Each call makes an op specific to that type, and the work is fixed as a direct call.

In plain words

Moving, filtering or folding a slice is usually written with pipe, without collection (§6.12 (2)). pipe does three things the way of (2) above cannot — it chooses ops by name, so one element type can have any number of transformations · predicates; it hands over values fixed at run time with with (§6.12 (7)); and if the op of map gives another type, the element type of the flow changes. Nor is there any need to wrap elements in a one-field struct. An op used in only one place is put inside that body as a local op (§6.10.5). collection is for general code in which the element’s type has to carry the work. Calling the old prefix map · filter · fold, the processor too mentions pipe first.

Example — moving is done with pipe — run-time values go in with

Source

module ex_pipe_move .

fn add_k input x u8 . input k u8 . output u8 . do
  return wrap_add x. k. . .
end .

proc shifted input xs slice u8 . input k u8 . input out mut slice u8 . output u64 . effects none . do
  return pipe xs. do
    map add_k with k. .
    collect into out. .
  end . .
end .

Output

shifted([1,2,3], 10, [0,0,0]) = 3

7 Effects and capabilities§

1 If §6 specified computation, this clause specifies how that computation touches the outside world.

7.1 Effects§

1 An effect is an influence an op has on the outside world. Reading a file, allocating memory, waiting and the like are each an effect.

2 Effects are a fixed set of atoms. The list of atoms is fixed, and the author cannot make new atoms.

Table 46 — Effect atoms

AtomWhat it means
noneno effect at all. This is the bottom
allocobtains or gives back memory from a fixed window — that window does not grow (⟦§8.1⟧)
heapobtains memory from a growing root — more can be fetched during execution. Exists only on machines with an operating system (⟦§8.1⟧)
ioexchanges data with the outside (files · connections · standard input and output)
waitwaits. It wakes up someday without anyone else’s help
concurrentits completion depends on the progress of another flow of execution
locktakes a lock
atomicdoes indivisible reads and writes
statechanges the state a module or actor holds
paniccan end the program with an explicit panic — traps such as out-of-bounds indexing and overflow are the business of contracts, not effects (⟦§6.4.6⟧)
devicetouches a device directly
unsafedoes what the language cannot check
page_faulta memory access may cause a page fault
blockingcan hold up a flow of execution
cancelcan be cancelled
detachdetaches a flow of execution it made

2a Of these, five — lock · page_fault · blocking · cancel · detach — have no core op yet that does that work. They can be written and spread upward to the callers ((4) below), but the processor has no way of finding out whether the body does that work, so it believes what is written. It does not refuse them when declared but not done ((6)), and it reports that it took them on trust.

2b device is produced by touching device registers (read_volatile · write_volatile aimed at a device’s registers, reading and writing the fields of a device register view) and by interrupt handlers (ops with a vector clause). Like other effects, if produced it shall be written, and if written but not produced it is reported ((6)).

3 An op shall write the effects it produces in its effects clause. If it produces more effects than written, translation is refused.

4 Effects spread upward to the callers. Calling an op adds that op’s effects to the caller’s effects.

4a state means changing the state the op itself holds (module state · the state fields of an actor). Writing to storage the caller handed over (mut · mut_ref positions, collect into <name>) is not state — that write is already declared by the type, and the caller sees it through its own name. It is not hidden state, so there is nothing to count as an effect. Such an op writing effects state is allowed, and writing it is not counted as «declared but not done» (W-EFFECT-OVER).

5 Some effects bring other effects along. The following table is all of them.

Table 47 — Effects brought along (the closure relation)

If writtenalso counts as writtenWhy
concurrentwaitif it has to wait for a peer’s progress, it may be suspended

5a So a declared effect set S is looked at closed — close(S) = S ∪ {wait | concurrent ∈ S}. Comparing two sets is also done after closing: a requirement I is contained in a declaration D when close(I) ⊆ close(D). So writing concurrent makes writing wait separately unnecessary, and writing only wait while doing something concurrent is refused.

5b Closing happens in one place only. The table of atoms (above) stays flat, and the inclusion relation is not mixed into that table.

Diagram — effects spread upward to the callers and are compared after closing

 worker  effects concurrent         close = {concurrent, wait}
   ▲ called by boss
 boss    effects state wait         close = {state, wait}               → E-EFFECT (no concurrent)
 boss    effects state concurrent   close = {state, concurrent, wait}   → ok

In plain words

What an effect declaration does, in one sentence — “what can happen when this function is called is written in its signature.” It can be known without opening the body and without following the other functions it calls.

6 Conversely, an effect declared but not produced is a mistake too. The processor reports it. Declared effects are a cost the caller has to bear (a pure op cannot call an op that produces io), so writing effects that are not produced overstates what this op does.

7 An fn is an op whose purity is fixed by contract, so its effect set is already none. So no effects clause is written on an fn. If one is written, the processor refuses the program — the meaning is the same, but it becomes two notations for one meaning, and then readers and tools have to know both. proc is different: there the effects clause narrows. A proc always writes that clause — without it, it is E-EFFECT-MISSING (if there are no effects, effects none .). When a meaning was fixed for the absence of the clause, that meaning parted from place to place where it was read.

Example — declaring effects

Source

module ex_effect .

rem A pure op — it touches nothing in the outside world. `fn` itself is that declaration.
export fn double input n u32 . output u32 .
  requires le n. 1000 . .
do
  return mul n. 2 . .
end .

rem An op that produces effects — which effects is written in the contract.
export proc note_and_add input n u32 . output u32 .
  effects panic .
  requires le n. 1000 . .
do
  if gt n. 500 . do panic . end .
  return add n. 1 . .
end .

Caution — An effect declaration is a check, not documentation

It differs from writing “this function reads a file” as a comment. A comment stays the same even when the code changes, but an effect declaration makes translation refuse the moment the body does more. And doing less is reported too — if the declaration and the body differ either way, that clause is a lie.

7.1.1 How to write the effects line§

1 The effects line is a set, not a list. Writing the same word twice is refused (E-EFFECT-DUP) — the second says nothing the first did not.

2 A word not in the list is refused (E-EFFECT-UNDEF). The names of effects come from the closed set of §7.1.

3 none cannot come together with anything else (E-EFFECT-NONE-MIX). Something cannot have effects and have none at once — either write the effects or write that there are none.

4 Adding effects none . to an op that is already pure by contract is refused (E-EFFECT-REDUNDANT). One meaning has one spelling.

5 A pure op cannot declare effects (E-EFFECT-CALC). Being pure and having effects are different kinds, and what chooses the kind is a word, not a clause.

6 via <type> can be written in the effects line. It means «the allocation-family effects (alloc · heap · atomic) written by that type’s ops are also this op’s declaration». If a generic op writes a type parameter (effects state via a .), that type decides for each monomorphised instance — opened with a bump over borrowed bytes it is only state, and opened with an allocator standing on a growing root, heap stands in the instance’s declaration. Effects that came in through via are not counted as «declared but not produced» even if this op produces only some of them.

In plain words

Without via, a generic container has to do one of two things — always write the largest effect any allocator could have (false when used with a bump), or write only the smallest (a hidden allocation when used with a heap). via lets the type argument decide that choice.

In plain words

What (1) · (4) prevent is not a wrong program but a second way of saying the same thing. When there are two ways, readers go looking for the difference between them and spend time finding out that there is none. And someday only one of them gets fixed.

Rejected example — a pure op cannot declare effects

Source

module ex_calc_eff .

fn f output u8 . effects io .     rem if it has effects, it is a `proc`
do
  return 1 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-EFFECT-CALC

7.2 Capabilities§

1 To produce an effect, there shall be the right to produce it. The value that stands for that right is a capability.

2 Capabilities are handed over as arguments. There is no global capability that can be taken out and used anywhere.

3 Capabilities have kinds. Which kind is written in the type, and if the kind does not match, translation is refused. A place that requires a capability authorises by kind — the mere existence of a capability value is not enough.

Table 48 — Kinds of capability

KindWhat can be done with it
cap iostandard input and output — reading and writing
cap clockasking the time
cap randomreading the operating system’s entropy
cap netmaking connections and exchanging data over them — turning names into addresses belongs here too
cap file_systemopening · reading · writing files and directories
cap processstarting other programs, reading their output, waiting for them to end or ending them
cap ttyasking the terminal’s size and changing its mode
cap allocatorobtaining memory from a fixed window (it does not grow)
cap heapobtaining memory from a growing root — can be received only on machines with an operating system
cap envreading the variables of the execution environment
cap ccalling C functions (§6.9)
cap mmioreading and writing device registers
cap argscounting · reading the arguments given when the program was called
cap machineissuing machine instructions directly (⟦§6.9⟧) — the processor cannot see inside

3a The names of capabilities are not closed — the author can make their own capability and receive it as a parameter. What is closed is the kinds whose meaning the processor fixes, and those are the rows of the table above.

3b To do work whose meaning the processor fixes (asking the time · opening a connection and the like), a capability of the kind that work requires shall be passed. If it does not match, it is refused in one of the following two ways.

Table 49 — The two shapes of a mismatched capability

What was passedWhat happens
a capability of another kind (cap io to a clock)refused as being of a different kind
a capability with a name the processor does not knowrefused because that effect cannot be produced with that capability

Note (informative)

Kinds are divided for least authority. If there were only one capability, a right received to read a file could also open connections, and then an op’s signature would no longer say “what this op can do”. Because kinds are divided, the extent of the outside world that can be reached is known from the signature alone.

Diagram — capabilities are received at the entry point and go down only as arguments

 environment ──▶ main  input out cap io .  input fs cap file_system .
                   ├──▶ save fs     holds file_system only → can open files, cannot print
                   └──▶ show out    holds io only          → can print, cannot open files
 an op handed no capability cannot have that effect — there is no global way to get one

4 The entry point of a program receives capabilities from the execution environment (§5.5). A capability the entry point did not receive exists nowhere in the program.

5 A capability is a mark at translation time, not a value at run time. So an op that receives a capability does not receive one more run-time argument because of it.

In plain words

That is, writing input k cap io . is free. An op that receives a capability and an op that does not have the same signature at the C boundary — a capability asks “is there the right to call this op” at translation, and nothing is left to ask at execution. The point of this design is that the cost of guarding the boundary does not come back as a run-time cost. “Making it safe makes it slow” is not true here.

6 Effects and capabilities are a pair. The effects line writes what the op does, and the capability inputs write who permitted it. The same thing is written from two sides, and each checks the other:

  • The work written in the effects line shall receive as an input the capability that permits it (refused if absent).
  • If the body does work that a capability opens, that effect shall be written in the effects line (E-EFFECT if absent).
  • Merely receiving a capability does not create an effect — a capability received and not used does no work.

Table 50 — Effects and the capabilities that permit them

EffectPermitting capabilityWhen absent
iocap io · cap file_system · cap net · cap tty · cap clock · cap randomE-EFFECT-NO-CAP
alloccap allocator (or a lexical region block)E-ALLOC-NOCAP
heapcap heapE-HEAP-NOCAP
atomiccap atomicE-ATOMIC-NOCAP
devicecap mmio — at the place that actually touches registersE-MMIO-NOCAP
state · panic · wait · concurrent— written without a capability—

In plain words

They are a pair so that one line of signature answers two questions. From effects io alone one knows that “it talks to the outside” but not with what. input fs cap file_system . narrows that — it can open files but cannot open connections. Conversely, with only a capability and no effects line, the caller cannot know whether that op is pure.

Example — it can be produced only with a capability received

Source

module ex_io .

use stdio .

rem To output, `cap io` shall be received as an argument.
proc main input out cap io . output u8 . effects io .
do
  let n usize stdio.write out. 1 "hello\n" . .
  return cast u8 n. . .
end .

Rejected example — without a capability not one byte can be put out

Source

module ex_io_bad .

use stdio .

proc main output u8 . effects io .
do
  let n u64 stdio.write 1 "hello\n" . .   rem no capability was received
  return narrow u8 n. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-EFFECT-NO-CAP

In plain words

The only difference between the two examples is one argument. The first received the output capability and the second did not. There is no hidden standard output — no function can put bytes outside without a capability.

6a There is no special notation for passing a capability on. The received name is written like any other argument.

7 An op that did not receive a capability cannot call an op that requires it. There is nothing to pass on.

Example — capabilities go down along the chain

Source

module ex_cap_chain .

use stdio .

rem The capability is received as an argument — its name is `k`, its type `cap io`.
proc say input k cap io . input msg slice u8 . output usize . effects io .
do
  return stdio.write k. 1 msg. . .
end .

rem The caller **just passes on by name** the `k` it received.
proc say_twice input k cap io . input msg slice u8 . output usize . effects io .
do
  let a usize say k. msg. . .
  let b usize say k. msg. . .
  return add a. b. . .
end .

rem The entry point receives the capability **from outside** — nobody can make one themselves.
proc main input k cap io . output u8 . effects io .
do
  let n usize say_twice k. "hi\n" . .
  guard eq n. 6 . else return 1 . .
  return 0 .
end .

In plain words

The chain looks like this — outside → k of main → k of say_twice → k of say → stdio.write → the builtin op write_out. An op that did not receive the capability cannot call say. There is nothing to pass on. That is, what guards the boundary is not grammar but arguments. There is no rule to memorise, and by looking at the call sites one can follow by eye where the right came from.

Note (informative)

The value of this rule lies in being able to answer “can this program touch files” by looking only at the entry point. No function deep inside can secretly open a file — without having received the capability it cannot open one, and if it did receive one, that chain is visible all the way to the entry point.

In plain words

It can be compared to a key. To enter a room there has to be a key, and a key exists only when someone hands it over. Nobody can make up a key when none was given.

7.3 What purity means§

1 An op that is effects none is pure. A pure op satisfies the following.

2 a) It always gives the same result for the same input.

3 b) Changing the order in which it is called does not change the result of the program.

4 c) If its result is not used, it need not be called.

5 A pure op cannot call an op that is not pure.

6 If an fn writes to storage it received as mut, that write is visible to the caller, so it is not pure (E-EFFECT-PURITY). Whether it writes directly, through a local name that sees that storage (rebound with subslice · view_array), or by passing it to a mut input of a proc, it is the same — if the receiver writes, the caller’s storage changes. Passing it to a mut input of an fn is not counted here, since that fn is checked by the same rule. Writing to storage built inside the op is not visible from outside, so it does not break purity.

Rejected example — an fn hands the mut piece it received to a proc to write

Source

module ex_purity_via .

proc put input out mut slice u8 . output u64 . effects none . do
  set idx out. 0 . 7 .
  return 0 .
end .

fn f input out mut slice u8 . output u64 . do
  return put out. . .     rem the caller's piece changes
end .

Diagnostic: E-EFFECT-PURITY

7.4 Where other languages are met§

1 Lowent can exchange with C. That boundary is marked with extern.

2 At the place that crosses the boundary, the language’s guarantees do not carry over by themselves. So at the boundary contracts shall be written, and the processor enforces those contracts at the boundary.

3 The names going out to the C side are decided by the author. The processor does not make up names — that name is a promise made to another language, so the one who promises shall write it.

4 The name on the C side is written with the clause link "<name>" .. This is the «name the author decides» that (3) speaks of, and the processor does not make it up from the op’s name.

5 An extern with the clause variadic . takes, after its fixed arguments, arguments whose number is not fixed. Contracts do not reach such arguments, so the caller is responsible for that place.

6 C can also be made to call Lowent. The address of an export extern op is obtained with unsafe_fn <op> and passed as a value.

7 At that place the direction of the boundary reverses. Handing it over is unsafe, since this language cannot know when, how many times, or in which flow it will be called, but the place where the call comes in is this side’s door, so that op’s contract is enforced on the arguments. That is, inside the door this language is responsible, outside the door C is.

Caution — A boundary is a door, not a hole

If the place where another language is met were left as “from here on there is no guarantee”, from that moment the guarantees of the whole program would become meaningless. Lowent sets up contracts at that place and makes people write what is promised to them and what they promise.

7.5 Doing things atomically§

1 Operations with the atomic effect are not split — another flow cannot see their middle. There are reading · writing · adding · bit operations · swapping (swap) · compare-and-swap.

1a The result of an atomic operation that gives a value is of the element type of that slot. A read gives the slot’s value, and add · subtract · bit operations · swap give the value before the change. Compare-and-swap (atomic_cas) gives 1 if it swapped and 0 otherwise, in that type.

1b Each machine has fixed widths for atomic operations done without a lock — the width for reads · writes and the width for read-modify-writes (add · subtract · bit operations · swap · compare-and-swap). If the element of the slot is wider than that, it is refused (E-ATOMIC-TARGET). In this processor cortex_m is 4 bytes for both, cortex_m0 is 4 bytes for reads · writes and has no read-modify-writes, and other machines go up to 8 bytes. The processor shows those numbers per machine.

2 atomic_fence computes no value and fixes only order — it makes what came before it visible before what comes after.

3 Atomic operations shall write their effect in the contract. Using them without writing it does not conform.

4 order <name> can be attached after an atomic operation to fix what is visible to other flows, and when. The names are the following five and no more; anything else is refused (E-ATOMIC-ORDER).

Table 51 — Memory ordering

NameMeaning
relaxedatomicity only, no order. Only that the value does not tear
acquirewhat comes after this read does not leak forward
releasewhat comes before this write does not leak backward
acq_relboth. Used for operations that read and write
seq_cstevery flow sees one and the same order. The strongest

5 If no order is written, it is taken as seq_cst. The strongest is the default because it is the easiest to reason about — weakening it is something done by someone who writes it knowing its value.

6 The orders that can be used differ by operation. Combinations without meaning are refused (E-ATOMIC-ORDER).

Table 52 — The orders each operation can use

OperationOrders that can be used
read (atomic_load)relaxed · acquire · seq_cst — a read cannot be release
write (atomic_store)relaxed · release · seq_cst — a write cannot be acquire
fence (atomic_fence)acquire · release · acq_rel · seq_cst — a fence with no order at all is not a fence, so relaxed cannot be used

In plain words

Why block combinations too? atomic_load … order release makes sense as a sentence but has no meaning — what would a read release? C left it as undefined behaviour, and undefined behaviour is silent. This language leaves nothing silent.

Note (informative)

Atomicity is not free. Using atomic operations on a value only one flow touches only makes that value slower. So this language does not make it the default but has it chosen by name — so that the cost is written in the signature.

7.6 Where devices are talked to§

1 Reading and writing device registers is not an ordinary memory access. It is written with read_volatile and write_volatile, and the processor shall not merge, remove or reorder those accesses.

2 These accesses require the device effect and the matching capability (§7.2).

3 Reading or writing that place like an ordinary field is refused (E-MMIO-PLAIN) — that is, reading with field <aggregate> <register> or writing with set field <aggregate>. <register> . <value> .. An ordinary field access is an operation the processor may merge or remove, so a device access written in that spelling cannot keep what (1) requires. If there are two ways of writing the same meaning and only one keeps that promise, the other is not a way but a trap.

Rejected example — touching a device register like an ordinary field

Source

module ex_mmio_plain .

build tier t1 .

struct gpio do
  mmio 0x40020000 .
  moder u32 rw .
end .

unsafe proc drive input dev cap mmio . input regs mut slice u8 . output u32 . effects device unsafe .
do
  let g mut view gpio view gpio regs. . .
  set field g. moder . 2 .       rem an ordinary field write — an operation the processor may remove
  return 0 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-MMIO-PLAIN

Note (informative)

The purpose of an ordinary read is “knowing the value”, so the processor may remove a second read. For a device register the act of reading is itself the work — reading sometimes changes the state. So it must not be removed, and the language has to know that.

7.6.1 Register blocks — mmio§

1 A device’s registers are written as an aggregate, and mmio <start address> . is attached to that aggregate to declare it a map of the device. The start address shall be an integer (E-MMIO-BASE) — a map has a start.

2 Opening that place without a map is refused (E-MMIO-NOBASE).

3 The address of a register block comes from a capability. Only an op holding cap mmio reaches that place (§7.2), and the start address is read as an address within the range that capability permits. On a machine whose pointers are capabilities (§5.3 (4a)), the block’s pointer is derived from the capability received by the entry point — no pointer is made from an integer address alone. On a machine without such capabilities, the capability is only a mark, and the meaning is the same.

Table 53 — Places where a register block is refused

DiagnosticWhat went wrong
E-MMIO-BASEthe start address is not an integer
E-MMIO-NOBASEtrying to open at its own address an aggregate that did not declare mmio
E-MMIO-FIELDa register is not of a shape the machine can reach at once — its width shall be 1 · 2 · 4 · 8 and it shall be in its own place
E-MMIO-BYVALUEtrying to receive a register block by value. Receiving by value is copying, and a device cannot be copied
E-MMIO-PERMtrying to write a read-only (ro) register
E-MMIO-PLAINreading or writing a register like an ordinary field — it shall be written with read_volatile · write_volatile
E-MMIO-NOCAP · E-MMIO-NOEFFECTthe capability (cap mmio) or the effect (device) is missing
E-MMIO-NOHOSTtrying to open an absolute address on a machine with an operating system — that address is not a device

In plain words

That a device cannot be received by value is the place in this clause people run into most often. Passing an aggregate by value is copying, and writing to the copy does not reach the device. A program written that way does nothing while saying nothing — so it is stopped at translation.

7.7 Where interrupts are received§

1 Attaching a vector <number> . clause to an op makes that op an interrupt handler. To write its urgency as well, attach priority <number> .. No new word is introduced — clauses do that work.

2 An interrupt handler is called by the machine. So it shall keep the following four.

Table 54 — What an interrupt handler shall keep

To keepIf violatedWhy
nobody calls itE-ISR-CALLEDif our code calls it, the interrupt runs in a wrong state, on the wrong stack and at the wrong priority
no parametersE-ISR-PARAMSthe machine passes no arguments
returns nothingE-ISR-OUTPUTthere is nobody to return to — the machine woke it, and the machine resumes what was interrupted
writes effects deviceE-ISR-EFFECTit is a place that exists because of a device

3 Shared state passes between interrupt handlers and ordinary code by a discipline of priorities and queues. That discipline is not fixed by this document; it is the work of libraries (§9.5).

7.2.1 Where capabilities mismatch§

1 Capabilities are judged by kind. Receiving a capability of another kind does not open that door (E-CAP-KIND). Not having one but what it is makes the authority.

1a Core ops and builtin ops that require a capability (input/output · terminals · clocks · random numbers · files · the network · the environment · arguments) write that capability as their first operand. Not writing it does not conform (E-CAP-MISSING) — merely having received the capability does not let it be used, and there is no way to reach a capability without a name.

1b The name written in that place is the name of a capability this op received as an input. A capability is not moved around like a value but handed from head line to head line (§7.2). So a line that binds a capability to a local name (let · var written with cap <kind>) does not conform by itself (E-CAP-LOCAL) — whether or not that name is used.

1c The same holds at the place that calls an op taking a capability as an input.
