The name written in that op’s capability position shall be the name of a capability the calling op received as an input (E-CAP-LOCAL), the kind shall be the same (E-CAP-KIND), and an op whose first input is a capability cannot be called without a capability (E-CAP-MISSING). Builtin ops that require capabilities are wrapped and provided by the standard library (§9.3 (1a)), so the place where an application program meets this rule is usually the line that calls that wrapper.

1d An op does not give out capabilities. An op that writes cap <kind> in its output clause — as it is, or inside another type such as option — does not conform (E-CAP-OUTPUT). An op that returns a capability becomes a second source invisible in the caller’s head line. Spawning an actor with capability fields is specified separately by §8.6 (E-CAP-FORGE).

Rejected example — binding a received capability to a local name

Source

module ex_cap_local .

proc main input out cap io . output u8 . effects none .
do
  let k cap io out. .   rem this line is refused even if it is not used
  return 0 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-CAP-LOCAL

Rejected example — an op that gives out a capability

Source

module ex_cap_output .

fn screen input out cap io . output cap io .
do
  return out. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-CAP-OUTPUT

2 Requiring a capability the machine being built for cannot give is refused (E-CAP-NOHOST).

3 An op that writes the alloc effect and does not receive a capability to obtain it is refused (E-ALLOC-NOCAP). It wrote that it would use the heap but not where that place is received from. The capability is a cap allocator input, a region parameter, or a region block of a kind other than heap.

3a The heap effect is the same: written without a cap heap input or a region <name> heap block, it is refused (E-HEAP-NOCAP). cap allocator is the capability of a fixed window and does not authorise growing.

3b On a machine without an operating system (machine.no_heap), a growing root cannot be requested from any place — the heap effect, a cap heap input and a region <name> heap block are all refused (E-HEAP-NOHOST). On the same machine, alloc carving a fixed window can be used.

4 An op marked unsafe that does not write the unsafe effect is refused (E-UNSAFE-UNUSED) — the mark has meaning only when it reaches the caller, and what carries it there is the effects line. Conversely, doing such work without the effect is refused (E-UNSAFE-UNDECLARED).

4a unsafe spreads all the way along calls. There is only one place where it stops: an op that writes the clause absorbs machine <name> .. That op can call unsafe things in its body and does not carry it in its own effects line. And <name> is a cap machine inside that body — that is where that capability is born. No other kind can be absorbed (E-ABSORB-SCOPE): making up a capability that reaches the world would create an authority the caller cannot see.

4b An absorbing op shall have all of the following; if even one is missing, it is refused.

Table 55 — What absorbing requires

To haveDiagnosticWhy
an effects line of noneE-ABSORB-IMPUREabsorbing stops only unsafe — the caller has to know about other effects
reference <op> .E-ABSORB-NOREFwithout a pure version that gives the same answer, there is no way to tell «correct» from «consistently wrong»
one or more requiresE-ABSORB-NOCONTRACTthe inside cannot be seen but the door can — length · alignment · range are checked at the door
why "…" .E-ABSORB-NOWHYa promise whose assumptions are not written can be confirmed by nobody
permission in the manifestE-ABSORB-PLACEwho may vouch is decided not by the source that wants that right but by the project’s identity file

4b′ The manifest writes by name the modules permitted to absorb. A module not written there that absorbs is refused, and if there is no manifest at all, nobody can absorb — the default is no permission.

4c The processor shall be able to say where absorbing happened — which op absorbed what, and on what ground, one line each. A zone it cannot see is not hidden but left visible.

4d Absorbing is a choice, not a duty. An op that does not absorb passes unsafe on as now.

In plain words

Spreading is right — if the caller does not know what the processor cannot see, the effects line becomes a lie. But without a place where it stops, that door becomes a door nobody can use (measured 2026-09-23: uses of asm in the standard library, 0). So a place to stop is opened narrowly and checkably. The tool lists those places, and the ledger holds the signature and the hash of the body — what people agree to keep is either counted or disappears.

Rejected example — saying it will use the heap without receiving that capability

Source

module ex_alloc_nocap .

proc f output u8 . effects alloc .    rem there is no `input k cap allocator .`
do
  return 1 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-ALLOC-NOCAP

7.2.2 Where things come in and where they go out§

1 The op at which a program starts receives only capabilities (E-ENTRY-PARAMS). It receives no data — there is nobody to hand it over.

2 The capabilities the starting place can receive are a closed row — args · env · io · allocator · heap · file_system · net · process · tty · clock · random · atomic. (process — the right to start other programs and watch them.) If the starting place requires any other capability, it is refused (E-ENTRY-CAP) — writing a right nobody can give is decoration, and decoration is a lie.

In plain words

Why only seven? The capabilities of the starting place shall be what the executing side can actually hand over. Capabilities not on the list (for example atomics · machine instructions · devices) cannot be made up by the program itself, nor are they something the executing side can hand over — such capabilities shall be made at a place entitled to give them and flow as arguments. Devices (mmio) and machine instructions (machine) are such, and so is the door into C (c).

3 Names of other modules can be used only if that side exported them (E-VISIBILITY). Writing the module in front of a name does not create an authority that was not there.

In plain words

The one thing these clauses repeat — capabilities are handed over, not picked up. The heap, the door into C, machine instructions, devices and the starting place are all the same. A power whose source is not written does not exist in this language.

8 Memory and ownership§

1 This clause specifies where values live and when they disappear. Lowent has no garbage collector, and none of the mistakes that come from releasing by hand — in place of both there are translation-time rules.

8.1 Where values live§

1 A stored value lives in some region. The region is fixed together with the type and is written in the source.

1a Not every value has a region. A small value that exists only during a computation may have no place to be stored, and such a value has no region. A region says where a value is stored, not how a value exists.

2 The regions of stored values are of three kinds.

Table 56 — Where values live

KindExplanation
localborn inside an op and gone when the op ends. The most common
staticexists for as long as the program lives
obtainedobtained from a root. Who gives it back is decided by ownership (§8.4)

2a There are two roots to obtain from. They are carved separately and rewound separately.

Table 57 — The two roots

RootCarved withEffectProperty
fixed windowcap allocator · regions other than heapallocdoes not grow. When used up it gives none. On a machine without an operating system it lies between two bounds the linker gives — the size is not fixed into the executable, and that place is written when the device boots
heapcap heap · region <name> heapheapgrows. More is fetched during execution, and bytes already given are not moved. Exists only on machines with an operating system

3 Which region a value lives in is written in the source. The processor does not move it secretly.

3a Apart from what is obtained from roots, the values that some · ok · spawn actor make live in a finite pool of the processor (the place and lifetime of element lists lit array · lit slice are specified by §6.2.6 (7), and the place of struct value lits by §6.2.6 (7c-1) — they live in the op’s frame, and when they go into the load of some · ok · a container · a channel · a letter · a task they are copied as values and live in the pool). This pool is the op’s implicit ⟦frame⟧ — it has no effect, writes no alloc · heap, and needs no capability. The pool is rewound on every iteration of a loop, and if the values alive at the same time exceed the size of the pool, it stops on the spot (not a value but a halt). The size is decided by the machine (small on machines without an operating system), and the person building can adjust it. This size is not normative but a fact the processor states.

3a-1 For each entry op, the processor counts at translation the upper bound of values the deepest call path can leave in the pool — it adds up the number of places on that path that make values (a place inside a loop counts one iteration’s worth · struct values living in frames are not counted). For paths that cannot be counted (recursion · piling up in fields · loops that cannot be rewound), it states the reason instead of a number. If the counted bound is larger than that machine’s pool, the program does not conform (E-POOL-BOUND). This count is shown by --stack-report (the same place as the frame count of §6.2.6 (7c)).

3b A root’s cursor is not shared between flows of execution. So an op spawned as a task cannot obtain from a root (E-ALLOC-TASK).

3c One allocator being placed in two or more flows at the same time at one place does not conform (E-ALLOC-SHARED) — handing it to two tasks, or handing it to a task while using it alongside. If its reserve moves the cursor atomically (the atomic effect), it is not so. Giving each task its own allocator conforms — there is one cursor each, so there is nothing to compete over.

In plain words

Until 2026-09-16, (3c) blocked «handing an allocator to a task» itself. So doing exactly what the diagnostic recommended — «give each task its own allocator» — was refused with the same diagnostic (defect note 75). If the way a diagnostic recommends is blocked, that diagnostic closes a way rather than opening one.

In plain words

What are commonly called «the stack» and «the heap» correspond to local and obtained. The names differ because in this language they are not machine structures but properties of values.

8.2 Places that receive regions§

1 Regions are handed over as arguments. It is written input <name> region <type> . ., and the type after it is the name by which that region is called.

2 An op that obtains places from a region has the alloc effect (§7.1). That is, there is no op that allocates secretly — an op that uses places has that written in its contract.

3 What is obtained from a region cannot outlive that region. When the region ends, what was obtained from it ends too.

3a A region is not given back piece by piece. It is collected all at once when the region ends.

Example — receiving a region and obtaining places

Source

module ex_region .

type scratch u64 .

proc build input temp region scratch . output u64 . effects alloc .
do
  let s stack u64 stack_new temp capacity 4 . .
  push s. 10 .
  push s. 20 .
  return 2 .
end .

In plain words

Note that effects alloc is written. Obtaining places is an effect, so a pure op (fn, effects none) cannot use regions. It means that where memory comes from is written in the signature.

4 The statement that opens a region writes its name together with its kind: region <name> <kind> do … end .. The kinds are a closed eight — stack · frame · arena · static · heap · mmap · disk · device. Any other word does not conform (E-REGION-KIND).

4a Carrying out of a region a value obtained inside it does not conform (E-REGION-ESCAPE). When the region closes, that value no longer exists, so a name left outside points at what does not exist. The places of carrying out are return, assigning to a name outside the region, and assigning to a field or element of a name outside the region (set field h. store . b. .). Values that do not hold the region’s bytes — those bound to scalar types like integers · truth values, and expressions that give scalars like len · idx — are not carried out. Whether something holds the region’s bytes is decided by following the flow: the result of an op call holds only what belongs to the inputs that flow into the result in that op’s body. If the result is an aggregate built with lit, each field is decided separately — taking out and carrying away a field that holds the region’s bytes does not conform, and fields that do not hold them do not matter. If the flow cannot be traced, as when the body puts the result into a local and then returns it, it is regarded as coming from all the inputs.

Rejected example — putting a slice of the region into a field of an outer aggregate

Source

module ex_region_field .

struct holder do store mut slice u8 . end .

proc f output u64 . effects alloc . do
  var h lit holder do store subslice "abcd" 0 0 . . end . .
  region r arena do
    let g option mut slice u8 alloc_bytes r. capacity 16 . .
    if is_some g. . do
      let b mut slice u8 some_value g. . .
      set field h. store . b. .
    end .
  end .
  return len field h. store . . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-REGION-ESCAPE

4b Opening a region with a name that is not a region parameter of that op does not conform either (E-REGION-UNDEF).

4c Carving with the name of an outer source while a region of the same root (§8.1 (2a)) is open inside does not conform (E-ALLOC-NESTED). This holds for the name of an outer region block, for a region parameter, and for a capability of the same root (cap allocator for the fixed window · cap heap for the heap). Each root has one carving place, so when the inner region ends, those bytes are rewound too. A region of another root being open inside does not matter.

Diagram — one cursor per root — when the inner region ends, the cursor is rewound

            ▼ where outer was opened
                        ▼ where inner was opened
            [ a ][ ··· ][ b ][ ✘ c ]
                        ▲ when inner ends, the cursor is rewound to here → c disappears with it
   a = outer's · b = inner's · c = carved with outer's name while inner was open (✘ E-ALLOC-NESTED)

Rejected example — carving from the outer region while the inner region is open

Source

module ex_region_nested .

proc f output u64 . effects alloc . do
  region outer arena do
    region inner arena do
      let g option mut slice u8 alloc_bytes outer. capacity 8 . .
    end .
  end .
  return 0 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-ALLOC-NESTED

4d Sending bytes obtained inside a region to an actor born outside the region does not conform (E-ALLOC-OUTLIVES). The actor outlives the region, and whether it keeps the slice it received is not visible at translation. Make the actor inside the region, or hand over memory that outlives the region.

4e On every path that leaves a region block — reaching the end, return, break · continue of a loop enclosing the region — that region is rewound. If the name of a region block is passed as an argument, the receiver takes it as a region parameter and carves from the same root.

5 The kind decides which root it carves from (§8.1 (2a)): heap carves from the heap, and the other seven carve from and rewind the fixed window. Regions of the two roots can be opened overlapping within one op, and rewinding the inner region leaves outer values of the other root in place. The processor of this edition does not treat the seven other than heap differently from one another — those seven are words that tell the reader where the storage comes from, and having the processor distinguish them is the work of a later edition.

Note (informative)

Closing the vocabulary and separating behaviour are different things. If any word could be used, that word would say nothing — because it was closed at eight, someone who reads region t arena knows “it is bump allocation and is given back all at once”. The distinction of behaviour goes as far as the two roots (fixed window · heap), and there is no distinction yet among the other seven. That fact is written down here.

8.3 Moving and copying§

1 When a value is put into another name, if that value has ownership it is moved (the original name can no longer be used). If it is a value without ownership, it is copied.

2 Using the original name after it has been moved is refused at translation.

8.3.1 Where a value is put — temporaries and destinations§

1 Sometimes an expression that makes a value has a place where it will be put (a destination). The initial value of let, the value of return, argument positions and the fields of aggregates are such.

2 If all of the following three hold, that value is made directly in the destination — neither an intermediate temporary nor moving it happens.

Table 58 — The three conditions for being made directly

ConditionMeaning
the destination is emptylet initialisation · a field not yet filled · a place emptied by a move
the value does not fail midwaythere is no path that leaves before reaching the destination
the destination belongs to that value alonetwo values do not compete for the same place

3 If the conditions do not hold, a temporary is made. A temporary is put in the smallest enclosing scope, and when that scope ends the temporaries disappear in the reverse order of their making.

4 A reference pointing at a temporary cannot go outside that scope (§8.4.1).

5 There is no separate operation that moves a value. Moving is changing places, not making a value.

Note (informative)

Why write «made directly» as a norm? Left as “do it if possible”, the same source would copy on some processors and not on others — then the cost would not be visible in the source. The three conditions above are things a reader can count from the source alone, so when they hold one may believe there is no copy.

Note (informative)

C++ solves this place with «value categories» and «move constructors». Lowent does not bring in that vocabulary — moving is the business of places, not of types. So there is no special declaration for moving, nor any rule that calls one.

8.4 References§

1 A reference points at a value somewhere else. There are two kinds.

shared reference (ref)

A read reference. Several can hold one at the same time. A value cannot be changed through it.

exclusive reference (mut_ref)

A write reference. For one value there can be only one at a time, and meanwhile there can be no read references either.

2 Violating this rule is refused at translation.

3 A reference is not a value. Using a name that received a reference as it is in arithmetic or comparison does not conform (E-TYPE-REFVAL) — to use the value pointed at, take it out in writing with deref.

4 This edition has no notation for borrowing a field of a struct separately. Writing a field borrow does not conform (E-BORROW-FIELD) — borrowing takes the whole value as its unit (§8.12).

Rejected example — using a reference like a value in arithmetic

Source

module ex_ref_value .

fn twice input p ref u64 . output u64 .
do
  return add p. p. . .    rem the value pointed at is taken out with `deref p`
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-REFVAL

8.4.1 A reference cannot outlive what it points at§

1 A reference pointing at a local value remaining after that value has disappeared is called an escape. Lowent refuses escapes at translation time.

2 That is, a reference pointing at a value made inside an op cannot be returned outside that op.

Example — several can hold read references together

Source

module ex_ref .

export fn sum_two input a ref u32 . input b ref u32 . output u32 .
  requires le deref a. . 1000 . .
  requires le deref b. . 1000 . .
do
  return add deref a. . deref b. . . .
end .

Rejected example — a reference pointing at a local value cannot be returned

Source

module ex_escape .

export fn leak output ref u32 .
do
  let here u32 42 .
  return ref here . .     rem `here` disappears when this op ends
end .

Diagnostic: E-ESCAPE: reference to a local escapes the op (dangling)

In plain words

If the code above were translated, the returned reference would point at a value that has already disappeared. If that reference were read, nobody knows what value would come out. So this language does not accept that code at all — it is known at translation, not by running it and finding out.

Caution — There are no null references

A reference always points at a live value. If “pointing at nothing” has to be expressed, use option (§6.2.8) — then the processor enforces a check before taking it out.

Note (informative)

The rule that separates many readers from one writer prevents two things at once. One is another flow changing it at the same time, and the other is the value changing while being read. Both are kinds of mistake that cannot be known by reading the source, so they are removed by rule.

8.5 Ownership and destroying§

1 ownership says who is responsible for destroying a value. owned t is a value that has ownership.

2 A value with ownership shall be destroyed exactly once. Destroying it twice, and discarding it without destroying it, are both refused at translation time.

Diagram — ownership moves around — the responsibility to destroy is always in one place

 var h be owned buffer v .      h ──▶ [ buffer ]            responsibility: here
 consume h .                    h (empty name)  ──move──▶ h of consume ──▶ [ buffer ]   responsibility: consume
 drop h .   (inside consume)                                  [ buffer ] gone          responsibility: ended
 using h again                  E-OWN-MOVED                 (use after move)

3 Destroying a value is of two kinds, and this language treats the two differently.

release (해제)

Work that always succeeds and does not wait, like giving memory back. It has no failure, so it may happen silently where the value’s lifetime ends. There is no failure to swallow.

completion (완결)

Work that can fail and can wait, like closing a file · flushing a buffer. If it happened silently, there would be no place to hand that failure to. So the author shall always call it in writing.

4 Whether a value requires completion is decided by the program’s own declarations — if there is an op that takes that type by value and returns a result, that is the declaration “ending this can fail”.

5 Discarding without calling it a value that needs completion does not conform. The processor issues a diagnostic (E-OWN-INCOMPLETE).

6 Using a moved value again does not conform either (E-OWN-MOVED).

7 When binding a value that needs completion, owned shall be written in that place (E-OWN-BARE). Without that word nothing requires ending, and what the type says and what the binding says part.

8 At a place where branches split and meet again, the state of ownership shall be the same on every path (E-OWN-JOIN). If it is destroyed on one path and kept alive on another, nobody can say whether that value is alive after they meet.

8a A path that leaves a loop is a path too. A path that leaves a loop with break meets the ordinary end after that loop — if the value was moved on only one side, it is refused (E-OWN-JOIN). A break with a number meets after the loop that many levels outward. A while whose condition is true ends only with break, so there is no path to meet — the state of that path is the state after. Going to the next iteration with continue after moving a value is refused, since the next iteration meets the moved value again (E-OWN-MOVED).

9 After moving one owned field of an aggregate, moving the whole aggregate again does not conform (E-OWN-PARTIAL). The receiver believes it received a whole aggregate, but one field inside it already belongs to someone else.

In plain words

These three are all different faces of the same question — that “who is responsible for destroying this value now” shall always have one answer. If the answer differs by branch (8), or only part has moved (9), or the question was never asked (7), the end of that value becomes a place nobody is responsible for.

Example — release — it cannot fail, so drop is enough

Source

module ex_own .

type buffer u8 .

fn sink input h owned buffer . output u8 .
do
  drop h. .
  return 0 .
end .

Rejected example — it cannot be destroyed twice

Source

module ex_own_bad .

type buffer u8 .

fn twice input h owned buffer . output u8 .
do
  drop h. .
  drop h. .     rem destroys again what is already gone
  return 0 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-OWN-MOVED

In plain words

It matters that there is only one criterion separating the two kinds — can ending it fail. If it cannot fail, no information is lost when the language cleans up by itself. If it can fail, someone has to receive that failure, and the only one who can is the author.

Note (informative)

It is also worth noticing that no new word was made. Completion is an ordinary op — a function that takes an owned value and returns a result. That shape itself becomes the declaration, so there was no reason to add a word to the language.

In plain words

Catching «forgot to destroy» at translation time is the core of this clause. It does not expect the operating system to clean up when the program ends, so the same code holds even in an environment without an operating system (§5.1).

self-owning node (스스로를 품는 마디)

A struct one of whose fields is option (owned <its own type>). That field is not a value but one address slot of another node built in allocator bytes, so the size is fixed, and nodes are linked to build trees.

10 A self-owning node is a struct with a field <name> option (owned <itself>) . (§6.2.7 (3b)). Nodes are built only in bytes an allocator gave — the binding’s use chooses that allocator: let n use al. option (owned node) lit node do … end . .. If there is not enough room, the value is none. Nodes are treated like values that need completion — discarding an owned node · option (owned node) without moving or returning it (end of block · an early return), or overwriting a value still held, does not conform (E-OWN-INCOMPLETE).

11 A node is given back only with drop <name>. use <allocator>. .. If that allocator gives back piece by piece (freeing_allocator), the bytes go back to it; otherwise the allocator keeps holding the bytes. If the name is an option and empty, it does nothing. dropping a node without an allocator, or writing use on a value that is not a node, does not conform (E-DROP-USE).

12 Giving back a node whose child fields still hold something does not conform (E-OWN-NODE-KIDS) — move the fields out first. If that node is inside an option and not visible at translation, execution stops on the spot (E-VM-NODE-KIDS). Deep trees are torn down with loops, not recursion.

13 Taking a child out of a node borrowed with ref does not conform, and a child taken out of a node borrowed with mut_ref shall be put back before the op ends (E-OWN-NODE-REF) — the lender gets back a node with a hole. Only reading (is_some field t. left .) is not taking out. Moving as a whole a node whose child was taken out does not conform, as in (9) (E-OWN-PARTIAL).

Example — a borrowed node is only read

Source

module ex_node_read .

struct node do
  key u64 .
  left option (owned node) .
end .

fn has_left input t ref node . output bool . do
  return is_some field t. left . . .
end .

Rejected example — a node is given back to its allocator

Source

module ex_node_drop .

struct node do
  key u64 .
  left option (owned node) .
end .

fn lose input n owned node . output u64 . do
  drop n. .
  return 0 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-DROP-USE

In plain words

Nodes do not hold their allocator because it would be waste — the head of a tree holds one allocator. So a node does not know «where it came from», and the one who writes where to give it back is the author. If the node type is not exported from its module, the mistake of giving one tree’s nodes back to another allocator is confined to that module (the standard library tree).

8.6 How far memory safety is guaranteed§

1 This document writes the strength of its guarantees separately. Otherwise it would make promises it could not keep.

Table 59 — Strengths of guarantee

StrengthMeaning
provedthere is a machine-checked proof
staticprevented by translation-time rules. There is no proof of the rules themselves yet
dynamicchecked during execution. Violations trap

2 The exclusivity rule of references, the ban on escapes, and destroying owned values once are static.

3 Slice bounds and contracts are dynamic, but checks disappear when the processor proves them (§6.4.6).

Caution — The word «safe» means something only when it says what is safe and how

When a language claims “memory safety”, it matters whether that is prevented at translation time or checked during execution, and whether it is proved or only a rule. This document writes the strengths separately so as not to make promises it cannot keep.

8.7 Reading bytes again — bit_cast and view§

1 Reading the same bytes as another type is divided into two kinds in this language. They are divided because if that work were put off wholesale as «unsafe», nobody could say how far the harm spreads.

Table 60 — The two kinds of reading bytes again

KindopWhat it is
moving a value’s bits as they arebit_castbetween two scalars of the same width. The bit pattern is left as it is and only the way of reading changes
laying a shape over bytesview · try_view · view_arrayreads a piece of bytes with the layout of some shape. It does not copy

8.7.1 bit_cast — the bits as they are, only the way of reading§

2 The target type of bit_cast shall be a plain scalar. plain means a type in which every bit pattern is a useful value and there is no padding.

3 bool and enum are not plain — because they have useless bit patterns (trap
representations). So these two are outside this kind and are refused (E-TYPE-BITCAST).

Caution —

«Is it plain» shall be asked directly. A way that asks indirectly, treating what is not in the width table as not plain, silently flips the day the width table is filled. A verdict that held by coincidence disappears without a word when that coincidence disappears.

Rejected example — bool has trap representations, so it is not plain

Source

module ex_bitcast .

fn f input a u8 . output bool .
do
  return bit_cast bool a. . .     rem not every bit pattern is true or false
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-BITCAST

8.7.2 view — laying a layout on§

4 view lays the layout of a shape over a piece of bytes. It does not copy — it reads the bytes in that place as they are.

5 Two things shall fit for it to be laid on.

Table 61 — The conditions for view to stand

ConditionIf violatedWhy
the piece is as long as the layoutE-VM-VIEWreading missing bytes is exactly what this language prevents
the start address keeps alignE-VM-ALIGNalign n is a contract, and this boundary is exactly where the contract is checked

6 try_view does the same, but if it cannot stand it gives absence instead of a lie (⟦option⟧). That is, the failure comes back as a value.

7 view_array lays several of the same shape on. The conditions are the same.

7a Row views — view_array array m t b. lays one row (m × |t| bytes) on as one element. The result is slice array m t (the same type as the sequence of rows of §6.2 (9a)), and idx g. r. is a slice t that sees row r. The conditions are the same as (7) — if the length of b is not a multiple of the row bytes it stops (E-VM-VIEW), t shall be plain (bool is E-TYPE-BITCAST), and m is a literal of 1 or more. Whether it can be written follows b, and its source is b (§8 (8a)) — it does not copy, so the rules of invalidation · escape stand as those of b.

In plain words

If alignment is treated as «a matter of speed», places that break it pass silently and one day collapse only on some machine. This language calls it a contract and checks it at the boundary — it refuses misaligned addresses for exactly the same reason it refuses short pieces. Both are «reading what is not there as if it were».

8.8 Changeability flows only as written§

1 Pieces and references are read by default. To change them, mut shall be written in that place.

Table 62 — Places where changeability does not match

DiagnosticWhat went wrong
E-TYPE-MUTwriting to an element of a piece not written mut
E-TYPE-REFwriting through a shared read reference — to change it, it shall be mut_ref
E-TYPE-ARGMUTpassing a read-only value to a place that takes mut · owned · mut_ref
E-TYPE-RETMUTwriting that it gives something changeable and returning something read-only

2 That is, changeability narrows in one direction only. Passing something changeable as read is allowed, but passing something read-only to a changeable place is not.

In plain words

What this rule protects is the reader’s trust. If I received a value as read, I shall be able to trust that it does not change while I am looking at it, and that trust stands only when nobody secretly obtains the right to write. So there is only one direction of narrowing.

8.9 Same name or not, types differ§

1 Even with the same representation, different names are different types (E-TYPE-NOMINAL). Those separated with newtype, and different integer types of the same size, are such.

2 An undeclared type name is refused (E-TYPE-UNDEF).

3 Integers and floating-point numbers do not mix by themselves (E-TYPE-MIX). To cross, use the op written for it.

4 A type holding itself is refused (E-TYPE-CYCLE). Even when the processor cannot follow that recursion to the end, it does not pass silently but says so (E-TYPE-CYCLE-LIMIT).

Rejected example — integers and floating point do not mix by themselves

Source

module ex_mix .

fn f input a u32 . input b f64 . output f64 .
do
  return add a. b. . .     rem to cross, use the op written for it
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-MIX

8.10 Storage the linker gives — storage reserved§

1 A storage reserved . block opens static storage that the linker sets aside for that type. It is not obtained by the program during execution; its place is already fixed when it is built.

2 An op that opens this block shall have the following three.

Table 63 — What storage reserved shall have

To haveIf missingWhy
a capabilityE-RESERVE-NOCAPa place the linker hands over is also handed over, not picked up
effects stateE-RESERVE-NOEFFECTit is a place that remains while the program lives, so touching it is touching state
the machine can give itE-RESERVE-NOHOSTon an operating system, the linker does not give that place that way

8.11 Parentheses do not cross blocks§

1 An opened ( shall be closed within the same block. If the block ends while it is unclosed, it is refused (E-PAREN-ESCAPE), and if it is never closed, it is refused (E-PAREN-UNCLOSED).

2 A ) without a partner, and a ) that looks for its partner across a block boundary, are refused (E-PAREN-STRAY).

In plain words

If parentheses could look for their partners across blocks, a source missing one parenthesis could be read wholly with another meaning. Then the processor would build a different program instead of reporting the mistake. If they cannot cross the boundary, an omission always shows as an omission.

Rejected example — the parenthesis is not closed before the block ends

Source

module ex_paren .

fn f output u8 .
do
  return (add 1 2 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-PAREN-ESCAPE

8.12 Exclusivity — many readers or one writer§

1 The ways that reach a value at one moment are many reads or one write, and those two overlapping does not conform (E-EXCL). If they overlapped, nobody could say when the value the reader sees changes.

Diagram — borrows of one value n, in time order

 time →          ①        ②        ③        ④        ⑤
 ref r1 n     ├─────────────────┤                          two reads may overlap
 ref r2 n              ├─────────────────┤
 mut_ref w n                                   ├────────┤  a write only when alone
 ✘ ref r n    ├──────────────────────────┤
   set n 5                   ●                             the value changes while being read  E-EXCL

2 What is borrowed cannot outlive the value it was borrowed from. If the lent value has been moved while the borrow remains, it does not conform (E-EXCL-MOVED).

3 A borrow cannot go outside the block that opened it (E-BORROW-ESCAPE).

4 Holding something borrowed from an actor and talking to that actor again does not conform (E-BORROW-EXCL) — while that actor changes its own state, we are looking at that state.

5 A name bound with let cannot be changed (E-IMMUTABLE). Bind a name to be changed with var.

6 One call passing the same storage to two or more mut positions does not conform (E-EXCL). There are two writers, so it violates (1) directly. Which write remains is decided not by the calling place but by the order of the callee’s statements, and the caller’s text has no way of knowing it.

6a Whether it is the same storage is decided not by name but by place: an alias of one name, overlapping pieces cut from the same storage, the same field of the same value, and the whole of a value and its field are the same storage. Different fields of one value (field s. a . and field s. b .) are different storage — passing a field to a mut position passes that field’s place, so two different fields can be passed together to two write positions.

7 Passing the same storage together to one mut position and a reading position (an in-place operation) conforms only when the callee has declared the pair of those two inputs with the clause inplace <write input> <read input> . (§6.4.1 (3a)) and the two storages are the same range (E-EXCL-INPLACE). The same range means the whole of the same storage, or pieces cut from the same storage with the same start and the same end. If the ranges overlap out of step or one is part of the other (partial overlap), it does not conform even with the declaration — a body that reads and writes element by element is correct only on the same range, and the caller cannot know where in the storage the callee writes.

7a The inplace clause writes two names: the first is a mut input of that op, the second another input of that op. One clause is written per pair. A mismatched shape is E-INPLACE-FORM. Whether that declaration is true — whether the body runs correctly on the same range — shall be shown by one of two shapes of the body: ⓐ it does not write to the write input up to the last statement that reads the read input, or ⓑ within that last statement it writes only with one index V as set idx <write>. V. . and reads only with the same index as idx <read>. V. ., and, inside a loop, does not read after writing in the same iteration (in place, element by element). Passing the same pair to an op that declared it inplace is left to that op’s declaration. If it is not shown by either shape, it is E-INPLACE-UNPROVEN. This check is conservative — it may refuse a correct body but never admits a wrong one, and a refused body is rewritten as ⓐ or ⓑ.

7b When whether two ranges overlap cannot be decided at translation (when a cut end is not a constant), the processor asks (6) · (7) during execution. On entering, an op looks, for each pair of slice · view parameters — at least one of them mut — at whether the two pieces overlap by even one byte, and stops if they do (E-VM-EXCL; natively a panic with the same sentence). It passes if that op allowed the write · read pair with inplace and the two pieces are the same range. An empty piece overlaps nothing. Overlap is decided by byte places, so two windows with different element widths overlap too if they see the same bytes. Calls already decided at translation (constant ranges) are refused with the diagnostics of (6) · (7), and this check does not change the answers of calls that do not overlap. This check looks only at pieces that are parameters — pieces inside struct fields are not compared. The processor may drop the comparison of one pair from an op’s entry. But only when every way of reaching that op within the translation unit is a call site (not reached through function values · actors · tasks), and every such site already shows that the two pieces cannot overlap. The shapes that show it are four: ⓐ two pieces cut from the same storage are empty, or the end of one piece does not pass the start of the other — an end is a constant, or an expression that reads, for the same run-time value v, as k + m·v (k · m constants, m ≥ 0) (from 0 up to before k and from k up to before n · from 0 up to before n and from n up to before 2n). If that pair was allowed with inplace, it may be the same range. A piece seen as other elements with view_array is the same bytes. A piece cut again from a piece with a constant end (or that piece seen with view_array) lies inside that piece even if its end is unknown — subslice stops execution if the end is outside (E-VM-BOUNDS) — so if the two enclosing constant ranges split front and back, they do not overlap, ⓑ the two pieces are two inputs of the calling op (or pieces inside them), and the calling op has already compared that pair as not overlapping at its own entry (for a pair compared with inplace, only when both are passed as they are and the receiver also allows that pair with inplace), ⓒ one piece is a string literal (or a piece of one), or a name whose value is always a string literal — a writable place cannot point at read-only bytes (§6.2.6 (7b)), ⓓ one piece is a piece of a list the calling op built itself (a slot in the frame of §6.2.6 (7) ⓐ) or of bytes that op obtained with alloc_bytes (its some_value), and the other piece is a list of another declaration · the answer of another alloc_bytes, or an input of the calling op — each declaration and each obtaining has its own bytes (a root does not give bytes it gave again, and rewinding happens only when a region ends — §8.1 (2a) · §8.2 (4c)), so they do not overlap each other, nor bytes that existed before the call. Taking the same answer out into two names gives the same bytes. A name that binds a piece with let is seen as the value of that let — only when that name and the names in the value are bound once in the op and not lent by reference (for an input, only when the body does not write it again). An entry from outside the translation unit (a name C calls) compares the dropped pairs at that entry. So a call that can overlap is compared and stopped whichever way it comes — dropping changes neither answers nor stops.

In plain words

Until 2026-11-04, (7b) was «not asked». So wr2 (subslice s 0 k) (subslice s 0 k) passed the check and the later write overwrote the earlier one (external report REQ-0036). Of three options — treating the same name and same-number ends as the same range at translation, writing it down as a limit and allowing it, or comparing during execution — the owner chose the third (2026-11-04). When comparing began, three calls in the corpus stopped: two that passed a whole buffer and a window cut inside it with view_array to writing places (the static check could not follow that alias), and one where the wrapper of an op that declared it takes the same range had not copied over that permission (inplace). All three were fixed to cut the storage without overlap or to write the permission.

In plain words

Until 2026-09-21, (7) was the author’s duty for «ops that write only after they have finished reading», and the tool did not look (RFC-0115 §8-15 ⓒ). So violating calls showed up not as diagnostics but as wrong answers. Now the permission is written in the op’s head and can be asked at the one place of the caller (RFC-0116 D2 B1, owner decision ⓑ of 2026-09-22). Partial overlaps the old rule allowed are blocked by the new rule — the seven such calls in the corpus were fixed to cut the storage without overlap.

Rejected example — passing the same storage as write and read together to an op that did not declare it

Source

module ex_inplace_undeclared .

proc scale input o mut slice u64 . input a slice u64 . output u64 . effects none . do
  set idx o. 0 . mul idx a. 0 . 2 . .
  return 1 .
end .

proc f input w mut slice u64 . output u64 . effects none . do
  return scale w. w. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-EXCL-INPLACE

Example — an in-place call of an op that allowed the same range

Source

module ex_inplace_ok .

proc scale input o mut slice u64 . input a slice u64 . output u64 . effects none . inplace o a . do
  set idx o. 0 . mul idx a. 0 . 2 . .
  return 1 .
end .

proc f input w mut slice u64 . output u64 . effects none . do
  return scale subslice w. 0 4 . subslice w. 0 4 . . .
end .

Rejected example — writing inplace on a body that reads after writing on the same range

Source

module ex_inplace_unproven .

proc late_read input o mut slice u64 . input a slice u64 . output u64 . effects none . inplace o a . do
  set idx o. 0 . 1 .
  return idx a. 0 . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-INPLACE-UNPROVEN

8 An op can write the clause invalidates <input> . (§6.4.1 (3a)) in its head to declare that it makes views that came from that input’s storage invalid — giving a block back, moving to another place while growing, and rewinding are such. It writes one name, and it shall be an input of that op (E-INVALIDATES-FORM).

8a Within the body of one op, a view (a local or borrowed name whose type contains slice) carries as its source the storage that appeared in its initialiser — making a view from a view continues the source. Using that view after passing that storage to an op that declared invalidates does not conform (E-VIEW-INVALIDATED). Reassigning the name as a whole revives it with a new source. If it becomes invalid on any one branch, it is invalid after they join, and in a loop the invalidity continues to the head of the next iteration. Values such as handles and numbers are not views.

8b The sources of views are lumped together — the result of a call is regarded as coming from all its arguments. So it may refuse safe uses but never misses an invalidated view. Op boundaries are crossed like this:

  • Even an op that does not write invalidates is regarded as an op that invalidates an input if, in its body, it passes that input to an op that declared invalidates (including ops so inferred)

— invalidation does not disappear by going through a wrapping op.

  • If a view is put into a field of an aggregate (set field h. f . <view> .), that field holds the view. Reading and using that field after the field’s source becomes invalid is

E-VIEW-INVALIDATED, and reassigning the field revives it.

8c Two mut views obtained by calling an op without side effects (effects none) twice with the same arguments are regarded as the same storage. Keeping both alive outside a borrow and using them violates (6) (E-EXCL) — taking out two write views with the same handle of a pool is such. If the arguments are not names or constants, whether they are the same is not asked.

Rejected example — using an old view after an op that moves while growing

Source

module ex_view_invalidated .

struct buf do
  data mut slice u8 .
  n u64 .
end .

proc grow input b mut buf . output u64 . effects none . invalidates b .
do
  set field b. n . add field b. n . 1 . .
  return 1 .
end .

proc view_of input b buf . output slice u8 . effects none .
do
  return field b. data . .
end .

proc f input b mut buf . output u64 . effects none .
do
  let v slice u8 view_of b. . .
  let r u64 grow b. . .
  return len v. . .       rem `grow` invalidated the views of `b` — take the view again
end .

Diagnostic: E-VIEW-INVALIDATED

In plain words

What this rule protects is the same as §8.8 — the reader’s trust. But where §8.8 decides «can it be written» by type, this one decides «who is here together right now». There are places where, even with the types right, things must not be together, and seeing them is the exclusivity rule.

Caution —

mut ref slice is not used (E-MREF-SLICE) — mut slice already lets its elements be changed, so the former gives nothing the latter does not. It is a second spelling that says the same thing.

Rejected example — a name bound with let cannot be changed

Source

module ex_immutable .

fn f output u8 .
do
  let a u8 1 .
  set a. 2 .            rem to change it, it shall be bound with `var`
  return a. .
end .

Diagnostic: E-IMMUTABLE

8.13 Allocators — capability · policy · state§

allocator (얼로케이터)

A value that hands out small places from bytes received from a root or borrowed. In this language it is an actor that provides the trait byte_allocator.

1 What hands out places over a root (§8.1 (2a)) is called an allocator. An allocator is not a special device of the language but an actor that provides the trait byte_allocator. There are three things in that word.

Table 64 — The three that make up an allocator

LayerWhen it existsWhat it is
capabilityonly at translationwhether a root may be reached — cap allocator · cap heap (⟦§7.2⟧)
policyat translation (the type)by which rule it carves — a type that provides byte_allocator
stateduring executionthe cursor · backing bytes — a value of that type

2 Capability fields (root cap heap . · root cap allocator .) can be put in an actor’s state. Calling alloc_bytes with that field carves from that capability’s root. A capability is not a run-time value, so that field has no size.

3 An actor with capability fields can be spawned only in an op where a capability of the same kind is visible — one that received that capability as an input, or an op of an actor that itself has a field of the same kind (E-CAP-FORGE). This is so that a way of reaching a root in one line cannot be made up in a place without the capability.

4 On a machine without an operating system, actors with a cap heap field and ops of actors that write the heap effect are refused (E-HEAP-NOHOST) — except that actors spawned nowhere in that unit are not asked about.

5 The standard library gives two default allocators in this shape: allocs.fixed_bytes (fixed window) · allocs.heap_bytes (heap). So default allocators and allocators the user built go into the same place — the receiving side (input comptime a type . input al a . requires allocs.byte_allocator a. . .) does not tell the two apart.

6 The heap effect means only «the root grows». Giving places back piece by piece is the work of policy, and such an allocator provides the trait freeing_allocator — in addition to the ops of byte_allocator, release takes a piece and answers with a truth value whether it took it back. So even on a machine without an operating system, an allocator that gives back piece by piece over a fixed window can be used.

7 When an allocator asks «is this the piece I gave last», it asks by identity, not by length: grow and release take the piece itself, and the core op same_slice a. b. . checks whether two slices start at the same place and have the same length. Recognising by length alone would grow or take back someone else’s piece of the same length. same_slice does not expose addresses — the answer is a truth value.

Rejected example — spawning an actor with a capability field without the capability

Source

module ex_cap_forge .

actor grower do
  state do
    root cap heap .
  end .
  proc take input n u64 . output u64 . effects heap . do
    let g option mut slice u8 alloc_bytes root. capacity n. . .
    if is_some g. . do return n. . end .
    return 0 .
  end .
end .

proc f output u64 . effects heap state . do
  var g grower spawn actor grower . . rem there is no `input h cap heap .`
  return send g. take 8 . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-CAP-FORGE

8.14 Choosing an allocator per object — the use clause of an op head and the use · keep of bindings§

1 An op can write the clause use <name> <type> . in its head to declare the allocator it carves from. <type> is a type that provides byte_allocator, or a comptime type parameter with such a bound (requires allocs.byte_allocator a. . .). In the body, <name> is an ordinary name.

1a An op writes only one use clause (E-USING-DUP). The clause is two words, a name and a type. The allocator clause of a binding is written directly after the name as use <source> (gives back at the end of the block) or keep <source> (does not give back) (§6.5.1 (3c)) — a mismatched shape is E-USING-FORM.

1b The receiving side and the choosing side use one word — use <name> <type> . in an op head is «this op carves here», and use <source>. of a binding is «this object with this». The use of a head is told apart from importing (§6.10) by place: importing stands outside declarations, and this clause stands between the op’s name and do. A head clause has no distinction between giving back and carrying out — keep stands only on bindings. The old spelling using <name> <type> . is refused (E-USING-OLD).

2 The use clause is not an input. The caller does not write that allocator positionally, and if <type> is a type parameter, it does not write that type argument either — it comes from the allocator’s type.

3 The caller chooses the allocator at the binding: let <name> use <source> <type> <expr> .. The chosen source goes into the head call of that initialiser. If the head call is an op without a use clause, it does not conform (E-ALLOC-USING-UNUSED) — an object that already holds its own allocator does not take the caller’s choice. If the initialiser is a lit, its bytes are received from the chosen source (§6.2.6 (7e)). A self-owning node (§8.5 (10)) is bound as owned, so it is not given back at the end of the block — the responsibility for giving it back is carried by that value, and the drop … use of §8.5 (11) gives it back.

4 If nothing is chosen, the default is decided in the following order. The first that fits is used.

Table 65 — The order that decides the default

OrderSource
1the binding’s use <source> · keep <source>
2the name of this op’s own use clause (if the type fits)
3the only one among this op’s inputs · bindings whose type fits
noneE-ALLOC-NOSOURCE — no allocator is visible
two or moreE-ALLOC-AMBIGUOUS — it does not guess. Write it in the binding

5 The default does not cross op boundaries. The source comes only from that op’s signature and body, and the caller’s allocator does not flow in by itself. Fields are not counted as sources either. That is, there is no global allocator.

5a The choice of allocator is resolved on the tree — after the processor has stood forms up by arity, before monomorphisation. If checked in a comparison mode that does not build the tree, unresolved clauses remain, and the processor does not silently check them as an op short of one input but says so with E-USING-UNRESOLVED.

6 The root operand can be left out, as in alloc_bytes capacity <n>, only inside a region block, and then the innermost region goes into that place. Capabilities (cap allocator · cap heap) cannot be left out and are written by name — so as not to make a way of reaching a root without a name. An alloc_bytes that points at no root — left out outside a region, or naming something that is none of a capability input · a region parameter · an open region block · a capability field — does not conform (E-ALLOC-NOROOT). The root of stack_new is a region parameter and cannot be left out (the same code).

6a The size is written after the capacity marker: alloc_bytes <root> capacity <n> · stack_new <region> capacity <n>. Without the marker it does not conform (E-ALLOC-CAPACITY-MARK) — that marker is what separates the shortened form from the form with a name.

In plain words

An allocator is a value — the cursor and the backing bytes have to live somewhere during execution. The use of an op head moves the place where that value is written without hiding it: the receiving side writes «I carve from this allocator» in its signature, and the caller writes «with this» on the line that makes the object. The machine code between the two is the same as passing one argument.

Example — alternating between two allocators in one op

Source

module ex_using .

trait carver do
  reserve input s self . input n u64 . output u64 . effects state .
end .

rem Two policies — one counts what is asked, the other twice as much
actor exact do
  satisfies carver .
  state do
    used u64 .
  end .
  proc reserve input n u64 . output u64 . effects state . do
    set used. add used. n. . .
    return used. .
  end .
end .

actor doubled do
  satisfies carver .
  state do
    used u64 .
  end .
  proc reserve input n u64 . output u64 . effects state . do
    set used. add used. mul n. 2 . . .
    return used. .
  end .
end .

rem The receiving side — the caller writes neither the allocator nor its type
proc take input comptime a type . use al a . input n u64 . output u64 . effects state . requires carver a. . . do
  return send al. reserve n. . .
end .

proc main output u8 . effects state . do
  var e exact spawn actor exact . .
  var d doubled spawn actor doubled . .
  let x use e. u64 take 3 . .      rem 3
  let y use d. u64 take 3 . .      rem 6
  return narrow u8 add x. y. . . .
end .

Rejected example — the allocator clause of an op head is use — tests (1b)

Source

module ex_using_head_old .

trait carver do
  reserve input s self . input n u64 . output u64 . effects state .
end .

proc take input comptime a type . using al a . input n u64 . output u64 . effects state . requires carver a. . . do
  return send al. reserve n. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-USING-OLD

Rejected example — two allocators are visible and none was chosen

Source

module ex_using_ambiguous .

trait carver do
  reserve input s self . input n u64 . output u64 . effects state .
end .

actor exact do
  satisfies carver .
  state do
    used u64 .
  end .
  proc reserve input n u64 . output u64 . effects state . do
    set used. add used. n. . .
    return used. .
  end .
end .

proc take input comptime a type . use al a . input n u64 . output u64 . effects state . requires carver a. . . do
  return send al. reserve n. . .
end .

proc main output u8 . effects state . do
  var e exact spawn actor exact . .
  var f exact spawn actor exact . .
  let x u64 take 3 . .              rem e or f? — it does not guess
  return narrow u8 x. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-ALLOC-AMBIGUOUS

9 Library§

1 This clause does not specify a list of individual library functions. What it specifies is the rules a library shall keep. As long as those rules are kept, any library is within the guarantees of this language.

In plain words

The C standard holds the language and the library in one document and specifies function by function. Lowent does not, because libraries change much faster than languages. If a list of functions were written here, this document would soon be out of date. Writing rules instead does not go out of date.

9.1 Libraries are written in the same language§

1 The portable layer of the standard library is written in the language this document specifies and has no privileges. That part receives the same rules as code the author writes directly.

1a But the library as a whole is not one layer. At the places that reach the outside world there are narrow leaves, and they cross a trust boundary (§4.4) using unsafe or extern. That boundary is kept narrow, and where it is is written in the source.

1b Turning names into addresses (DNS) is a different leaf from making connections. Both receive cap net and produce the io effect, but there is a reason to separate them: a program that already has an address never touches name resolution, and tests can measure connections without a network by giving addresses by hand. And name resolution can give different answers depending on the machine and the moment, and which line that wavering is on is visible in the source.

In plain words

For convenience, one connection leaf that also takes names would be better. But then “how many times does this line touch the network” could not be known from the source alone — the same question this language repeats in capabilities and effects, and it gets the same answer: name what is hidden.

2 That is, library ops also write contracts (§6.4), declare effects (§7.1), and receive capabilities as arguments (§7.2).

Note (informative)

That libraries have no privileges is a matter of checkability. If there were a hidden passage only libraries could use, the program’s guarantees would break the moment that passage was crossed.

9.2 What libraries shall keep§

1 a) They return failure as a value. They do not express failure with special values (−1 and the like) (§6.2.8).

2 b) If destroying can fail, they do not hide it (§8.5).

3 c) They do not make up capabilities themselves. Capabilities they need are received as arguments.

4 d) They declare effects honestly, including the effects of what they call inside.

5 e) For numbers whose base can differ, they write which base. For values such as rows · columns · indices · page numbers, where counting from 0 or from 1 can differ, the op’s lowdoc or the module contract shall state it. This language’s indices are 0-based, so libraries are 0-based by default, and if an outside convention is 1-based (ANSI cursor positioning of terminals and the like), that conversion is done inside the op — if the caller had to know the outside convention, that would be knowledge outside the source (§1.3).

Note (informative)

Example: the row and column of term.goto <buffer> <position> <row> <column> are 0-based (the top left of the screen is 0 0). ANSI is 1-based, so the op adds 1 inside before putting it out — that addition is not the caller’s business.

Example — libraries keep the same rules

Source

module ex_lib .

enum parse_error do
  too_short .
end .

rem Failure is returned as a value — no special number (−1 and the like) is used.
export fn first_byte input data slice u8 . output result u8 parse_error .
  errors too_short lt len data. . 1 . .
do
  guard ge len data. . 1 . else return error too_short . . .
  return ok idx data. 0 . . .
end .

In plain words

These four are not new rules; they say that libraries keep the rules already fixed too. If libraries were made exceptions, the program’s guarantees would break the moment such a library was used.

9.3 What is language and what is library§

1 The core ops and builtin ops of §6.3.3 are language. Their names are fixed. The names of core ops cannot be hidden by the author, and builtin ops stand only after call_builtin, so they do not meet the author’s names.

1a Builtin ops are material for building libraries. The standard library provides ops that wrap builtin ops, and application programs call those ops — calling builtin ops directly is the business of places that build system libraries and processors.

2 Everything else is library, used by importing modules.

3 For a feature to enter the language, the following shall hold — it cannot be expressed as a library, or expressing it as one hides a cost.

4 Between the language and libraries there is one more layer — the leaf.

leaf (리프)

The thinnest piece that touches the operating system or hardware. It cannot be written in Lowent, so the processor provides it. A leaf shall be thin — what can be written in Lowent is not a leaf but a library.

5 Whether a piece qualifies as a leaf is decided by the following question — “can this be written in Lowent.” If it can, it is a library. Convenience does not qualify something as a leaf.

Table 66 — The three layers

LayerWho makes itQualification
languagethis documentcannot be expressed as a library, or expressing it as one hides a cost
leafthe processortouches the operating system · hardware. Cannot be written in Lowent
libraryanyoneeverything else. Written in Lowent

Diagram — the three layers — reading one file

 library   files.open, files.read, files.slurp   Lowent code: owned handle, result
    |
 leaf      file_open, file_read, file_close      from the processor, needs cap file_system
    |
 OS        open, read, close                     the kernel
 language  add, len, idx, narrow ...           fixed words (6.3.3), used at every layer

In plain words

For example, «opening a file and receiving an integer handle» touches the kernel, so it is a leaf. Wrapping that handle in a value that cannot be forgotten can be written in Lowent, so it is library. «A convenience function that reads a whole file» is library too — being convenient does not make it a leaf.

Caution — Convenience is not a reason to enter the language

“It is convenient to write it this way” is the business of libraries. Adding a word to the language makes that word something every program has to memorise (§6.1.2). So the language accepts only what cannot be expressed.

9.4 How ripe is it — the ladder of maturity§

1 Each library module writes its maturity. There are four rungs, and a higher rung includes all the duties of the lower rungs.

Table 67 — The rungs of maturity and their duties

RungThe duties of that rung
experimentalthe maturity is written. Nothing more — that is why it is an experiment
incubating＋ a decision document that owns it actually exists
standard＋ there is a description for people to read, and a regression witness calls that module by name
deprecated＋ what to switch to is written

2 A module that does not keep the duties of the rung it writes does not conform. Raising a rung comes after meeting the duties; if they are not met, it shall stay on the lower rung.

Note (informative)

The value of the ladder is not in the upper rungs but in the lower ones. Writing experimental is writing “do not rely on this yet” in the source, and that one line answers for the user “may I design relying on this”. Without rungs every module looks the same, and then the least ripe is read as if it were the ripest.

Note (informative)

A rung is the size of a promise, not a ranking of quality. A well-made experimental module can be better than a sloppy standard module — what differs is “how much it has said it will not change”.

9.5 What enters the standard library§

1 Things enter the library by evidence, not by existing. The following six are the measures that decide entry.

Table 68 — The library charter

MeasureWhat it means
if it can be written in this language, write it in this languagesomething becomes a leaf inside the processor only when it uses an outside authority or is work this language cannot express. Convenience is not a ground
the signature is the ledger of costs and authorityeffects · capabilities · the caller’s storage · allocation · ownership · completion · machine constraints shall be visible at the boundary
separate computation from authoritybuilding a shape and putting it out, parsing and opening a file, the next step of random numbers and obtaining entropy, protocol encoding and carrying are each different things
divide places along several axesauthority · storage · execution tier (⟦§10.8⟧) · profile (⟦§10.7⟧) are not squeezed into one ladder
keep what is inside smallup to a portable base and thin, audited host adapters. Large outside dependencies · policy-heavy facilities · surfaces still in research are not brought in
each module has evidenceit declares its support boundary and has unit · negative · oracle · failure · profile gates

2 This document does not write a list of libraries. What exists is decided by a ledger that is counted again from the source each time, and if this document copied it, they would part from the moment of copying.

In plain words

If the charter doubled as a list, something still a proposal would look as if it already existed just because it was in the table. That really happened — three APIs that did not exist were read as fact because they were in the table. So this clause specifies only what enters, and leaves what has entered to the counting side.

10 Concurrency§

1 This clause specifies the places where several things proceed together. Concurrency is not a special feature in this language but one more place where the effect rules of §7 apply unchanged.

10.1 Concurrency is an effect too§

1 Making another flow of execution, or waiting for its progress, is an effect. wait and concurrent are those (§7.1), and they shall be written in the contract.

2 What separates wait from concurrent is who wakes it up.

Table 69 — The two kinds of waiting

EffectMeaning
waitit can be suspended. What wakes it is not another task of the same program (the kernel · a device wakes it)
concurrentit ends only when a peer runs. There has to be an executor to run that peer

In plain words

For example, waiting for data to come from a file is wait — what wakes it is not another task of the same program but the outside.

Note (informative)

Why this distinction matters: a small device without an operating system may have no «executor that runs peers». Then a program that uses only wait runs as it is, but a program that uses concurrent does not. It is written in the effects, so it can be known at translation time.

10.2 Actors§

1 An actor is a unit of execution with its own state. The state exists only inside the actor and cannot be touched directly from outside.

1a So reading the state fields of an actor value from outside is refused (E-ACTOR-FIELD). The only way to reach the state is messages — put an op that returns what is needed in the actor.

1b A state field cannot be a borrow (ref · mut_ref) (E-ACTOR-STATE-REF). A borrow cannot outlive what it was borrowed from (§8.4.1), but a state field remains for as long as the actor lives, and there is no place to write what was borrowed.

1c The state fields of an actor just made with spawn actor are all 0. 0 is not a slice, so reading before setting up a field that holds a slice or a borrow does not conform (E-ACTOR-UNINIT). The processor refuses the case where the first message sent to that actor reads such a field without setting it up — it looks within the straight order of statements.

In plain words

Until 2026-09-16, before (1c), that place was where the two back ends parted: the VM stopped with «not a slice», and native silently gave none (defect note 53). One of the two was a lie, but the canon did not say which. Instead of choosing an answer, it makes that place unreachable.

1d A name holding an actor can start with the literal 0 — an actor not yet spawned (the same as handle 0 of §10.3 (2d)). Other numbers do not go into an actor place (E-TYPE-LET · E-TYPE-VAR). Sending a message with send · spawn send · try spawn send to an actor that is 0, or draining it, stops the program (E-VM-HANDLE-ZERO). Use it after it holds spawn actor <name>.

Rejected example — filling an actor place with a number other than 0

Source

module ex_actor_number .

actor counter do
  state do
    n u64 .
  end .
  fn get output u64 . do
    return n. .
  end .
end .

export proc f output u64 . effects state .
do
  var c counter 7 .
  return 0 .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-VAR

2 Ops inside actors also follow the kinds of §6.4.1 — if it changes state it is proc and shall write effects state; if it only reads, it is fn.

3 Declaring it pure while changing state is refused at translation.

4 If an actor’s op panics (§6.5.9), that actor can be set up again. Then that actor’s state returns to the start and that message is processed again. What comes back to life is only its own state; other actors are not touched.

5 Until when it is set up again is decided by the clause failure restart <policy> .. The policies are the following three and no more.

Table 70 — Actor failure policies — a closed set of three

PolicyMeaning
max <number> [within <number> <unit>]sets it up again that many times. When used up, passes it upward
neverdoes not set it up again — passes it upward the first time it blows up
alwayskeeps setting it up again without counting

5b The units of within are a closed three, and both singular and plural are accepted — second/seconds · minute/minutes · hour/hours. Any other word does not conform.

5c If the processor cannot count the period of within, it says so (W-NOT-YET). It does not silently read a clause that writes a period as max <number>.

Note (informative)

Both singular and plural are accepted for reading’s sake — within 1 second and within 5 seconds are written as people write. This is not a second way of saying the same thing (§7.1.1): the meaning is one and only the spelling differs with the number, and the processor reads both as the same number.

5a An actor has one failure policy (E-ACTOR-FAILURE). So does its mailbox policy (E-ACTOR-MAILBOX, §10.5). With two policies nobody could say which stands, and the answer would become the processor’s internal affair.

6 If it blows up after using them all, that failure is passed upward. No actor is left silently stopped.

7 Setting up again applies only to panic. A broken contract (§6.4) is not set up again — that is the program breaking a promise it wrote itself, so doing it again gives the same.

In plain words

Why «let it blow up»? There are failures for which setting up again is better — cases where the state somehow became strange. Code that rolls back such failures one by one by hand is long, and when it is long that code itself goes wrong. Returning the state to the start is short recovery that is always right.

Example — an actor

Source

module ex_actor .

actor counter do
  state do
    value u64 .
  end .

  rem It changes state, so it is `proc` and `effects state`.
  proc inc output u64 . effects state .
  do
    set value. expr value. + 1 . .
    return value. .
  end .

  rem It only reads, so it is `fn` and has no effects.
  fn get output u64 .
  do
    return value. .
  end .
end .

Rejected example — declared pure while changing state

Source

module ex_actor_bad .

actor counter do
  state do
    value u64 .
  end .

  fn inc output u64 .
  do
    set value. expr value. + 1 . . rem it changes state but is an `fn`
    return value. .
  end .
end .

Diagnostic: E-EFFECT-PURITY

10.3 Making flows of execution§

1 spawn makes a new flow of execution. Making one is an effect and shall be written in the contract.

2 Whether it waits for the flow it made to end or detaches it is separated by effects — detaching is detach.

2a spawn <op> [arguments…] gives a handle that points at that flow. The type of the handle is task T, where T is that op’s output type. If it is used only as a statement without receiving the handle, that flow is detached.

2b await <handle> waits until that flow ends and gives its result. The type of the result is the handle’s T. Waiting for a value that is not a task T is refused (E-TYPE-KIND). While waiting, other flows proceed — waiting does not stop the machine.

2c Waiting for something that cannot end stops the program. The processor shall say where.

2d Handle 0 is a handle that points at no flow. A name declared task T can start with the literal 0 — the spelling for leaving the place empty before receiving a flow. No other number can be a handle (E-TYPE-LET · E-TYPE-VAR — numbers do not point at flows). Waiting for handle 0 stops the program (E-VM-HANDLE-ZERO). The processor builds the handles spawn gives as values other than 0, and filters out 0 at every place that uses a handle — an empty handle never silently points at another flow.

Rejected example — making a handle from a number other than 0

Source

module ex_handle_number .

fn one output u64 . do
  return 1 .
end .

export proc pick output u64 . effects state concurrent .
do
  var h task u64 7 .
  task_group do
    set h. spawn one . .
  end .
  return await h. . .
end .

Diagnostic: E-TYPE-VAR

Note (informative)

Handle 0 is in the same position as the null pointer of other languages. Being able to leave it empty is convenient, but using what was left empty shall stop on the spot rather than grab something unrelated. This language counts handles from 1 so that 0 overlaps no flow, and checks for 0 at every place it is used.

Note (informative)

Why waiting is an effect. await computes no value but spends time — the caller has to put that cost into its budget. So a pure op cannot wait (§7.3).

3 spawn actor makes an actor, and send sends a message to that actor. An actor processes messages one at a time.

3a Values can be carried with a message — written side by side after the message name, as in send <actor> <message> <value>…. On the receiving side the actor instance is treated like the first parameter, and the values written follow it.

3b Sending a value with ownership hands over that ownership. The sender can no longer use that value, and using it is refused at translation (§8.5).

In plain words

Why hand it over? If two flows hold the same value together, races arise. If ownership moves only through messages, at any moment only one flow can touch that value, and then races cannot happen — not prevented, but with no place to exist.

Example — making an actor and sending a message

Source

module ex_spawn .

actor counter do
  state do
    value u64 .
  end .

  proc inc output u64 . effects state .
  do
    set value. expr value. + 1 . .
    return value. .
  end .
end .

rem Make an actor (`spawn`) and send a message (`send`).
export proc use_counter output u64 . effects state .
do
  var c counter spawn actor counter . .
  return send c. inc . .
end .

In plain words

It matters that an actor processes messages one at a time — the state inside is not touched at the same time. That is why locks need not be taken by hand.

Caution — Concurrency is not a world outside the checks

In many languages concurrency is the area of “be careful from here on”. In Lowent the same effect rules apply unchanged — what it waits for, whether it changes state, whether an executor is needed, are all written in the contract and checked by the processor.

10.4 The place that binds flows — task_group§

1 task_group do <statements> end . binds into one the flows made inside it. At the place where the block ends, the bound flows have all ended, and it does not leave with even one remaining.

2 So a flow cannot outlive the block that made it. This is how this language handles the lifetime of flows, and detaching (§10.3 (2)) is the only exception to that discipline.

Diagram — the lifetime of flows is confined inside the task_group block

op        ──┬── task_group do ─────────────────── end ──▶ continues
            │
h1          │   spawn ├── work ──┤ end
h2          │       spawn ├── work ──────────┤ end
            │
            └─ opened at task_group and closed at end --- flows live only in between
   await h1 · await h2 wait for each flow to end. No flow remains when passing end

3 Inside a group, spawn <op> [arguments…] puts a job in the queue. It is the same word, but spawn actor <name> makes one actor, and the two are told apart by what follows.

4 task_group cancel_on_error do … end . propagates cancellation. If one bound flow ends with an error, the siblings not yet started or waiting are treated as having ended with that error.

5 Cancellation does not roll back. The effects of flows that already did work do not go back, and cancellation only keeps work still to come from being done. So “which siblings were cancelled” depends on order, and this document does not fix that order.

6 The error itself shows through await (§10.3 (2b)) — cancellation does not swallow errors.

In plain words

Why bind them? A flow made and forgotten remains after the program ends, or touches what is already gone, or produces a failure nobody reads. When the block holds that lifetime, those three disappear by grammar — there is no place to forget.

10.5 Delivering messages later§

1 send (§10.3 (3)) waits until the actor processes the message. spawn send <actor>
<message> [arguments…] does not wait but puts it in the mailbox and returns at once.

1a try spawn send … does not stop when the mailbox is full but gives that as a value — its type is result u64 mailbox_full.
